at the same rate would require explicit enumeration of raised-index Riemann contractions, beyond the scope of this proof).

Proof sketch.

Work in adapted coordinates
\theta^{1}=u
,
\theta^{\alpha_{0}}
as the singled-out non-degenerate direction satisfying
\partial_{1}g_{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{0}}\neq 0
. The Fisher block has entries
F_{11}=\Theta(t^{2(k-1)})
,
F_{1\alpha_{0}}=O(t^{k-1})
,
F_{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{0}}=g_{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{0}}+O(t)
with
g_{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{0}}(0)>0
. The inverse metric gives
F^{11}=\Theta(t^{-2(k-1)})
. Christoffel symbols at the relevant orders:

•

\Gamma^{1}_{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{0}}=\tfrac{1}{2}F^{11}(2\partial_{\alpha_{0}}F_{1\alpha_{0}}-\partial_{1}F_{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{0}})=\Theta(t^{-2(k-1)})
: under
\partial_{1}g_{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{0}}\neq 0
, the second piece
\Theta(1)
dominates the first
O(t^{k-1})
, multiplied by
F^{11}=\Theta(t^{-2(k-1)})
;

•

\Gamma^{1}_{11}=\tfrac{1}{2}F^{11}\partial_{1}F_{11}=\Theta(t^{-2(k-1)})\cdot\Theta(t^{2k-3})=\Theta(t^{-1})
;

•

\Gamma^{\alpha}_{1\alpha_{0}}
via the non-degenerate inverse block:
\Theta(1)
;

•

Other cross-symbols
\Gamma^{\beta}_{\gamma\delta}
with no index in
\{1\}
are
\Theta(1)
.

The Riemann component
R^{1}_{\alpha_{0}1\alpha_{0}}
(with the standard sign convention) has leading contributions
\partial_{1}\Gamma^{1}_{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{0}}=\Theta(t^{-(2k-1)})
and
\Gamma^{1}_{11}\Gamma^{1}_{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{0}}=\Theta(t^{-1})\cdot\Theta(t^{-2(k-1)})=\Theta(t^{-(2k-1)})
, both at rate
t^{-(2k-1)}
. Cross-contractions
\Gamma^{1}_{\alpha_{0}\beta}\Gamma^{\beta}_{1\alpha_{0}}
for
\beta\neq 1
contribute at most
\Theta(t^{-2(k-1)})
, strictly subleading for
k\geq 2
. Adding the codimension-
1
genericity condition that the leading sum is
\Theta(t^{-(2k-1)})
rather than
o(t^{-(2k-1)})
from accidental coefficient cancellation between the two leading terms (a non-zero-measure condition on the analytic Taylor data of
g
at
\theta_{0}
), we obtain
R^{1}_{\alpha_{0}1\alpha_{0}}=\Theta(t^{-(2k-1)})
. Multiplying by
F_{11}=\Theta(t^{2(k-1)})
gives the covariant Riemann numerator
R_{1\alpha_{0}1\alpha_{0}}=\Theta(t^{-1})
. The denominator
F_{11}F_{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{0}}-F_{1\alpha_{0}}^{2}
is the Gram determinant of the two tangent vectors in the
(u,\partial_{\alpha_{0}})
plane; under Theorem
2
’s assumption (ii) (the score-independence hypothesis, which guarantees the Schur complement
c_{F}-b^{\top}g^{-1}b>0
), the Gram determinant is
\Theta(t^{2(k-1)})
, not a sub-leading cancellation. Dividing gives
K_{\mathrm{sect}}=\Theta(t^{-1})/\Theta(t^{2(k-1)})=\Theta(t^{-(2k-1)})
.
∎

Corollary 9

(Fisher-Riemannian volume scaling, single degenerate direction)
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
8
with a single degenerate direction (
r=1
) of KL order
k\geq 2
, the
Fisher-Riemannian
volume of the high-curvature set in a fixed compact neighbourhood
U\ni\theta_{0}
satisfies

\mathrm{Vol}_{F}\bigl(\{|K_{\mathrm{sect}}|>M\}\cap U\bigr)\;=\;\Theta\bigl(M^{-k/(2k-1)}\bigr)\quad\text{as }M\to\infty,

where
\mathrm{Vol}_{F}
uses the Fisher volume form
\sqrt{\det F}\,d^{n}\theta
. The corresponding Lebesgue volume scales as
\Theta(M^{-1/(2k-1)})
(by integrating the constant
1
instead of
\sqrt{\det F}
); the Fisher-volume scaling is the natural one for sectional-curvature-defined regions because
K_{\mathrm{sect}}
is itself a Fisher-metric quantity. Watanabe’s RLCT volume formula
(
Watanabe 2009
)
gives
\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathrm{Leb}}(\{K<\varepsilon\})\sim\varepsilon^{\lambda}
with
\lambda=1/(2k)
in the single-degenerate case, on the function-value level set rather than the curvature level set; the two scalings are different invariants of the same singular minimum.

Proof sketch.

|K_{\mathrm{sect}}|>M
requires
|t|<cM^{-1/(2k-1)}
(Proposition
8
). The Fisher-Riemannian volume form in adapted coordinates is
\mathrm{dvol}_{F}=\sqrt{\det F(t)}\,dt\,d\theta_{\perp}
. By the Schur expansion
\det F(t)=\det(g+O(t))\cdot(c_{F}-b^{\top}g^{-1}b)t^{2(k-1)}+O(t^{2k-1})
(same expansion as Theorem
2
), with the Schur factor
c_{F}-b^{\top}g^{-1}b>0
bounded away from zero by assumption (ii) of Theorem
2
,
\sqrt{\det F(t)}=\Theta(t^{k-1})
on the degenerate factor and
\Theta(1)
on the non-degenerate block. Integrating the degenerate factor
\int_{0}^{cM^{-1/(2k-1)}}t^{k-1}\,dt=\Theta(M^{-k/(2k-1)})
gives the claim after multiplying by the bounded non-degenerate Fisher-volume
C_{\perp}=\int_{\perp}\Theta(1)\,d\theta_{\perp}
.
∎

Proposition 10

(Fisher-volume scaling, additive multi-direction case)
.

Suppose the KL function admits the additive transversal normal form

K(u)\;=\;u_{1}^{2k_{1}}+u_{2}^{2k_{2}}+\cdots+u_{r}^{2k_{r}}+(\text{non-degenerate quadratic in remaining coordinates}),

with each direction carrying KL order
k_{i}\geq 2
and the per-direction non-degenerate-score genericity of Proposition
8
holding for each
u_{i}
. Then the Fisher-volume of the multi-direction high-curvature polydisc
\bigcap_{i}\{|t_{i}|<c_{i}M^{-1/(2k_{i}-1)}\}
satisfies

\mathrm{Vol}_{F}\bigl(\{|t_{i}|<c_{i}M^{-1/(2k_{i}-1)}\text{ for all }i\}\cap U\bigr)\;=\;\Theta\!\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}M^{-k_{i}/(2k_{i}-1)}\right),

which provides a lower bound on
\mathrm{Vol}_{F}(\{|K_{\mathrm{sect}}|>M\})
when the polydisc lies inside the high-curvature set (a cross-plane non-cancellation condition).

Comparison to RLCT volume.
Watanabe’s RLCT formula gives the Lebesgue volume of the
function-value
level set
\{K<\varepsilon\}
as
\sim\varepsilon^{\lambda}\cdot(-\log\varepsilon)^{m-1}
with
\lambda=\min_{i}(a_{i}+1)/(2k_{i})
in the resolved-Hironaka monomial form
K=\prod_{i}u_{i}^{2k_{i}}
(which is multiplicative, not additive). The two formulas are different invariants of the same singularity:
\lambda
is set by the
slowest-vanishing
direction (min over
i
), while the per-direction Fisher-curvature volume above is a
product
over all directions because the curvature integral factorises across the additive normal form. The Hironaka-resolved monomial-form analysis is left to future work.

Validation on parametric families with known
k
.

The chain is validated on analytic families with known KL order
k
, on the controlled population-Fisher approach (the freeze-probe of §
B
, where the residual stays
\Theta(1)
and the rate readout is not subject to the learned-trajectory confound of Remark
4
). The rate side (Theorem
2
) is the selection-rule recovery of Table
8
: transversal exponents matching
2(k-1)
on the
2
- and
3
-component Gaussian-mixture merges and on deep-linear reduced-rank regression, recovering
\hat{\lambda}\approx 1/4
. The curvature side (Proposition
8
) is verified symbolically: on the 2-component mixture merge (
k=2
,
w=0.35
to break the equal-weight symmetry of Remark
12
),
|K_{\mathrm{sect}}|\sim\delta^{-3}
, matching the predicted
-(2k-1)=-3
. The volume side (Corollary
9
) follows analytically from the curvature rate by integrating the Fisher volume form over the high-curvature tube (
-k/(2k-1)=-2/3
at
k=2
); it is a consequence of the curvature face rather than an independent measurement. The rate and curvature faces are thus the two independent checks, on singularities of qualitatively different structure (multi-degenerate merges and gauge-coupled rank deficiency).

Remark 11

(Asymptotic-regime measurement precondition)
.

Theorems
2
and Proposition
10
state pointwise rate exponents in the asymptotic limit
t\to 0
. Empirical measurement of these exponents requires the trajectory to reach a regime where the leading-order rate dominates sub-leading corrections, which in turn requires the dead-direction value at the start of the measurement window to sit substantially above the loss-side noise floor (the irreducible
\sigma_{\mathrm{noise}}^{2}
from data noise, or in noise-free testbeds the floating-point cov-accumulation floor
\sigma_{\max}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{fp}}}
). On a constructed canonical-aligned bridge
W_{\ell}=\mathrm{diag}(1,\ldots,1,t_{0},\ldots,t_{0})
with
r
dead diagonal entries, the natural depth-invariant measurement-control variable is
t_{0}^{L}
(the singular value of the composed forward map at initialization). When comparing rate predictions across
L
at fixed
\sigma_{\mathrm{noise}}
, holding
t_{0}^{L}
constant isolates the rate prediction from the asymptotic-regime accessibility; varying
t_{0}
across
L
at fixed value conflates the two. Empirically the measurement test passes when
t_{0}^{L}\gtrsim\sigma_{\mathrm{noise}}^{2}
and fails (slope unfittable;
R^{2}
collapses) when
t_{0}^{L}\ll\sigma_{\mathrm{noise}}^{2}
.

Remark 12

(Genericity limits of the curvature rate)
.

The condition
\partial_{1}g_{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{0}}\neq 0
in Proposition
8
is generic but not universal. At equal-component mixture weights (
w=1/2
) the non-degenerate metric is symmetric in the degenerate direction,
\partial_{1}g_{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{0}}=0
, and the sub-leading Christoffel contribution gives a slower divergence; in some cross-planes the Riemann-tensor contributions cancel and
K_{\mathrm{sect}}
remains finite. Empirically, the 2-component Gaussian mixture at
w=1/2
has
K_{\mathrm{sect}}\to-1/2
(finite) in the
(\delta,\bar{\mu})
plane (verified by direct symbolic computation). The full 3-parameter model has empirical
K_{\max}\sim\delta^{-6}
in the
(\bar{\mu},w)
cross-plane. This empirical
\delta^{-6}
rate is consistent with (but not derived from) Proposition
10
: with two simultaneously-degenerate directions
(k_{1},k_{2})=(2,2)
, the cross-plane sectional curvature can pick up the rates of both directions multiplicatively, giving formally
\Theta(\delta^{-3})\cdot\Theta(\delta^{-3})/\Theta(1)=\Theta(\delta^{-6})
via a heuristic two-direction Christoffel argument. A rigorous derivation of multi-direction sectional curvature requires extending Proposition
8
’s Christoffel analysis to the cross-plane case, which is left to future work; we present the
\delta^{-6}
here as a target prediction that the multi-direction extension should reproduce.

Figure 5
:
The Fisher–curvature–volume rate chain: three measurable faces of the single KL invariant
k
. (a) The Fisher smallest eigenvalue decays as
\Theta(t^{2(k-1)})
(Theorem
2
). (b) The Fisher–Riemannian sectional curvature blows up as
\Theta(t^{-(2k-1)})
(Proposition
8
). (c) The high-curvature Fisher volume scales as
\Theta(M^{-k/(2k-1)})
as the threshold
M\to\infty
(Corollary
9
). Illustrative power laws for
k=2,3
; each exponent is a different function of the same
k
.

We collect here the supporting definitions and remarks that frame the rate-chain results of §
6
(Theorem
2
, Theorem
3
, Proposition
8
, Corollary
9
, Proposition
10
). The content below names the genericity hypotheses, makes the Fisher matrix structure explicit, compares the rate exponents to Watanabe’s RLCT, and indicates how the smooth-fiber result extends to stratified and resolved singular sets.

Definition 13

(Non-degenerate boundary approach)
.

A path
\theta(t)\to\theta_{0}
as
t\to 0
approaches a singularity
non-degenerately
if:
(i)
\lambda_{\min}(F(\theta(t)))\to 0
;
(ii) some derivative
\partial_{k}F_{ij}
is bounded away from zero;
(iii)
\lambda_{\min}(F(\theta(t)))=\Omega(t^{q})
for some
q>0
.

For algebraic-statistical models (real-analytic
\theta\mapsto p_{\theta}
with analytic singular locus), conditions (i)–(iii) hold automatically at every singularity
(
Watanabe 2009
)
. This covers Gaussian mixtures, Boltzmann machines, HMMs, reduced-rank regressions, and neural networks with smooth activations (tanh, sigmoid, softplus). Networks with piecewise-linear activations (ReLU) are not strictly real-analytic, but the singular locus structure is qualitatively similar
(
Watanabe 2009
)
; the formal results of this paper apply to the analytic case.

Comparison to Watanabe’s RLCT theory.

Watanabe’s RLCT theory
(
Watanabe 2009
)
gives the asymptotic form of the marginal log-likelihood and the KL order
k
along resolved coordinates obtained via Hironaka resolution. It does not give a pointwise rate for
u^{\top}F(\theta(t))u
or for
\lambda_{\min}(F(\theta(t)))
along an analytic path approaching the singular minimum: the resolved-coordinate analysis runs on the Jacobian of the blow-up, and the Fisher rate in original coordinates falls outside it. Theorem
2
’s contribution is a rate on the
unresolved
Fisher in original parameter coordinates, expressed in terms of the same KL order
k
.

Corollary 14

(Fisher matrix structure near a single-degenerate singularity)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2
, the Fisher matrix in adapted coordinates
(\theta^{1}=u,\theta^{\alpha})
satisfies:

F_{11}=c_{F}\,t^{2(k-1)}+O(t^{2k-1}),\quad F_{1\alpha}=b_{\alpha}\,t^{k-1}+O(t^{k}),\quad F_{\alpha\beta}=g_{\alpha\beta}+O(t),

where
c_{F}=2ck^{2}>0
,
b_{\alpha}=k\,\mathbb{E}_{p^{*}}[a_{k}\cdot\partial_{\alpha}\log p^{*}]
,
g_{\alpha\beta}=\mathbb{E}_{p^{*}}[(\partial_{\alpha}\log p^{*})(\partial_{\beta}\log p^{*})]
is positive definite, and
c_{F}>b^{\top}g^{-1}b
(assumption (ii) of Theorem
2
).

Proof.

The expressions follow from the proof of Theorem
2
via
F_{u}(t)=\mathbb{E}_{p^{*}}[s_{u}^{2}\exp f]
and the score decomposition
s_{u}=k\,a_{k}\,t^{k-1}+O(t^{k})
,
s_{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}\log p^{*}+O(t)
.
∎

Fiber structure resolves the apparent circularity in selection.

To apply the selection rule (Theorem
3
), one must identify which eigenvalues are transversal. This requires knowing the fiber
S
(from the model structure), not
k
itself. At the Gaussian mixture merge,
S=\{\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}\}
is known from the model definition and the normal bundle is the
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
subspace; for reduced-rank regression,
S
includes the gauge orbit and the normal bundle contains the rank-deficiency direction. With the fiber fixed, the controlled freeze-probe recovers transversal exponents matching
2(k-1)
on all three families (Table
8
).

Extension to singular fibers.

Theorem
3
requires
S
to be a smooth submanifold (so that
N_{\theta_{0}}S
and
\Pi
are well-defined). Two extensions beyond smooth fibers are possible without the full resolution machinery:

(i) Isolated singularities.
If
S
is smooth except at finitely many points, puncture the singular points and apply Theorem
3
to
S\setminus\{\text{singular points}\}
. The selection rule holds on any approach path avoiding these points.

(ii) Stratified fibers.
If
S
decomposes into smooth strata (typically a Whitney stratification, of which normal-crossing varieties
\{x_{1}\cdots x_{k}=0\}
are the canonical example), apply Theorem
3
to each stratum separately; the eigenvalue rates depend on which stratum is approached. Practical machine-learning singular sets are almost always of this type because they arise from products of coordinate-aligned conditions:

•

Multi-component Gaussian mixtures with several pairwise collapses, e.g. for
K=3
components the stratification
\{\mu_{1}{=}\mu_{2}\}\cup\{\mu_{2}{=}\mu_{3}\}\cup\{\mu_{1}{=}\mu_{3}\}
meeting along the triple-collapse line
\{\mu_{1}{=}\mu_{2}{=}\mu_{3}\}
.

•

Rank-stratified reduced-rank regression:
\{\mathrm{rank}(W)\leq r\}
decomposes into
\{\mathrm{rank}=r\}\sqcup\{\mathrm{rank}\leq r{-}1\}\sqcup\cdots
, each rank stratum smooth.

•

Mixture models with simultaneous weight and center collapse (
w_{i}\to 0

and

\mu_{i}\to\mu_{j}
); strata indexed by the combinatorial type.

•

Overparameterised neural networks with intersecting gauge orbits (dead-neuron loci, permutation symmetry, layer-wise rescaling), which generically meet transversally and form a normal-crossing pattern.

On each stratum the selection rule applies; the rates one measures depend on which stratum the trajectory
\theta(t)
is converging to.

(iii) Genuinely non-stratified fibers.
For singular sets that are not unions of smooth pieces meeting transversally (cuspidal singularities
y^{2}=x^{3}
, the Whitney umbrella
x^{2}=y^{2}z
, tangent self-intersections such as
\{x^{2}=y^{4}\}
, higher-codimension high-order vanishing
\{f_{1}=f_{2}=0\}
with both
f_{i}
vanishing to high order along the same locus), the smooth-fiber assumption fails and the piecewise machinery does not apply directly. Hironaka’s resolution of singularities
(
Hironaka 1964
)
provides a birational map
\phi:\Theta^{\prime}\to\Theta
under which the resolved fiber
\phi^{-1}(S)
is smooth, so Theorem
3
applies in the resolved coordinates. Translating the eigenvalue rates back to the original coordinates requires controlling the Jacobian of
\phi
, which has the structure
|J_{\phi}|\sim\prod|u_{i}|^{a_{i}}
. This pullback analysis is a natural direction for future work.

We are not aware of standard deployed architectures whose loss landscape produces case (iii); the families validated in this paper (Gaussian mixtures, reduced-rank regression) all have smooth fibers, and the standard combinatorial multi-collapse cases above are covered by case (ii).

Cross-coupling in the multi-degenerate case.

The decoupled assumption in Proposition
10
excludes cross-coupling between degenerate directions. When Fisher cross-terms
F_{u_{i}u_{j}}
create super-degenerate eigenvectors (
\lambda_{\min}\sim t^{a_{1}+a_{2}}
where
a_{i}
are individual exponents), curvature in the
(u_{i},u_{j})

cross-plane
can diverge faster than in any single
(u_{i},v)
plane. Numerical verification for the 2-component Gaussian mixture merge (3-parameter model) shows
|K_{\max}|\sim\delta^{-6}
in the
(\bar{\mu},w)
cross-plane, versus
\delta^{-3}
from the single-direction prediction. The product lower bound in Proposition
10
captures the per-direction contributions but not this cross-coupling effect, which can only increase the actual high-curvature volume. A tight multi-degenerate volume formula would require analyzing curvature in all
\binom{r}{2}
cross-planes.

Selection-rule recovery on Gaussian-mixture boundaries.

The selection rule (Theorem
3
) recovers
\hat{\lambda}
from the Fisher eigenvalue with exponent
2(k-1)
on each transversal singular family. We check the two canonical boundaries, the weight singularity
w\to 0
and the merge
\mu_{1}\to\mu_{2}
.

Part (a): Weight singularity (tangential).
At
w\to 0
, the degenerate direction is
\mu_{1}
(the mean of the vanishing component), but this direction lies
within
the singular set
\{w=0\}
: changing
\mu_{1}
at
w=0
does not change the distribution (
K(w{=}0,\mu_{1})=0
for all
\mu_{1}
). Hence Theorem
2
’s assumption
K(\theta_{0}+tu)=ct^{2k}
along the degenerate direction is violated: the KL is identically zero, not a polynomial in
t
.

The Fisher diagonal entry
F_{\mu_{1}\mu_{1}}=w^{2}\int[(x{-}\mu_{1})/\sigma^{2}]^{2}\phi_{1}^{2}/p_{\theta}\,dx
. Naïvely, the integral converges to a constant as
w\to 0
, predicting
F_{\mu_{1}\mu_{1}}\sim w^{2}
. However, near
x=\mu_{1}
where
p_{\theta}\approx w\phi_{1}
, the integrand is
O(\phi_{1}/w)
, contributing an
O(1/w)
correction to the integral. Hence
F_{\mu_{1}\mu_{1}}\sim w
(not
w^{2}
) at leading order, with a slowly varying correction.

The remaining eigenvalues are not constant:
F_{ww}
grows as
w\to 0
(the score
(\phi_{1}{-}\phi_{2})/p_{\theta}
diverges as
p_{\theta}\to\phi_{2}
). Numerically, fitted exponents are
1.11
,
-0.16
,
-0.81
, giving
\kappa\sim\delta^{-1.92}
and
\hat{\lambda}=0.256
. The approximate agreement with
\lambda=1/4
is empirical: the compounding of a slower-than-predicted
\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}
decay with
\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}
growth yields a condition number exponent that coincidentally approximates
-1/(2\lambda)=-2
.

Part (b): Merge singularity (transversal).
At
\mu_{1}\to\mu_{2}
, the degenerate direction is
\delta=\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}
. We have
K(\delta)=c\delta^{4}+O(\delta^{5})
for symmetric weights, so
k=2
. The merge direction is transversal to the singular set
\{\delta=0\}
, satisfying Theorem
2
’s assumption. Direct computation gives
\lambda_{\min}(F)=\Theta(\delta^{2})
(matching the prediction
2(k{-}1)=2
), recovering
\hat{\lambda}=1/(2k)=1/4
.

Parts (a) and (b) bracket the rule’s precondition. At the merge, the degenerate direction leaves the singular set and the KL grows as
\delta^{4}
, so the recovery returns
\hat{\lambda}=1/4
exactly. At the weight boundary, the degenerate direction lies inside
\{w=0\}
where the KL stays identically zero; Theorem
2
’s polynomial-KL hypothesis fails there, and the near-match to
1/4
is coincidental, the condition-number exponents happening to compound to
-2
. The rule reaches transversal degeneracies; a tangential one enters only after the fiber and its normal bundle are identified (the fiber-structure remark above).

The same transversal recovery holds across the three families of Table
8
: on the deep-linear reduced-rank model the full Fisher splits into
{\sim}55
gauge-killed eigenvalues,
{\sim}15
constant, and
5
–
6
transversal at exponent
\approx 2(k-1)
, the structure the selection rule predicts. Generalisation to arbitrary analytic families is open, but the consistency across qualitatively different singularity structures (multi-degenerate merges and gauge-coupled rank deficiency) suggests the rule is general for transversal singularities.

Part III  The bridge framework for deep networks

Part II read the rate off a single dead direction in one Fisher matrix. A trained network is deeper and messier: its Fisher is layered, carries a reparameterisation gauge that contributes many uninformative near-zero eigenvalues, and is assembled from repeated architectural primitives. This part lifts the rate primitive to that setting, the bridge’s deep-network half, in three moves. The multi-layer K-FAC bridge (Section
7
) carries the exponent across layers as a per-layer rate ladder, read in coordinates that quotient out the gauge zeros; within it, a forward-backward duality (Section
7.1
) makes the two K-FAC factors mirror images whose product is depth-independent. Composition additivity (Section
8
) adds rates across stacked blocks where a scalar-transfer hypothesis holds, and marks where pure attention chains break it. The architectural instantiations (Section
9
) then derive a closed-form rate for each primitive of a modern transformer from the primitive’s own algebra. One KL-order exponent runs through all three; only its reading point changes.

Reach.

These results are architecture-specific, the first narrowing from Part II’s model-agnostic reach. The K-FAC bridge assumes a layered architecture with canonical alignment of the dead direction across layers; composition additivity assumes the scalar-transfer hypothesis, which MLP and pre-norm residual chains satisfy and pure attention chains break at depth
\geq 4
; and each architectural instantiation carries its own assumptions, named where it is stated. The exception is the
\sigma_{\min}
depth-invariance corollary (Corollary
58
): it makes no canonical-alignment assumption, depending only on the additive-identity skip structure, and holds for any residual DAG (the directed acyclic computation graph of a residual network) with exact-identity skips regardless of what lies on the weight edges.

7  The multi-layer K-FAC bridge

Lifting the single-direction rate to a deep network runs straight into the reparameterisation gauge. A layered Fisher has
\sim h^{2}L
parameters and an inner-matrix gauge group
GL(h)^{L-1}
that contributes
\sim h^{2}(L-1)
near-zero eigenvalues independent of the singular geometry, so the obvious diagnostic
\lambda_{\min}(F_{\rm raw})
on the full Fisher is dominated by gauge zeros and says nothing about the singularity rate. K-FAC
(
Martens and Grosse 2015
)
is the fix: factorise the per-layer Fisher as
F_{\ell}\approx A_{\ell}\otimes G_{\ell}
. The block-diagonal restriction discards the cross-layer Fisher blocks where the gauge kernel lives, leaving per-layer factors whose smallest eigenvalues carry the singularity signal directly. This section lifts the rate into those coordinates. Under canonical alignment of the dead direction across layers, the per-layer K-FAC gradient factor inherits a layer-dependent rate
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})=\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
, and the dual activation factor
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})=\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})
. The product is layer-independent:
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})\,\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})=\Theta(t^{2(L-1)})
at every
\ell
. The proof reduces to a Schur complement on the non-dead Fisher block at each layer, controlled by the gauge-kernel structure of
GL(h)^{L-1}
acting on cross-layer Fisher blocks. We carry the framework in coordinates Amari’s lineage already uses; what is new is the rate.

The dead-direction rate
\Theta(t^{2K(\ell)})
across every bridge result in this paper (the feedforward case (Theorem
21
), the cross-entropy case (Theorem
36
), the residual-DAG case (Theorem
43
), the rectangular-width case (Theorem
103
), the multi-direction asymmetric case (Theorem
106
), the bias-augmented case (Theorem
112
), and the single-head attention case (Theorem
66
)) is established by a single shared mechanism: the dead-direction Schur reduction of Lemma
17
below. Each architectural extension is an instantiation that verifies (i) canonical alignment of the dead direction at every layer (or its rectangular / multi-direction / residual-DAG generalisation), (ii) Schur-reducible Fisher block in canonical coordinates (dead diagonal
\Theta(t^{2K(\ell)})
, dead-row off-diagonals
O(t^{K(\ell)})
, non-dead block
\succ 0
at
\Theta(1)
), and (iii) dead-direction-only
t
-scaling on the backward chain. The architecture-specific work is the verification; the rate conclusion follows from the lemma.

We refer to the result of Lemma
17
as the
Bridge Framework Lemma
and the architecture-specific verifications above as
instantiations
; Theorem
21
is the canonical instantiation, and the architectural subsections that follow instantiate the framework on the relaxations relevant to actual neural networks.

Consider an
L
-layer network
f(x;W_{1},\ldots,W_{L})=W_{L}\phi(W_{L-1}\phi(\cdots\phi(W_{1}x)))
with
W_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{h\times h}
, no biases, activation
\phi
in class (P1), (P2), or (P3) of the theorem. Data
x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{h})
, target
y=M^{*}x+\varepsilon
with
M^{*}=\diag(1,\ldots,1,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{h\times h}
(so the dead-direction component of the target is pure noise:
y^{(h)}=\varepsilon^{(h)}
),
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{h})
, MSE loss. The singular configuration is
W_{\ell}^{*}=\diag(1,\ldots,1,0)
in canonical coordinates for all
\ell
, with the same dead direction
e_{h}
shared across layers (the symmetric canonical-aligned approach). The transversal approach is
W_{\ell}(t)=W_{\ell}^{*}+t\cdot e_{h}e_{h}^{\top}=\diag(1,\ldots,1,t)
for all
\ell
. For activation class (P1) linear,
f(x;\theta(t))=(\prod_{\ell}W_{\ell}(t))\,x=\diag(1,\ldots,1,t^{L})\,x
, so the dead-channel residual is
f^{(h)}-y^{(h)}=t^{L}x^{(h)}-\varepsilon^{(h)}
and
K(\theta(t))-K(\theta^{*})=\tfrac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}t^{2L}\mathbb{E}[(x^{(h)})^{2}]=\tfrac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}t^{2L}
. For (P2) and (P3) the constant changes (e.g., factor
1/2
from the half-Gaussian survival event for ReLU) but the order is
\Theta(t^{2L})
in all three classes, so
k=L
uniformly.

Lemma 15

(Forward dead-component propagation)
.

Along the symmetric approach, for each
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
:
linear (P1)
a_{\ell}^{(h)}=t^{\ell}x^{(h)}
;
smooth (P2)
a_{\ell}^{(h)}=(\phi^{\prime}(0))^{\ell-1}t^{\ell}x^{(h)}+O(t^{\ell+1})
;
ReLU (P3)
a_{\ell}^{(h)}=t^{\ell}x^{(h)}\mathbf{1}[x^{(h)}>0]
for
t>0
.
For (P1) and (P2),
a_{\ell}^{(h)}=\Theta(t^{\ell})
pointwise on
\{x^{(h)}\neq 0\}
(full-probability event under Gaussian input). For (P3),
a_{\ell}^{(h)}=\Theta(t^{\ell})
on the survival event
\{x^{(h)}>0\}
(probability
1/2
) and is identically zero on
\{x^{(h)}<0\}
. In all three classes the second moment satisfies
\mathbb{E}_{x}[(a_{\ell}^{(h)})^{2}]=\Theta(t^{2\ell})
, with the constant
\mathbb{E}[(x^{(h)})^{2}]=1
for (P1),
(\phi^{\prime}(0))^{2(\ell-1)}
for (P2), and
\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[(x^{(h)})^{2}\mathbf{1}[x^{(h)}>0]]=1/2
for (P3). This is the form used by all downstream lemmas.

Proof.

Induction on
\ell
. Base
a_{1}^{(h)}=t\cdot x^{(h)}
. Step:
a_{\ell+1}^{(h)}=t\cdot\phi(a_{\ell}^{(h)})
. Linear gives
t^{\ell+1}x^{(h)}
. Smooth: Taylor expand
\phi(u)=\phi^{\prime}(0)u+O(u^{2})
and apply IH. ReLU: on
\{x^{(h)}>0\}
,
a_{1}^{(h)}>0
for
t>0
and
\phi
acts as identity, so
a_{\ell+1}^{(h)}=t\cdot a_{\ell}^{(h)}
recurses to
t^{\ell}x^{(h)}
; on
\{x^{(h)}<0\}
,
\phi(a_{1}^{(h)})=0
zeroes the chain at
\ell=2
and all subsequent
\ell
. The pointwise statement is therefore not almost-sure for ReLU, but the second-moment statement holds because both events have positive Gaussian probability and the surviving event contributes
\Theta(t^{2\ell})
.
∎

Lemma 16

(Backward dead-component magnitude)
.

\mathbb{E}[(\delta^{(L,h)})^{2}]=\sigma^{2}+O(t^{2L})=\Theta(1)
, and
\mathbb{E}[(\delta^{(\ell,h)})^{2}]=\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
for
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L-1\}
.

Proof.

By the canonical structure of
W_{\ell+1}(t)=\diag(1,\ldots,1,t)
,
(W_{\ell+1}^{\top}\delta^{(\ell+1)})^{(h)}=t\cdot\delta^{(\ell+1,h)}
(the
h
-th row of
W_{\ell+1}^{\top}
has only the diagonal entry
t
). The backward chain rule gives

\delta^{(\ell,h)}=\phi^{\prime}(a_{\ell}^{(h)})\cdot t\cdot\delta^{(\ell+1,h)}.

By Lemma
15
,
\phi^{\prime}(a_{\ell}^{(h)})=\Theta(1)
at leading order: linear gives
\phi^{\prime}\equiv 1
; smooth gives
\phi^{\prime}(0)+O(t^{\ell})
; ReLU gives
1
on the survival event
\{x^{(h)}>0\}
,
0
otherwise (probability
1/2
). At the output
\delta^{(L,h)}=-\varepsilon^{(h)}+\Theta(t^{L})
, so
\mathbb{E}[(\delta^{(L,h)})^{2}]=\sigma^{2}+O(t^{2L})
. Backward induction
\delta^{(\ell,h)}=\Theta(t)\cdot\delta^{(\ell+1,h)}
gives
\mathbb{E}[(\delta^{(\ell,h)})^{2}]=\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
for
\ell<L
.
∎

Lemma 17

(Bridge Framework Lemma: dead-direction Schur reduction)
.

The dead-direction-row entries of the K-FAC factors
A_{\ell}
(input covariance) and
G_{\ell}
(gradient covariance) admit, in canonical coordinates,

(A_{\ell})_{h,h}=\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)}),\quad(A_{\ell})_{h,j}=O(t^{\ell-1})\quad(j\neq h);

(G_{\ell})_{h,h}=\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)}),\quad(G_{\ell})_{h,j}=o(t^{2(L-\ell)})\quad(j\neq h);

and the non-dead block (entries with
j,j^{\prime}\neq h
) is positive-definite at
\Theta(1)
. Hence the Schur complement at the dead row gives
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})=\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})
and
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})=\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
, even when (for ReLU at
\ell\geq 2
) the
A
-side off-diagonals exceed the dead diagonal in raw magnitude.

Proof.

A_{\ell}
: entries.
In canonical coordinates the dead-direction activation factorises as
a_{\ell}^{(h)}=t^{\ell}f(x^{(h)})
from Lemma
15
(with
f(x)=x
,
f(x)=(\phi^{\prime}(0))^{\ell-1}x
, or
f(x)=x\mathbf{1}[x>0]
depending on activation class), so
\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(h)})=t^{\ell-1}\tilde{f}(x^{(h)})
for an analogous
\tilde{f}
, depending on
x^{(h)}
alone. Non-dead activations
\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(j)})
(
j\neq h
) propagate without the dead-direction
t
-factor and remain
\Theta(1)
, depending on
x^{(j)}
alone (because the canonical
W_{m}^{*}=\diag(1,\ldots,1,0)
structure routes coordinate
j
only to itself and the dead coordinate
h
only to itself). Hence
\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(h)})\perp\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(j)})
under Gaussian-isotropic input, and
(A_{\ell})_{h,j}=\mathbb{E}[\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(h)})]\cdot\mathbb{E}[\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(j)})]
. For (P1) linear
\mathbb{E}[t^{\ell-1}x^{(h)}]=0
, killing the cross term identically. For (P3) ReLU
\mathbb{E}[t^{\ell-1}x^{(h)}\mathbf{1}[x^{(h)}>0]]=t^{\ell-1}/\sqrt{2\pi}
, multiplied by
\mathbb{E}[\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(j)})]=\Theta(1)
, gives
(A_{\ell})_{h,j}=\Theta(t^{\ell-1})
. For (P2) smooth,
\mathbb{E}[\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(h)})]=O(t^{\ell})
since
\mathbb{E}[x^{(h)}]=0
, giving
(A_{\ell})_{h,j}=O(t^{\ell})
. The dead diagonal is
(A_{\ell})_{h,h}=\mathbb{E}[\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(h)})^{2}]=\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})
, and the non-dead block has
(A_{\ell})_{j,j^{\prime}}=\mathbb{E}[\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(j)})\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(j^{\prime})})]=\Theta(1)
as the input layer’s data covariance.

A_{\ell}
: eigenvalue via Schur.
Note that for ReLU at
\ell\geq 2
, the off-diagonal
\Theta(t^{\ell-1})
exceeds the dead diagonal
\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})
in raw magnitude. Despite this, the dead eigenvalue is determined by the Schur complement of the non-dead block. Writing
A_{\ell}
in block form as
\begin{pmatrix}M&v\\
v^{\top}&d\end{pmatrix}
with
M
the non-dead
(h-1)\times(h-1)
block (
\succ 0
,
\Theta(1)
),
d=(A_{\ell})_{h,h}=\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})
, and
v
the dead-row vector of off-diagonals (of order
\Theta(t^{\ell-1})
for ReLU, smaller for linear/smooth), the dead-direction eigenvalue is given by the Schur complement

d-v^{\top}M^{-1}v.

For ReLU,
v^{\top}M^{-1}v=\Theta(t^{\ell-1})\cdot\Theta(1)\cdot\Theta(t^{\ell-1})=\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})
. The Schur complement is
\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})-\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})
, and the leading constants combine: the dead-row’s outer-product structure
vv^{\top}
(rank 1 since
v
is computed via independence as
\mathbb{E}[\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(h)})]\cdot\mathbb{E}[\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(j)})]_{j}
) absorbs at most a fixed fraction of
d
’s magnitude, leaving the residual
\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})\cdot(1-c)
with
c<1
. Under the canonical setup of this lemma the constant is closed-form
c=(h-1)/(\pi(\pi+h-2))\leq 1/\pi
(Lemma
18
); under more general input distributions only the qualitative bound persists (Remark
19
). Hence
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})=\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})
. For (P1) linear and (P2) smooth the off-diagonals are smaller still (giving
v^{\top}M^{-1}v=O(t^{2\ell})
, strictly subleading), so the Schur complement is dominated by the diagonal
d
and the same conclusion holds without the rank-1 cancellation argument.

G_{\ell}
: entries (cleaner than
A_{\ell}
).
Symmetric argument using Lemma
16
:
\delta_{\ell}^{(h)}=t^{L-\ell}\cdot g(\varepsilon^{(h)},x^{(h)})
with
g
a (class-dependent) function of the dead-direction noise and input only, while
\delta_{\ell}^{(j)}
for
j\neq h
depends on
(\varepsilon^{(j)},x^{(j)})
. Independence across coordinates gives
\mathbb{E}[\delta_{\ell}^{(h)}\delta_{\ell}^{(j)}]=\mathbb{E}[\delta_{\ell}^{(h)}]\mathbb{E}[\delta_{\ell}^{(j)}]
. For the
G
-side, the noise
\varepsilon^{(h)}
has zero mean, and
\mathbb{E}[\delta_{\ell}^{(h)}]
is a
t^{L-\ell}
-prefactored expectation of
-\varepsilon^{(h)}+\Theta(t^{L})
, which is
O(t^{L-\ell+L})=O(t^{2L-\ell})
via the noise’s zero mean (cleaner than the
A
-side because
\varepsilon
is zero-mean while the activations are not). Hence
(G_{\ell})_{h,j}=O(t^{2L-\ell+L-\ell+0})=o(t^{2(L-\ell)})
, strictly subleading to the dead diagonal. The Schur reduction is therefore unnecessary for
G_{\ell}
:
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})=(G_{\ell})_{h,h}(1+o(1))=\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
.
∎

Lemma 18

(Schur-cancellation constant under isotropic Gaussian input)
.

Under the canonical setup of Lemma
17
with ReLU activation and isotropic Gaussian input
x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{h})
, the rank-1 outer-product cancellation in the
A_{\ell}
Schur complement at any
\ell\geq 2
obeys, in closed form,

c\;=\;\frac{h-1}{\pi(\pi+h-2)},\qquad c\;\leq\;\frac{1}{\pi}\;<\;1\quad\text{uniformly in }h\geq 2,

with
c\to 1/\pi
as
h\to\infty
. Consequently the dead-direction Schur complement satisfies the explicit lower bound

\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})\;\geq\;\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl(1-\tfrac{1}{\pi}\bigr)\cdot t^{2(\ell-1)}\;\approx\;0.341\,t^{2(\ell-1)}.

Proof.

By Lemma
15
for ReLU,
\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(h)})=t^{\ell-1}x^{(h)}\mathbf{1}[x^{(h)}>0]
, and the canonical-coordinate structure
W_{m}^{*}=\diag(1,\ldots,1,0)
recursing through idempotent ReLU gives
\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(j)})=\mathrm{ReLU}(x^{(j)})
for
j\neq h
. The dead diagonal is
d=(A_{\ell})_{h,h}=\mathbb{E}[(\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(h)}))^{2}]=t^{2(\ell-1)}\mathbb{E}[(x^{(h)})^{2}\mathbf{1}[x^{(h)}>0]]=\tfrac{1}{2}t^{2(\ell-1)}
. The dead-row off-diagonal
v_{j}=(A_{\ell})_{h,j}
factors by the canonical-coordinate independence of
x^{(h)}
and
x^{(j)}
as
\mu_{h}\mu_{j}
with
\mu_{h}=t^{\ell-1}/\sqrt{2\pi}
and
\mu_{j}=1/\sqrt{2\pi}
(half-Gaussian first moments), so
v=(t^{\ell-1}/(2\pi))\mathbf{1}_{h-1}
. The non-dead block has entries
M_{jj^{\prime}}=\tfrac{1}{2}
for
j=j^{\prime}
and
\tfrac{1}{2\pi}
for
j\neq j^{\prime}
, i.e.
M=\alpha I+\beta J
with
\alpha=\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{1}{2\pi}
,
\beta=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}
, and
J=\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
. Since
\mathbf{1}
is an eigenvector of
M
with eigenvalue
\alpha+(h-1)\beta=\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{h-2}{2\pi}=(\pi+h-2)/(2\pi)
,
M^{-1}\mathbf{1}=(2\pi/(\pi+h-2))\mathbf{1}
. Hence

v^{\top}M^{-1}v\;=\;\frac{t^{2(\ell-1)}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\cdot\frac{2\pi(h-1)}{\pi+h-2}\;=\;\frac{t^{2(\ell-1)}(h-1)}{2\pi(\pi+h-2)}.

Writing the Schur complement as
d(1-c)
with
c=(v^{\top}M^{-1}v)/d
gives the stated
c=(h-1)/(\pi(\pi+h-2))
. The bound
c\leq 1/\pi
follows from
(h-1)/(\pi+h-2)\leq 1
, with equality only in the limit
h\to\infty
. The lower bound on
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})
follows by substituting
c\leq 1/\pi
into
d(1-c)
.
∎

Numerical verification of the closed form.

The closed-form ratio
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})/(A_{\ell})_{h,h}=1-c
is verified by direct Monte Carlo computation of
A_{\ell}
on the canonical setup above. At
t=0.1
and
n=2\times 10^{6}
Gaussian input samples (seeds
\{0,1,2\}
), the empirical ratio matches the predicted
1-(h-1)/(\pi(\pi+h-2))
to relative error
<5\times 10^{-4}
at
\ell\in\{2,3\}
across
h\in\{4,8,16,32,64,128\}
(e.g.
h=4
: predicted
0.81427
, measured
0.81427
;
h=128
: predicted
0.68697
, measured
0.68720
). Finite-
t
Taylor corrections are visible at
t=0.5
(max relative error
\sim 3\%
at
h=4
) and shrink monotonically as
t\to 0
, consistent with the leading-order statement.

Remark 19

(Scope of the quantitative constant; per-instantiation status)
.

Lemma
18
pins down the Schur-cancellation constant
c
in the canonical setup of Lemma
17
(Theorem
21
) (ReLU, isotropic Gaussian input, canonical-coordinate alignment). The proof generalises with no structural change to any activation
\phi
for which the dead and non-dead activation moments factor by canonical-coordinate independence: writing
\mu_{h}=\mathbb{E}[\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(h)})]
,
\nu=\mathbb{E}[\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(j)})]
,
d=\mathbb{E}[\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(h)})^{2}]
,
d_{\mathrm{nd}}=\mathbb{E}[\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(j)})^{2}]
, the Schur-cancellation constant takes the form

c\;=\;\frac{\mu_{h}^{2}\,\nu^{2}\,(h-1)}{d\cdot\bigl(d_{\mathrm{nd}}+(h-2)\,\nu^{2}\bigr)},

recovering the ReLU value above when the corresponding half-Gaussian moments are substituted, and reducing to
c=0
trivially whenever
\nu=0
or
\mu_{h}=0
at leading order (linear, tanh, GELU, SiLU, and any LN-output input where coordinate means vanish by construction). The qualitative bound
c<1
is a property of the rank-1 outer-product structure together with
\mathrm{Var}[\phi(a_{\ell-1}^{(h)})]>0
(any non-constant
\phi
on a non-degenerate input), and persists in each architectural instantiation that invokes Lemma
17
. The per-instantiation status of the closed form is as follows.

•

Multi-direction (§
A.2
), MSE.
Trivially
c_{i}=0
for every direction
i
: by canonical-coordinate independence and zero-mean Gaussian noise, both the rank-1 outer-product term
v_{i}^{\top}M^{-1}v_{i}
and the cross-direction terms
w_{ij}^{2}/d_{j}
vanish at leading order (
v_{i},w_{ij}
are products of zero-mean expectations,
O(t^{\Pi^{(i)}+L})
), so the dead-direction Schur complement equals the dead diagonal at leading order without cancellation. The closed form is
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})\cap u^{(i)}=(G_{\ell})_{u^{(i)}u^{(i)}}(1+o(1))
.

•

Multi-direction, cross-entropy.
Non-trivial: the data-averaged softmax Hessian off-diagonal
-\mathbb{E}_{x}[p_{i}p_{j}]=\Theta(1)
makes
w_{ij}=\Theta(t^{\Pi^{(i)}+\Pi^{(j)}})
load-bearing, and the constant
c_{i}
depends on the data-averaged Hessian’s full block structure, which we do not pin down here.

•

Residual DAG (§
9.2
), single shortest path.
When
|\mathcal{P}^{*}(\ell)|=1
(typical for residual blocks of standard transformers, where one residual skip dominates the graph distance to each layer), the path-Gram at the dead row reduces to the parent’s structure with
L-\ell
replaced by
K(\ell)
, and the parent constant transfers verbatim:
c\leq 1/\pi
.

•

Residual DAG, multi-shortest-path.
When
|\mathcal{P}^{*}(\ell)|>1
(e.g. the two-layer block at the chain end of Corollary
48
), cross-path correlations enter the path-Gram off-diagonal and the constant depends on graph topology (how many shortest paths share which layers); the qualitative bound
c<1
still applies but the closed form is graph-specific.

We carry the qualitative
c<1
in the body and rely on Lemma
18
plus the per-case statements above for any instantiation that needs the explicit constant.

Lemma 20

(Non-dead entries (square-case; rectangular extension via Lemma
17
adapted to non-square widths))
.

For
j\neq h
:
(A_{\ell})_{jj}=\Theta(1)
and
(G_{\ell})_{jj}=\Theta(1)
, both independent of
t
at leading order (since non-dead diagonals of
W_{\ell}^{*}
are identity).

Algorithm 1

Per-Layer Structural Monitoring via K-FAC G-Factor

0:

Model with K-FAC-tracked layers; monitoring interval
K
; threshold
\tau
; at least
n/d\geq 100
gradient samples per tracked layer (so
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})
, hence
\kappa
, is not floor-biased)

1:

for
every
K
training epochs
do

2:

Compute K-FAC
G_{\ell}
for each tracked layer
\ell

3:

\kappa^{*}\leftarrow\max_{\ell}\kappa(G_{\ell})
;
\ell^{*}\leftarrow\arg\max_{\ell}\kappa(G_{\ell})

4:

Depth profile:
compute mean
\kappa(G_{\ell})
for early vs. deep layer groups

5:

if

\kappa^{*}>\tau

then

6:

Flag:
structural transition at layer
\ell^{*}

7:

Optional (quantitative
\hat{\lambda}
):
recover
(\hat{\lambda},m)
from the Fisher spectrum along the approach via the selection rule (Theorem
3
, §
5
) and its multi-component extension (Theorem
89
), without posterior sampling; fall back to SGLD-based LLC estimation
(
Lau et al. 2025
)
when the singular structure is not trajectory-identifiable

8:

end

if

9:

end

for

For parametric models where all parameters are meaningful (no overparameterization), Algorithm
1
simplifies: compute the full Fisher eigenvalues and distinguish singularity types by eigenvalue degeneracy count.

Estimand for quantitative recovery.

The flagged G-factor is the empirical Fisher (training-loss gradients), the right object for detecting a transition. Quantitative
\hat{\lambda}
recovery instead reads the expected (true) Fisher: off the optimum the empirical G-factor carries a residual prefactor that corrupts the rate exponent (Remark
4
, Corollary
39
).

Position in the observable hierarchy.

Algorithm
1
sits at the
periodic
tier (per-
K
-epochs) of the four-tier observable hierarchy:
\sigma_{\min}(X_{\ell})
on the residual stream is the
real-time
tier (cheap forward-pass at every step),
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})
via K-FAC is the
periodic
tier as deployed here,
u^{\top}Gu
at a fixed direction is the
checkpoint
tier (single backward pass on demand), and the full Fisher spectrum is the
offline
tier. All four are computable at LLM scale; the trade-off is cost-vs-information rather than feasibility. Algorithm
1
is appropriate when periodic structural-transition flags are needed during training.

Detecting task expansion from the K-FAC G-factor.

Task expansion is a common continual-learning transition: a trained classifier’s head grows to cover new classes, with the new outputs randomly initialised and the encoder reused. Such a transition is invisible to the loss alone, yet it leaves a sharp signature in the gradient geometry, because the fresh outputs inject a high-variance gradient block that the old, near-converged classes lack. The G-factor
G_{\mathrm{head}}
isolates that signature from the input-side conditioning, and the mechanism below shows
\kappa(G_{\mathrm{head}})
spikes at the transition.

For a linear layer
y=Wx+b
, the K-FAC approximation
(
Martens and Grosse 2015
)
gives
F_{\ell}\approx A_{\ell}\otimes G_{\ell}
with
A_{\ell}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n}a_{n}a_{n}^{\top}
(input covariance) and
G_{\ell}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n}g_{n}g_{n}^{\top}
(gradient covariance).

Protocol.
In the controlled task-expansion verification setup analysed here, the classification head is
expanded
from
k_{1}
to
k_{2}>k_{1}
outputs: old
k_{1}
output weights are preserved (assumption A2), new
k_{2}-k_{1}
outputs are randomly initialized (A1), and the encoder is unchanged (A3).

Proof.

Part (a).
The per-sample output gradient decomposes as
g_{n}=(g_{n}^{(\mathrm{old})},g_{n}^{(\mathrm{new})})\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-k_{1}}
. By (A2), the old class outputs are near-converged, so per-sample losses in old class directions are small:
\mathbb{E}[g_{n}^{(\mathrm{old})}(g_{n}^{(\mathrm{old})})^{\top}]=\sigma_{\mathrm{old}}^{2}I_{k_{1}}+E_{\mathrm{old}}
where
\sigma_{\mathrm{old}}^{2}
is small and
E_{\mathrm{old}}
is a small perturbation. By (A1), the new class outputs are randomly initialized: per-sample losses are large, giving
\mathbb{E}[g_{n}^{(\mathrm{new})}(g_{n}^{(\mathrm{new})})^{\top}]=\sigma_{\mathrm{new}}^{2}I_{k_{2}-k_{1}}+E_{\mathrm{new}}
with
\sigma_{\mathrm{new}}^{2}\gg\sigma_{\mathrm{old}}^{2}
.

By (A1), the new weights are drawn independently of the prior task’s training history, so the cross-covariance
\mathbb{E}[g_{n}^{(\mathrm{new})}(g_{n}^{(\mathrm{old})})^{\top}]
is zero in expectation over the weight initialization (the new output directions are random relative to the learned old-class structure). Hence
G_{\mathrm{head}}
is approximately block-diagonal:

G_{\mathrm{head}}\approx\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{\mathrm{new}}^{2}I_{k_{2}-k_{1}}&0\\
0&\sigma_{\mathrm{old}}^{2}I_{k_{1}}\end{pmatrix}+E,

with
\|E\|_{\mathrm{op}}
small relative to
\sigma_{\mathrm{new}}^{2}
. The eigenvalues of
G_{\mathrm{head}}
are approximately
\{\sigma_{\mathrm{new}}^{2},\ldots,\sigma_{\mathrm{old}}^{2},\ldots\}
, giving
\kappa(G_{\mathrm{head}})\geq\sigma_{\mathrm{new}}^{2}/\sigma_{\mathrm{old}}^{2}-O(\|E\|/\sigma_{\mathrm{old}}^{2})
.

The input covariance
A_{\mathrm{head}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n}a_{n}a_{n}^{\top}
depends on penultimate-layer activations, which by (A3) are unchanged at the transition. Hence
\kappa(A_{\mathrm{head}})
is stable across the transition. The raw K-FAC Fisher
\kappa(F_{\mathrm{head}})=\kappa(A_{\mathrm{head}})\cdot\kappa(G_{\mathrm{head}})
is dominated by
\kappa(A)\sim 10^{12}
in overparameterized models; the
G
-factor isolates the task-change signal.

Part (b): Signal origin.
For hidden layer
\ell
, the gradient is
g_{n}^{\ell}=J_{\ell}^{\top}g_{n}^{\mathrm{head}}
where
J_{\ell}=\partial y/\partial h^{\ell}
is the Jacobian (fixed at transition by A3). The gradient covariance at layer
\ell
is:

G_{\ell}=J_{\ell}^{\top}G_{\mathrm{head}}J_{\ell}.

The head’s
G_{\mathrm{head}}
directly encodes the new/old class variance separation (part (a)); hidden layers see this through the linear transformation
J_{\ell}
, which can amplify, attenuate, or rotate the anisotropy depending on its singular value structure. The bound
\kappa(J^{\top}AJ)\leq\kappa(J)^{2}\kappa(A)
gives an upper bound on
\kappa(G_{\ell})
in terms of
\kappa(G_{\mathrm{head}})
and
\kappa(J_{\ell})
, but does
not
imply
\kappa(G_{\ell})\leq\kappa(G_{\mathrm{head}})
: hidden layers could in principle have larger
\kappa
if
J_{\ell}
concentrates the anisotropy. What (b) establishes is that the head is the
origin
of the task-change signal: hidden layers’ anisotropy is derived from the head’s via
J_{\ell}
, not generated independently. The temporal ordering of the cascade (head first, then deeper layers) is an empirical observation consistent with
J_{\ell}
initially attenuating the signal, but this ordering is not a formal consequence of (A1)–(A3) alone.

∎

Scope.

Parts (a)–(b) are proved under assumptions (A1)–(A3), which hold by construction in the controlled task-expansion verification setup. Extension to natural structural transitions (data quality changes, fine-tuning) lies outside these assumptions. The connection between
\kappa(G_{\ell})
and RLCT singularities is established for
L
-layer networks with smooth or ReLU activations (Theorem
21
).

Theorem 21

(Multi-layer K-FAC bridge)
.

Consider an
L
-layer network with shared hidden width
h
, weights
W_{\ell}
, MSE loss, and Gaussian-isotropic input. Let
W_{\ell}(t)=W_{\ell}^{*}+t\cdot e_{h}e_{h}^{\top}
for all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
be the symmetric canonical-aligned transversal approach to the singular minimum where every layer shares the dead direction
u=e_{h}
(so
W_{\ell}^{*}=\diag(1,\ldots,1,0)
has the same dead row across layers). For activation classes (P1) linear, (P2) smooth-nonlinear with
\phi^{\prime}(0)\neq 0
, and (P3) ReLU,

(a)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell}(\theta(t)))\;=\;C_{\ell}\cdot t^{2(L-\ell)}\cdot\bigl(1+r_{\ell}(t)\bigr)
for
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L-1\}
, with
C_{\ell}>0
and
r_{\ell}(t)\to 0
as
t\to 0
(identically zero for linear and ReLU);

(b)

\lambda_{\min}(G_{L}(\theta(t)))=\Theta(1)
;

(c)

the shallowest-layer rate
2(L-1)
matches Theorem
2
with KL order
k=L
;

(d)

the raw Fisher has
\sim h^{2}(L-1)
near-zero eigenvalues from cross-layer gauge directions associated with the inner-matrix reparameterisation group
GL(h)^{L-1}
, so
\kappa(F_{\mathrm{raw}})
is uninformative; K-FAC’s block-diagonal approximation discards these cross-layer blocks and exposes the per-layer dead-diagonal rate within each
G_{\ell}
;

(e)

(
A-factor dual and A–G duality.
) By the same Schur reduction applied to forward activations, the activation factor satisfies
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell}(\theta(t)))=C_{\ell}^{A}\cdot t^{2(\ell-1)}\cdot(1+r_{\ell}^{A}(t))
with the same activation-class dependence; consequently
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})=\Theta(t^{2(L-1)})
is layer-independent and recovers Theorem
2
’s global rate at
k=L
.

Sketch.

In canonical coordinates
G_{\ell}
is block-diagonal at leading order; the dead diagonal
\mathbb{E}[(\delta_{\ell}^{(h)})^{2}]
accumulates one factor of
t
per weight matmul on the backward path to layer
\ell
, giving
\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
. Non-dead diagonals stay
\Theta(1)
, so the smallest eigenvalue is the dead entry. The inner-matrix reparameterisation group
GL(h)^{L-1}
contributes a
t
-independent kernel of rank
\sim h^{2}(L-1)
to the raw Fisher
across
layer pairs
(W_{\ell},W_{\ell+1})
; K-FAC’s block-diagonal approximation discards these cross-layer Fisher blocks, leaving each per-layer block
G_{\ell}
to expose the dead-diagonal rate directly. Full proof: §
7
.
∎

Remark 22

(Three readings of the ladder)
.

The same content takes three useful forms.
As a per-layer ladder.

\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})
has slope
2(L-\ell)
versus
\log t
, monotone-decreasing toward the input: the deepest hidden layer reads
\Theta(1)
, the shallowest reads
\Theta(t^{2(L-1)})
. The slope of each rung is one integer step lower than the rung above.
As an A–G duality.
Whichever side of the K-FAC product carries the rate at layer
\ell
, the other side compensates:
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})=\Theta(t^{2(L-1)})
is layer-independent. The forward and backward signals share one global exponent; only the location of that exponent on the ladder rotates with
\ell
.
As a gauge-quotient statement.
The raw Fisher’s
\sim h^{2}(L-1)
near-zero eigenvalues are gauge zeros of
GL(h)^{L-1}
acting on cross-layer Fisher blocks. K-FAC’s block-diagonal restriction projects orthogonal to those gauge directions and leaves the per-layer block to carry the singularity signal. The ladder is what the rate primitive looks like in coordinates aligned to the network’s layered structure rather than its raw parameter vector. Figure
2
(b) shows the four rungs at
L=4
.

Remark 23

(Regime conditions on Theorem
21
)
.

The setup of Theorem
21
, made fully explicit in
Lemma
17
, places the trajectory in the

noisy regime
: target
y=M^{*}x+\varepsilon
with

\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{h})
along the dead direction

e_{h}
. The output backward signal
\delta^{(L,h)}=-\varepsilon^{(h)}+\Theta(t^{L})
inherits its
\Theta(1)
leading magnitude from the
noise variance
\sigma^{2}
, which then propagates inward as

\Theta(t^{L-\ell})
to give the stated
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})=\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
.

In the corresponding
noise-free regime
(
\sigma=0
, target

y=M^{*}x
exactly), the same algebra (Lemma
16

applied with the residual
-\varepsilon^{(h)}
replaced by
t^{L}x^{(h)}
) shifts the output backward signal to magnitude

\Theta(t^{L})
, propagating inward to
\Theta(t^{2L-\ell})
at layer

\ell
. This raises every per-layer rate by
+2L
in the exponent
(
+4
at
L=2
,
+6
at
L=3
, etc.):

\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})=\Theta(t^{2(2L-\ell)})
, slope

2(2L-\ell)
at
\sigma_{\min}(W_{\ell})\sim t
. The two regimes share
the proof framework but produce different exponents, so a trajectory
measurement that quotes the rate must declare which regime it is in.
A task with irreducible empirical loss (for example the sparse-parity
task of
Barak et al. 2022
) sits in the
noisy regime; static observables such as the residual-stream

\sigma_{\min}
(Corollary
58
) and the LN-kernel
direction (§
9.3
) are regime-independent and carry
no such caveat.

Proof of Theorem
21

By Lemma
17
,
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})
is determined by the dead-direction Schur complement, with the cross-term contribution strictly subleading on the
G
-side (using zero-mean of the noise
\varepsilon
). The dead diagonal
(G_{\ell})_{h,h}=\mathbb{E}[(\delta^{(\ell,h)})^{2}]
is
\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
for
\ell<L
and
\Theta(1)
for
\ell=L
by Lemma
16
. Non-dead diagonals are
\Theta(1)
(the same backward-induction argument with non-dead initial gradient
\Theta(1)
yields
\Theta(1)
propagation since
W_{\ell+1}^{\top}
’s non-dead rows are
\Theta(1)
). Hence
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})
equals the dead diagonal at leading order, yielding parts (a) and (b).

Activation-class corrections.
For linear and ReLU,
\phi^{\prime}(a_{\ell}^{(h)})
is constant along the approach (identically
1
for linear, in
\{0,1\}
on the sign cell for ReLU), so no Taylor correction occurs and
r_{\ell}^{G}(t)=0
. For smooth
\phi
,
\phi^{\prime}(a_{\ell}^{(h)})=\phi^{\prime}(0)+\phi^{\prime\prime}(0)\cdot\Theta(t^{\ell})+O(t^{2\ell})
; accumulating through the
L-\ell
factors in the backward chain gives
r_{\ell}^{G}(t)=O(t)
, which
\to 0
as
t\to 0
.

Part (c): the layer-
1
rate
2(L-1)=2(k-1)
for
k=L
matches Theorem
2
’s prediction at the singular minimum’s KL order.

Part (d): the
L
-layer network has the inner-matrix reparameterisation group
GL(h)^{L-1}
(
W_{\ell+1},W_{\ell}\mapsto W_{\ell+1}P,P^{-1}W_{\ell}
preserves the product) acting on
L-1
adjacent-layer pairs. At a generic point its tangent action contributes
(L-1)h^{2}
near-zero eigenvalues to the raw Fisher; at the canonical singular point
W_{\ell}^{*}=\diag(1,\ldots,1,0)
each
W_{\ell}^{*}
is rank-deficient so the gauge stabilizer is non-trivial, and the effective gauge dimension is at most
(L-1)(h^{2}-1)\sim h^{2}(L-1)
for moderately large
h
. These near-zero eigenvalues live on cross-layer Fisher blocks and dominate
\kappa(F_{\mathrm{raw}})
, obscuring the per-layer singularity signal. K-FAC’s block-diagonal approximation
F\approx\bigoplus_{\ell}A_{\ell}\otimes G_{\ell}
has no cross-layer coupling by construction, so the gauge directions, which require non-zero cross-layer entries to be nontrivial, do not appear in the K-FAC factors. The per-layer dead-direction rate
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})=\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
is then directly detectable on each
h\times h
block
G_{\ell}
(and within-block, the dead-direction is the only
G_{\ell}
kernel since the non-dead diagonal of
G_{\ell}
is
\Theta(1)
, so K-FAC does not remove the within-block singularity signal we want to detect). We do not claim K-FAC quotients out the gauge in any geometric sense; rather, the block-diagonal restriction projects away from the cross-layer directions where the gauge kernel lives. ∎

A-factor dual and A–G duality (proof of Theorem
21
(e)).

The dual statement follows by Lemma
15
applied to
(A_{\ell})_{hh}=\mathbb{E}[(X_{\ell-1}^{(h)})^{2}]=\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})
on the same Schur structure: the dead-direction diagonal is
\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})
and non-dead diagonals are
\Theta(1)
, giving
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})=\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})
with
\lambda_{\min}(A_{1})=\Theta(1)
(data-distributed input) and
\lambda_{\min}(A_{L})=\Theta(t^{2(L-1)})
. Multiplying the per-layer bounds gives
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})\cdot\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})=\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})\cdot\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})=\Theta(t^{2(L-1)})
at every layer. ∎

Theorem 24

(A-factor dual; restatement of Theorem
21
(e), forward part)
.

Under the setup of Theorem
21
,
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell}(\theta(t)))=C_{\ell}^{A}\cdot t^{2(\ell-1)}\cdot(1+r_{\ell}^{A}(t))
with the same activation-class dependence as in Theorem
21
.

Corollary 25

(A–G duality; restatement of Theorem
21
(e), product part)
.

\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})\cdot\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})=\Theta(t^{2(L-1)})
at every
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
.

Corollary 26

(Activation
\sigma_{\min}
collapse rate)
.

Let
X_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times h}
be the matrix of post-activation outputs at layer
\ell
over
N
data points. Then
\sigma_{\min}(X_{\ell}(\theta(t)))=\sqrt{N}\cdot\Theta(t^{\ell})
for
\ell\geq 1
;
\sigma_{\min}(X_{0})=\Theta(\sqrt{N})
(
t
-independent).

Proof.

By the strong law,
X_{\ell}^{\top}X_{\ell}/N\to A_{\ell+1}
, and
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell+1})=\Theta(t^{2\ell})
by Theorem
24
. Taking square roots:
\sigma_{\min}(X_{\ell})=\sqrt{N}\cdot\Theta(t^{\ell})
.
∎

Corollary 27

(Quotient global Fisher rate matches parametric theory)
.

On the gauge quotient
\bar{\Theta}=\Theta/GL(h)^{L-1}
(the parameter space with the inner-matrix reparameterisation redundancy divided out; Corollary
78
), the whole-network (global) Fisher
\bar{F}
satisfies
\lambda_{\min}(\bar{F}(\bar{\theta}(\bar{t})))=\Theta(\bar{t}^{2(L-1)})
, matching Theorem
2
with
k=L
. The raw-parameter Fisher on
\Theta
itself has
h^{2}(L-1)
gauge-null directions independent of
t
(the inner-matrix reparameterisation orbit, see Theorem
21
(d)) and so does
not
attain this rate at
\lambda_{\min}(F_{\mathrm{raw}})
; the rate is recovered on the quotient or, equivalently, via the per-layer K-FAC blocks
F_{\ell}
which are gauge-orthogonal to the inner-block
GL(h)
null directions by construction (each
F_{\ell}
lives on the per-layer parameter slice transverse to the cross-layer gauge action).

Proof.

The per-layer K-FAC blocks satisfy
F_{\ell}\approx A_{\ell}\otimes G_{\ell}
exactly for linear (P1) and approximately for nonlinear
(
Martens and Grosse 2015
)
; around the canonical singular point the activation–gradient cross-block coupling does not affect the per-block dead-direction rate. The cross-block coupling is either the same order
t^{2(L-1)}
as the within-block rate, in which case it is a
GL(h)^{L-1}
gauge direction that the block-diagonal restriction discards (below), or it is
\Theta(1)
and confined to the non-dead subspace, where it cannot enter the per-block
smallest
eigenvalue. (The within-block rates of Theorem
21
are exact statements about
A_{\ell}
and
G_{\ell}
as defined; only the identification with the global quotient Fisher in this corollary uses the K-FAC block-diagonal restriction.) Each block’s smallest eigenvalue is
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})\cdot\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})=\Theta(t^{2(L-1)})
by Corollary
25
. The minimum over
\ell
is therefore
\Theta(t^{2(L-1)})
, attained equally at every layer. The raw Fisher’s
h^{2}(L-1)
gauge-null directions live on cross-layer Fisher blocks (they require non-zero off-block entries to be nontrivial) and are absent from the K-FAC block-diagonal restriction by construction. Identifying K-FAC’s per-layer blocks with the horizontal lift of the quotient Fisher (Lemma
76
: the Fisher vanishes on vertical
GL(h)^{L-1}
orbit directions), the per-layer K-FAC eigenvalue equals the quotient Fisher eigenvalue at the corresponding lift. The minimum on the quotient is therefore
\min_{\ell}\lambda_{\min}(F_{\ell})=\Theta(t^{2(L-1)})
.
∎

, and Table
