.

7.1  The A–G duality

Corollary
25
makes the forward and backward halves of the Fisher’s K-FAC factorisation dual.
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})
encodes the rate at which forward signal in the dead channel decays from the input, and
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})
encodes the rate at which backward gradient decays from the output. Their exponents add:
2(\ell-1)+2(L-\ell)=2(L-1)
at every layer. The product
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})\,\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})
recovers Theorem
2
’s global rate exponent at
k=L
, independent of which layer one measures. The duality is a statement entirely in Amari’s metric language (it depends only on the Kronecker structure of the per-layer Fisher), but its exponent is Watanabe’s KL order propagating through the layered architecture. Measuring either side alone recovers the global rate.

The duality pairs a gradient-covariance dead direction
g_{\min}
with an
input-covariance dead direction
a_{\min}
. Their Kronecker product names the
flattest parameter direction of the layer, which gives a constructive way to read
the dead direction off the two small factors.

Corollary 28

(Kronecker lift of the dead direction)
.

Adopt the setup of Theorem
21
, with per-layer K-FAC factor

F_{\ell}\approx A_{\ell}\otimes G_{\ell}
, input covariance
A_{\ell}
, and gradient
covariance
G_{\ell}
. The eigenvectors of
A_{\ell}\otimes G_{\ell}
are the Kronecker
products
a_{i}\otimes g_{j}
of the factor eigenvectors, with eigenvalue

\lambda_{A,i}\,\lambda_{G,j}
. The smallest-eigenvalue parameter direction of
layer
\ell
is the bottom product
a_{\min}\otimes g_{\min}
, the rank-one
weight increment

u_{\ell}\;=\;g_{\min}\,a_{\min}^{\top}

on
W_{\ell}
. This direction is unique when the bottom factor eigenvalues are simple; under degeneracy it spans the corresponding Kronecker eigenspace, of which
u_{\ell}
is one representative. It lifts the gradient-covariance dead direction
g_{\min}
to
parameter space through the input factor
a_{\min}
. At the canonical singular
configuration
a_{\min}=g_{\min}=e_{h}
, so
u_{\ell}=e_{h}e_{h}^{\top}
recovers the
dead row of
W_{\ell}
. The lift reads from the two
h\times h
factor
eigendecompositions, and the
P\times P
parameter Fisher is never formed.

Proof.

The mixed-product property gives

(A_{\ell}\otimes G_{\ell})(a_{i}\otimes g_{j})=(A_{\ell}a_{i})\otimes(G_{\ell}g_{j})=\lambda_{A,i}\lambda_{G,j}\,(a_{i}\otimes g_{j})
, so each
a_{i}\otimes g_{j}
is an
eigenvector with eigenvalue
\lambda_{A,i}\lambda_{G,j}
. Since
A_{\ell}
and

G_{\ell}
are covariance (positive semidefinite) factors, their eigenvalues are
non-negative, so the product is smallest at the two bottom factor eigenvalues,
hence the bottom parameter direction is
a_{\min}\otimes g_{\min}
. In the vectorisation under which

F_{\ell}\approx A_{\ell}\otimes G_{\ell}
holds,
a_{\min}\otimes g_{\min}
is the
rank-one matrix
g_{\min}a_{\min}^{\top}
. The canonical alignment of
Corollary
25
sets both bottom factor eigenvectors to the dead
unit
e_{h}
, which gives
u_{\ell}=e_{h}e_{h}^{\top}
.
∎

The order lives in the cross-layer joint mode.

A single per-layer lift
u_{\ell}=g_{\min}a_{\min}^{\top}
is a gauge direction.
Stepping one layer’s dead row alone leaves the network map unchanged at leading
order, so
u_{\ell}^{\top}F(\theta(t))\,u_{\ell}
carries rate

\alpha\approx 0
. The finite KL order at a dead unit sits in the
canonical-aligned joint mode. That mode steps the unit’s incoming row

(W_{\ell})_{h,:}
and outgoing column
(W_{\ell+1})_{:,h}
together, the
construction of the two-layer worked example
(§
4.3
) under Theorem
2
’s
symmetric approach. The K-FAC factors identify which unit is dead. The
order-carrying parameter direction is then the cross-layer joint mode at that
unit, and
k
reads off it by the outward rate scan of
Theorem
2
.

7.2  Empirical illustration: TMS canonical configuration

The Toy Model of Superposition
(
Elhage et al. 2022
)
, in its smallest canonical configuration, gives the cleanest experimental anchor for the per-layer rate ladder. The same architecture has been studied from the SLT side by
Chen et al. 2023
, who characterise the relationship between Watanabe’s free-energy phase transitions and the dynamical phase transitions seen under Adam training; the parametric freeze-probe reading below is complementary, isolating the static rate exponent that the bridge theorem predicts at canonical alignment. Take the
L=2
linear autoencoder
X\mapsto W_{2}W_{1}X
with
d_{\mathrm{in}}=6
,
d_{\mathrm{hid}}=2
, and a rank-
1
target
W_{1}^{\star}=e_{1}f_{1}^{\top}
,
W_{2}^{\star}=(W_{1}^{\star})^{\top}
. The dead-direction parametric perturbation is the symmetric ray
W_{1}(t)=W_{1}^{\star}+t\,e_{2}v_{0}^{\top}
,
W_{2}(t)=W_{1}(t)^{\top}
, for
v_{0}
a unit vector in input space; this is the canonical-aligned approach the bridge theorem covers.

Sweeping
t\in[10^{-4},10^{-1}]
on a
30
-seed grid (the seed randomises only
v_{0}
; the asymptotic rate is independent of
v_{0}
but the leading prefactor is not) and fitting the slope of
\log\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})
versus
\log t
at each layer:

\text{slope}_{\mathrm{hidden}}\;=\;1.9999\pm 0.0009,\qquad\text{slope}_{\mathrm{input}}\;=\;3.9925\pm 0.0392.

TMS uses tied weights (
W_{2}=W_{1}^{\top}
), so the symmetric perturbation
W_{1}(t)=W_{1}^{\star}+t\,e_{2}v_{0}^{\top}
,
W_{2}(t)=W_{1}(t)^{\top}
moves both layers simultaneously: each backward path through the hidden layer’s Fisher block picks up two
t
-factors (one from
W_{1}
on the forward side, one from
W_{2}
on the backward side), giving
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\mathrm{hidden}})=\Theta(t^{2})
; the input layer’s Fisher block picks up two further
t
-factors from the doubled chain (
W_{2}W_{1}
in both directions), giving
\Theta(t^{4})
. The tied-weight rate ladder
(\mathrm{hidden},\mathrm{input})=(2,4)
is the tied-weight specialisation of Theorem
21
’s untied prediction
2(L-\ell)\in\{0,2\}
at
L=2
, with each rate raised by
+2
from the tying-induced doubled
t
-dependence. Three-decimal agreement at the hidden layer; the input-layer dispersion (
\sigma\approx 0.04
) reflects the sensitivity of the leading prefactor to
v_{0}
at the more strongly degenerate end of the ladder.

The probed direction matters too. Computing
u^{\top}Gu
at the canonical dead direction gives slope
2.000\pm 0.001
at the hidden layer, matching the directional rate prediction independently of the spectrum-bottom reading. The two channels (the bottom Fisher eigenvalue and the directional Fisher rate along the canonical dead direction) agree to three decimals (
1.9999
vs
2.000
), as required by the bridge theorem in the canonical-aligned regime.

This is the parametric-freeze-probe form of the bridge prediction:
30
-seed multi-direction noise on
v_{0}
, no SGD, no optimiser-induced drift, and the rate exponents come out at integer values to three decimals. A separate
trajectory
reading of TMS, in which the same architecture is trained with Adam and exhibits a phase transition, is governed by Remark
80
(Adam non-equivariance under the gauge symmetries of TMS) and is outside the scope of the bridge theorem’s canonical-aligned regime; the parametric reading above is the cleanest theorem-match available, free of the optimiser-side caveats.

8  Composition additivity and its failure

Theorem
21
covers a single feedforward stack of weight matmuls. Real networks compose heterogeneous blocks (MLPs, attention, residual sub-paths), each with its own internal singular structure. The question this section asks is: given the per-block rate of each constituent, what rate does the composition exhibit? Under a scalar-transfer hypothesis that holds for MLP and pre-norm residual chains, the per-block KL orders compose additively: the dead-direction Fisher rate at the input of block
B_{i}
in a chain
M=B_{n}\circ\cdots\circ B_{1}
is
\Theta(t^{2\sum_{j\geq i}k_{j}^{\mathrm{bk}}})
. Pure attention chains break this hypothesis at depth
\geq 4
. Softmax cross-block coupling produces an empirical saturation at a rank-collapse depth
k^{\star}
, with refined closed-form rates
\alpha_{W_{O}}=4\min(k,k^{\star})
,
\alpha_{W_{V}}=\alpha_{W_{O}}+2
, and
\alpha_{W_{Q}}=\alpha_{W_{K}}=4p+2
at a probe block at position
p
in a chain of length
N
with
k=N-1-p
. The
W_{V}
offset and the
W_{Q}=W_{K}
formula are parameter-free and match the measured rates to integer slope precision; the
W_{O}
formula matches once its saturation depth
k^{\star}
is fit to the data (a closed-form expression for
k^{\star}(d,n_{h},\mathrm{seq\_len})
is open).

Definition 29

(Block rate)
.

A block
B
on canonical coordinates with shared dead direction
u=e_{h}
has
backward block rate

k_{B}^{\mathrm{bk}}\geq 0
if, given input dead-direction backward gradient
\delta_{u}^{\mathrm{out}}=\Theta(1)
, the block’s output is
\delta_{u}^{\mathrm{in}}=\Theta(t^{k_{B}^{\mathrm{bk}}})
with non-dead components
\Theta(1)
;
forward block rate

k_{B}^{\mathrm{fwd}}
is defined symmetrically on activations.

This result extends Theorem
21
from a single feedforward stack to sequential compositions of heterogeneous blocks with known per-block rates.

Theorem 30

(Composition additivity for heterogeneous blocks)
.

For a sequential composition
M=B_{n}\circ\cdots\circ B_{1}
of blocks
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
, suppose: (i) each block
B_{i}
has a canonical dead direction
e_{h}
in the coordinates inherited from
B_{i-1}
’s output (i.e., the inter-block embedding maps preserve the dead direction in canonical coordinates, the same hypothesis used in Theorem
21
); and (ii) each block has well-defined backward and forward rates
k_{i}^{\mathrm{bk}},k_{i}^{\mathrm{fwd}}
in the sense of Definition
29
, defined intrinsically as
k_{i}^{\mathrm{bk}}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\log\bigl(\mathbb{E}[(\delta_{u}^{\mathrm{in}})^{2}]/\mathbb{E}[(\delta_{u}^{\mathrm{out}})^{2}]\bigr)/\log t
(and analogously for
k_{i}^{\mathrm{fwd}}
on the forward graph). Then

\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\delta_{u}^{\mathrm{input\ of}\ B_{i}})^{2}\bigr]=\Theta\bigl(t^{2\sum_{j\geq i}k_{j}^{\mathrm{bk}}}\bigr),\qquad\mathbb{E}\bigl[(X_{u}^{\mathrm{output\ of}\ B_{i}})^{2}\bigr]=\Theta\bigl(t^{2\sum_{j\leq i}k_{j}^{\mathrm{fwd}}}\bigr),

under the additional hypothesis that each block’s dead-direction transfer is
scalar
(the dead-direction backward (resp. forward) Jacobian acts as a
t
-homogeneous scalar multiplier on the dead-direction component of its input).

The scalar-transfer hypothesis is satisfied by MLP and pre-norm residual chains in canonical coordinates and yields both directions of the formula. For pure attention chains at depth
n\geq 4
, the scalar-transfer hypothesis fails: softmax couples the dead-direction backward (resp. forward) signal at
B_{i}
to the cumulative forward (resp. backward) signal at upstream (resp. downstream) blocks, so the per-block transfer becomes context-dependent rather than scalar, and additivity breaks (Remark
32
).

Proof.

(a) Backward.
Induction on
n-i+1
. Base (
i=n
):
\mathbb{E}[(\delta_{u}^{\mathrm{out}\ \mathrm{of}\ B_{n}})^{2}]=\Theta(1)
by the output-layer base case (MSE loss; the output gradient inherits the
\sigma^{2}
Gaussian noise floor). Step: under the scalar-transfer hypothesis, the dead-direction backward Jacobian of
B_{i}
acts as a deterministic scalar multiplier
t^{k_{i}^{\mathrm{bk}}}
on the dead-direction component of its output gradient (this is the operational content of Definition
29
when applied in canonical coordinates; for MLP blocks it follows directly from Lemma
16
). Squaring and taking the expectation, we get
\mathbb{E}[(\delta_{u}^{\mathrm{input\ of}\ B_{i}})^{2}]=t^{2k_{i}^{\mathrm{bk}}}\cdot\mathbb{E}[(\delta_{u}^{\mathrm{input\ of}\ B_{i+1}})^{2}]=\Theta(t^{2(m_{i+1}+k_{i}^{\mathrm{bk}})})=\Theta(t^{2\sum_{j\geq i}k_{j}^{\mathrm{bk}}})
with
m_{i+1}=\sum_{j\geq i+1}k_{j}^{\mathrm{bk}}
.

The deterministic-scalar property is what rules out cross-block destructive interference: the second moment
\mathbb{E}[(\cdot)^{2}]
depends only on the magnitude of the scalar multiplier, not its sign, so even if the multiplier varies in sign across blocks (e.g., for a smooth
\phi
with
\phi^{\prime}(0)<0
), the rate composition is unchanged. The residual-DAG case is handled by the same construction used in the proof of Corollary
58
below: the residual graph admits a path-sum decomposition where each path carries a scalar
t
-prefactor, and the dominant path determines the rate. The MLP-only restriction in the backward direction reflects that for attention chains, the per-block dead-direction backward Jacobian is
not
a scalar multiplier but a context-dependent function of upstream forward activations (Remark
32
).

(b) Forward.
Symmetric induction from
X_{u}^{\mathrm{input\ of}\ B_{1}}=X_{0}^{(h)}=\Theta(1)
(Gaussian-isotropic input). Under the scalar-transfer hypothesis, each block
B_{i}
multiplies the dead-direction input by a deterministic scalar
t^{k_{i}^{\mathrm{fwd}}}
. For MLP and pre-norm residual chains the scalar-transfer hypothesis holds on the forward direction (residual blocks have
k^{\mathrm{fwd}}=0
via the identity skip dominating the weight branch in the path-sum, see Corollary
58
proof). For attention chains, by the same softmax-coupling argument as the backward direction, the forward dead-direction at
B_{i}
’s output couples to the forward dead-direction at earlier blocks via attention scores, and scalar-transfer can fail; we therefore restrict the forward statement to MLP and residual chains as well, matching the backward restriction.
∎

Corollary 31

(Reduction to basic bridges)
.

Setting each
B_{i}
to a single weight matmul (
k_{i}^{\mathrm{bk}}=k_{i}^{\mathrm{fwd}}=1
) recovers Theorem
21
, under the index-map convention that “input of
B_{i}
” is identified with the pre-activation at depth
\ell=i
on the parent’s depth indexing (so the cumulative backward sum
\sum_{j\geq i}k_{j}^{\mathrm{bk}}=L-i+1
matches Theorem
21
’s
L-\ell
at
\ell=i-1
, i.e., the input of block
i
equals the post-activation of layer
i-1
). Setting each
B_{i}
to a pre-norm residual block (
k_{i}=0
) gives rate
0
everywhere on the
residual-stream observable
, matching Corollary
58
; on the weight-branch observable inside each residual block the per-Linear rate
1
is unaffected by the skip and accumulates as in the basic bridge.

Remark 32

(Attention composition anomaly)
.

The clean additivity of Theorem
30
does
not
extend to pure attention chains (non-residual sequential self-attention blocks). Parametric probes at
n\in\{4,6\}
,
d=16
,
n_{h}=2
give component-wise rates that deviate systematically from the naive
k_{\mathrm{attn}}^{\mathrm{bk}}=2
per-block additivity:

•

W_{O}
at probe position
\ell

saturates
at
\alpha\approx 8
for
n-1-\ell\geq 3
instead of growing additively as
6,10,14,\ldots
;

•

W_{Q}
and
W_{K}
rates scale with
\ell
(position from the
input
) rather than
n-1-\ell
(position from the output), consistent with their gradient path coupling to the cumulative
forward
signal through softmax Jacobians rather than the cumulative backward chain;

•

the invariant
\alpha_{W_{V}}-\alpha_{W_{O}}=2
is preserved across all
(n,\ell)
tested, matching the single-block standalone offset (every attention block has one Linear’s worth of gradient-side
t
-factor, and the softmax coupling does not alter this within-block sequence).

Block-composition validation at
n=2
(the four ordered attention/MLP block pairs at
d=4
) was consistent with simple additivity; the breakdown is specific to deeper attention-only stacks (
n\geq 4
). The structural mechanism is formalised in Proposition
69
as three closed-form formulas (
\alpha_{W_{O}}(p,N)=4\min(N-1-p,k^{\star})
,
\alpha_{W_{V}}=\alpha_{W_{O}}+2
,
\alpha_{W_{Q}}=\alpha_{W_{K}}=4p+2
) derived from the cumulative softmax-Jacobian rank collapse mechanism. The
W_{V}
offset (
\alpha_{W_{V}}-\alpha_{W_{O}}=2
) and the
W_{Q}=W_{K}=4p+2
formula are parameter-free and match the measured rates to integer slope precision across all
24
measurement cells at
(N,p)\in\{(4,0),(4,2),(4,3),(6,0),(6,3),(6,5)\}
; the
W_{O}
formula matches once its saturation depth
k^{\star}
is fit to the
W_{O}
data. The saturation depth
k^{\star}
is empirically
2
at
(d,n_{h},\mathrm{seq\_len})=(16,2,4)
; a closed form for
k^{\star}
in terms of these parameters is open. On practical residual-wrapped transformers the anomaly is bypassed: Corollary
58
’s skip edge gives
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}=0
regardless of what lies on the weight edges, so residual-stream
\sigma_{\min}
predictions are unaffected. The anomaly affects internal component rates (
W_{O},W_{V},W_{Q},W_{K}
) within each block, not the residual stream observable.

Figure 6
:
Composition additivity (Theorem
30
). The two regimes the theorem distinguishes. (a) MLP chains at
N\in\{6,8,12\}
,
d=16
: per-component measured slopes lie on the
y=x
diagonal with the predicted sum
\sum_{j}k_{j}^{\mathrm{bk}}
, validating clean additivity across slopes
0
–
34
. The scalar-transfer hypothesis holds and block rates add. (b) Attention chains at
N\in\{4,6\}
:
W_{O}
saturates at
\alpha=8
for
k>k^{\star}=2
, deviating from naive additivity
4(N{-}1{-}p)
(dotted line). The refined formula
4\min(k,k^{\star})
(solid line) matches the measurement; the saturation is the softmax-Jacobian rank-collapse mechanism. Practical residual-wrapped transformers bypass this saturation: the skip dominates the residual stream regardless of what lies inside the attention block (Cor.
58
).

9  Architectural instantiations

Modern networks are built from a small set of repeated primitives, and the bridge instantiates at each. The primitives cover a standard pre-norm transformer’s forward map end to end: the
structural
primitives that carry the forward map (rectangular widths for embeddings and MLP projections, residual DAGs, biases), the
normalisation
layer (layer normalisation, with RMSNorm as its companion), the
gated feedforward
block (SwiGLU, the gated MLP of modern large language models; GeGLU and ReGLU follow the same factorisation), and the
sequence-mixing
block (single-head attention). Three
analysis-side extensions
broaden the rate theorem’s hypotheses to the settings these architectures train in: multi-direction singularities, cross-entropy loss, and non-canonical alignment. Each instantiation has the same shape: a per-block KL order, the rate composing through the additivity theorem (Section
8
) where the scalar-transfer hypothesis holds, and the resulting singular-geometry consequence. Every predicted rate is checked by a static parametric freeze-probe on the theorem’s own trajectory; these validations are collected in Appendix
B.2
and summarised in Figure
9
.

Table
3
lays out how each primitive acts on the canonical rate ladder, and the table splits the primitives into the two groups this section treats differently. Most leave the ladder intact: rectangular widths, multi-direction singularities, and biases carry the per-block KL order through composition unchanged, the SwiGLU gate contributes a fixed forward order, and non-canonical alignment marks the boundary where a learned trajectory leaves the regime the ladder describes. These carry no behaviour beyond what the composition theorem already gives, so we record their rates in the table and develop them, with their freeze-probe validations, in the architectural catalogue of Appendix
A
. The four primitives developed here are the ones that change the picture, each introducing a phenomenon the bare ladder does not capture: cross-entropy loss moves the analysis onto the expected Fisher and brings in the logit-shift gauge (Section
9.1
); the residual skip makes the bottom-of-spectrum
\sigma_{\min}
depth-invariant and readable from a single forward pass (Section
9.2
); LayerNorm both lowers the integer ladder to a fractional rate and pins an algebraic kernel direction (Section
9.3
); and stacking single-head attention breaks composition additivity through the cumulative softmax Jacobian (Section
9.4
).

Two of these four are of a different character from the rate-decay theorems, and they hold across the optimiser regimes considered in this paper. The LayerNorm kernel direction
\gamma^{-1}/\|\gamma^{-1}\|
(Proposition
63
) is an exact algebraic identity:
\mathrm{cov}(\mathrm{LN}(X))\,v^{*}=0
holds for any input distribution because of LN’s mean-subtraction projector, with
v^{*}
fixed by the affine parameter alone. The residual-DAG bottom-of-active-spectrum statement (Corollary
58
) follows from the path-cost decomposition of the forward chain rule under a non-cancellation hypothesis on the residual branch, and is empirically falsifiable per architecture from a single forward pass. Both depend on the forward-map algebra rather than on a canonical-aligned trajectory, which is why they hold outside the optimiser-regime scope that bounds the rate predictions.

The list is not exhaustive. It covers the primitives that compose a pre-norm transformer’s forward map, with residual DAGs extending to ResNet-style skips. Primitives outside that slice, including multi-head and grouped-query attention, rotary position embeddings, and mixture-of-experts routing, are natural extensions following the same per-primitive recipe (a per-block KL order entering the composition) and are not treated here.

Table 3
:
How each architectural primitive acts on the canonical rate ladder. The five entries above the rule carry the ladder through composition (or, for non-canonical alignment, mark where it ceases to hold) without introducing new phenomena; they are stated and freeze-probe-validated in the architectural catalogue, Appendix
A
. The four below the rule each introduce behaviour the bare ladder does not capture and are developed in this section.

Primitive

Effect on the canonical ladder

Result

Rectangular widths

preserved, per factor

Thm
103

Multi-direction / asymmetric

per-direction ladder

Thm
106

Biases

preserved

Thm
112

SwiGLU gate

forward order
k=3

Prop
114

Non-canonical alignment

ladder ceases to hold

Prop
119

Cross-entropy loss

expected-Fisher rate; logit-shift gauge

Thm
36

Residual DAG

\sigma_{\min}
depth-invariant (rate
0
)

Cor
58

LayerNorm

fractional rate; kernel
\gamma^{-1}/\|\gamma^{-1}\|

Thm
60
, Prop
63

Single-head attention

standalone
k=2
; composition anomaly

Thm
66

9.1  Cross-entropy loss

Theorem
21
is stated for squared-error loss with Gaussian noise, where the output-layer
G_{L}=\sigma^{-2}I
is exactly isotropic and the residual at convergence is the noise
\varepsilon
. For supervised classification the loss is cross-entropy, and at a memorising parameter the empirical output gradient
g_{\mathrm{head}}=\mathrm{softmax}(z)-e_{y}
collapses to zero: the same denominator that kept the MSE rate readable is gone. The natural move is to replace the empirical Fisher with the
expected
Fisher (sample the label from the model’s own predictive distribution rather than the training label); the data-averaged softmax Hessian
H(x;\theta)
is bounded below on
\{1\}^{\perp}
by a non-degeneracy assumption on the data-averaged class distribution, and the base case of the bridge proof carries through with
c_{0}
replacing
\sigma^{-2}
. The rest of the backward-chain argument transfers unchanged.

Setup.

Take the same
L
-layer architecture and symmetric-approach parametrization
\theta(t)
as in Theorem
21
, with the square-hidden assumption specialized to
C
output classes by setting the final layer width equal to
C
(i.e.
h_{L}=C
and
h_{\ell}=h=C
for all
\ell
). The rectangular extension
h_{L}=C\neq h
combines with the present argument by composing this theorem’s output-head replacement (Lemma
33
) with the rectangular non-dead block control of
103
, since the two extensions touch disjoint parts of the proof; we discuss the joint scope in Remark
38
. The hidden-layer activations
\phi
still belong to classes (P1)–(P3); the softmax map is applied
only
at the output head, to convert logits into probabilities, and is not itself one of the hidden activations:

z(x;\theta)=f(x;\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{C},\qquad p_{\theta}(y\mid x)=\mathrm{softmax}(z(x;\theta))_{y}.

Let
q(x)
be the data distribution on
x
(independent of
\theta
). The
expected
Fisher is

F_{\mathrm{exp}}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim q}\mathbb{E}_{y\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\bigl[\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(y\mid x)\,\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(y\mid x)^{\top}\bigr].

The K-FAC G-factor
G_{\ell}
keeps its definition as the backward-gradient Gram at the pre-activation of layer
\ell
, where per-sample gradients are taken at labels sampled from
p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)
(rather than at observed training labels). Since
C=h
, the dead direction index
h
used throughout the bridge proof equally indexes a coordinate in the output logit space.

Lemma 33

(Output-head covariance under expected Fisher)
.

Let
\delta^{(L)}(x,y)=\mathrm{softmax}(z(x;\theta))-e_{y}
be the per-sample output gradient. Under expected Fisher, the conditional covariance of
\delta^{(L)}
given
x
is

H(x;\theta)\;:=\;\mathbb{E}_{y\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)}\!\bigl[\delta^{(L)}(x,y)\,\delta^{(L)}(x,y)^{\top}\bigr]\;=\;\mathrm{diag}\bigl(p(x;\theta)\bigr)-p(x;\theta)\,p(x;\theta)^{\top},

with
p(x;\theta)=\mathrm{softmax}(z(x;\theta))
. Moreover, for any
v\in\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}
and any full-support
p
:

v^{\top}H(x;\theta)\,v\;=\;\mathrm{Var}_{y\sim p}\!\bigl[v_{y}\bigr]\;=\;\sum_{y}p_{y}\,v_{y}^{2}-\Bigl(\sum_{y}p_{y}v_{y}\Bigr)^{2}\;\geq\;0,

with equality iff
v
is constant on
\mathrm{supp}(p)
. Hence the nullspace of
H
is
\mathrm{span}(\mathbf{1})\cup\{e_{i}:p_{i}=0\}
; for full-support
p
the nullspace is exactly
\mathrm{span}(\mathbf{1})
and
H
is positive-definite on
\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}
.

Proof.

Direct expansion:
\mathbb{E}_{y}[(p-e_{y})(p-e_{y})^{\top}]=pp^{\top}-p\mathbb{E}[e_{y}]^{\top}-\mathbb{E}[e_{y}]p^{\top}+\mathbb{E}[e_{y}e_{y}^{\top}]
. With
y\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot|x)
,
\mathbb{E}[e_{y}]=p
and
\mathbb{E}[e_{y}e_{y}^{\top}]=\mathrm{diag}(p)
. Thus
H=\mathrm{diag}(p)-pp^{\top}
, the standard softmax Hessian. The quadratic form
v^{\top}Hv=\sum_{y}p_{y}v_{y}^{2}-(\sum_{y}p_{y}v_{y})^{2}=\mathrm{Var}_{p}(v)
is the variance of the random variable
y\mapsto v_{y}
under
p
; it is non-negative by Jensen, zero iff
v
is
p
-a.s. constant. Combined with
1\in\ker H
(by shift-invariance of softmax), the nullspace characterization follows.
∎

Assumption 34

(Non-degenerate data-averaged output probabilities)
.

There exist constants
c_{0}>0
and a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
t=0
such that for all
t\in\mathcal{U}
,

\lambda_{\min}\Bigl(\mathbb{E}_{x\sim q}\bigl[\Pi_{\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}}\,H(x;\theta(t))\,\Pi_{\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}}\bigr]\Bigr)\;\geq\;c_{0},

where
\Pi_{\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}}=I-\tfrac{1}{C}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
. Equivalently, the model’s data-averaged class distribution at
\theta(t)
assigns at least
c_{0}/C
mass to the least-likely class (in the restricted-to-
\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}
sense).

Assumption
34
is satisfied whenever the singular configuration
\theta^{*}
itself is not a perfect classifier of
q
(i.e.,
p_{\theta}^{*}(\cdot|x)
is not a point-mass concentrated on a single class for
q
-almost-all
x
). In particular, it holds if
q
is supported on multiple classes and the model at
\theta^{*}
assigns each class a positive probability somewhere. This is the natural CE analog of “
\sigma^{2}>0
” in the MSE setup: the theorem is about approach to the singular stratum, not about the degenerate configuration in which all classes collapse to one.

Lemma 35

(Backward dead-component magnitude under cross-entropy)
.

Under Assumption
34
and the same symmetric approach, for the expected Fisher:

\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\delta^{(L,h)})^{2}\bigr]\;=\;\Theta(1),\qquad\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\delta^{(\ell,h)})^{2}\bigr]\;=\;\Theta\!\bigl(t^{2(L-\ell)}\bigr)\text{ for }\ell<L,

where
\delta^{(\ell,h)}
is the dead-direction backward delta at layer
\ell
.

Proof.

At
\ell=L
:
\delta^{(L)}(x,y)\in\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}
since
\mathbf{1}^{\top}\delta^{(L)}=(\mathbf{1}^{\top}p)-(\mathbf{1}^{\top}e_{y})=1-1=0
. Write
e_{h}=\Pi_{\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}}e_{h}+(1/C)\mathbf{1}
. Because
H\mathbf{1}=0
(Lemma
33
) implies
\mathbb{E}_{x}[H]\cdot\mathbf{1}=0
, the two cross-terms and the
(1/C)^{2}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{x}[H]\mathbf{1}
term all vanish, leaving the identity

\mathbb{E}_{x}\bigl[(\delta^{(L,h)})^{2}\bigr]\;=\;e_{h}^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{x}[H(x;\theta(t))]\,e_{h}\;=\;(\Pi_{\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}}e_{h})^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{x}[H]\,(\Pi_{\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}}e_{h}).

Applying Assumption
34
to the vector
v=\Pi_{\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}}e_{h}\in\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}
gives the lower bound

\mathbb{E}_{x}\bigl[(\delta^{(L,h)})^{2}\bigr]\geq c_{0}\|\Pi_{\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}}e_{h}\|^{2}=c_{0}(1-1/C)
,
uniformly for
t\in\mathcal{U}
. Thus
\mathbb{E}[(\delta^{(L,h)})^{2}]=\Theta(1)
on
\mathcal{U}
.

For
\ell<L
: the backward recursion
\delta^{(\ell,h)}=\phi^{\prime}(a_{\ell}^{(h)})\cdot t\cdot\delta^{(\ell+1,h)}
(Lemma
16
’s derivation depended only on the canonical structure of
W_{\ell+1}
and the chain rule, both loss-independent) combined with the base case above gives

\mathbb{E}[(\delta^{(\ell,h)})^{2}]=\Theta(1)\cdot t^{2}\cdot\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell-1)})=\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
by induction, exactly as in the MSE proof.
∎

Theorem 36

(Multi-Layer K-FAC G-factor Bridge, cross-entropy)
.

Under the expected-Fisher setup, Assumption
34
, and activation classes (P1)–(P3), let
u_{\ell}
denote the canonical dead-direction unit vector at layer
\ell
:

(a)

(Dead-direction entry, primary statement.)
For
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L-1\}
,

u_{\ell}^{\top}G_{\ell}(\theta(t))\,u_{\ell}\;=\;C_{\ell}^{\mathrm{CE}}\cdot t^{2(L-\ell)}\cdot(1+r_{\ell}^{G}(t)),

with
r_{\ell}^{G}(t)=O(t)
and activation-class-dependent constants matching Theorem
21
.

(b)

(Output layer.)

u_{L}^{\top}G_{L}(\theta(t))\,u_{L}=\Theta(1)
, with constant determined by
c_{0}
from Assumption
34
(replacing
\sigma^{-2}
in the MSE statement).

(c)

(Smallest-eigenvalue corollary.)
Under the additional hypothesis that the non-dead block of
G_{\ell}
is uniformly
\Theta(1)
in
t
(the square-hidden non-dead control adapted to CE; see proof),
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell}(\theta(t)))
equals the dead-direction entry up to multiplicative
\Theta(1)
, so the rate prediction transfers from
u_{\ell}^{\top}G_{\ell}u_{\ell}
to
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})
in this regime.

(d)

(Output-rate match.)
The shallowest-layer rate
2(L-1)
matches Theorem
2
at KL order
k=L
, unchanged from MSE.

Proof.

The proof of Theorem
21
uses Lemmas
15
,
16
,
17
, plus a non-dead control argument in canonical coordinates. Lemma
15
(forward dead-component propagation) and Lemma
17
(canonical-basis block structure with Schur reduction) are architecture-only statements, independent of the loss, and transfer verbatim. For the non-dead statement: for
j\neq h
, the dead-direction canonical structure leaves
(W_{\ell}^{*})_{jj}=1
, so non-dead-direction forward activations
a_{\ell}^{(j)}
are
\Theta(1)
independent of
t
, giving
(A_{\ell})_{jj}=\Theta(1)
; the backward non-dead
\delta^{(\ell,j)}
is driven at
\ell=L
by
\mathbb{E}_{y}[(\delta^{(L,j)})^{2}]=e_{j}^{\top}H(x;\theta)e_{j}
, which by Lemma
33
and Assumption
34
is bounded below by
c_{0}(1-1/C)>0
, hence
\Theta(1)
, and propagates backward through
\delta^{(\ell,j)}=\phi^{\prime}(a_{\ell}^{(j)})\delta^{(\ell+1,j)}
(no
t
-factor on the non-dead diagonal of
W_{\ell+1}
) to yield
(G_{\ell})_{jj}=\Theta(1)
uniformly in
t
. Lemma
16
’s base case changes and is replaced by Lemma
35
, which gives the same asymptotic form
\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
for
\ell<L
and
\Theta(1)
for
\ell=L
. Plugging these into the Schur-reduced eigenvalue argument of Theorem
21
(Lemma
17
: dead row/column
\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
, off-row entries
O(t^{L-\ell})
, non-dead diagonal
\Theta(1)
, so the dead-direction Schur complement is
\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
up to a fixed-fraction absorption from rank-1 outer products) yields claims (a) and (b). Claim (c) then follows when the non-dead block satisfies the square-hidden control above; under rectangular widths or attention gauges the dead-direction-entry statement of (a) remains the operational observable while
\lambda_{\min}
may pick up gauge zeros.

Remark on the activation classes.
Classes (P1), (P2), (P3) refer to hidden-layer nonlinearities only; the softmax at the output head is part of the loss construction, not a hidden activation. All three proofs for smooth Taylor corrections (
r_{\ell}^{G}
) in Theorem
21
carry through unchanged because they concern the
\phi^{\prime}(a_{\ell}^{(h)})
factors in the backward chain, which are loss-independent.
∎

Remark 37

(Static-Fisher rate vs. Adam+CE trajectory observable)
.

Theorem
36
is a statement about the
static
expected-Fisher spectrum at the parametric trajectory
\theta(t)
. CE introduces the logit-shift gauge
G=\mathbb{R}
as the loss’s continuous symmetry; under Adam, the empirical observation of gauge-mode drift on this gauge (Remark
80
) is what makes rate-fitting on an Adam+CE trajectory return drift rather than the theorem’s exponent. The robust static-checkpoint observable on Adam+CE is
\sigma_{\min}(X_{\ell})
on the residual stream (Corollary
58
). To recover the trajectory exponent under CE one applies a gauge fix at training time (the auxiliary
z
-loss penalty
(
Shazeer et al. 2018
;
Zoph et al. 2022
)
is the standard construction) combined with an optimizer whose residual non-equivariance under remaining symmetries is bounded; Remark
80
discusses both routes.

Remark 38

(Joint scope with the rectangular extension)
.

The CE replacement in Lemma
33
touches only the
\ell=L
output-head base case; the rectangular extension touches only the non-dead-block control through the Schur reduction (off-diagonal
A_{\ell}
entries under
h_{\ell}\neq h_{\ell-1}
) at the hidden layers. The two extensions therefore commute, and the joint statement holds: for any
L
-layer feedforward chain with rectangular widths
h_{1},\ldots,h_{L-1}
and output width
h_{L}=C
, expected Fisher under cross-entropy, and a canonical-aligned dead direction at each layer, the dead-direction-entry rate
u_{\ell}^{\top}G_{\ell}u_{\ell}=\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
holds for
\ell<L
and
u_{L}^{\top}G_{L}u_{L}=\Theta(1)
. The rectangular CE entries in Table
4
’s
6\to 8\to 4
network are a strict instance of this joint statement.

Remark on the observable in practice.
Theorem
36
(a) is stated on the dead-direction entry
u_{\ell}^{\top}G_{\ell}u_{\ell}
rather than
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})
for the same reason as the rectangular extension: the entry is the load-bearing scalar in the proof, while raw
\lambda_{\min}
requires the additional non-dead-block control of (c). When approaching the theorem’s assumption boundaries (rectangular widths, or transformers where cross-weight gauges contribute extra null directions), the dead-direction entry remains well-defined and carries the predicted rate, while raw
\lambda_{\min}
may pick up gauge zeros or plateau eigenvalues. An operational spectral-identification rule recovers the dead-direction entry from an unlabeled top-
K
eigenvalue spectrum by rate-matching to the theorem’s prediction.

Corollary 39

(Empirical Fisher collapse under memorization)
.

Let
F_{\mathrm{emp}}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{(x,y_{\mathrm{obs}})\sim\mathcal{D}}\bigl[\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(y_{\mathrm{obs}}\mid x)\,\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(y_{\mathrm{obs}}\mid x)^{\top}\bigr]
denote the empirical Fisher, where
\mathcal{D}
is a fixed data distribution of
(x,y_{\mathrm{obs}})
pairs with marginal
q
on
x
. Suppose the trajectory
t\mapsto\theta(t)
satisfies
p_{\theta(t)}(y_{\mathrm{obs}}\mid x)\to 1
for
\mathcal{D}
-almost-all
(x,y_{\mathrm{obs}})
as
t\to 0
(“perfect memorization of
\mathcal{D}
”). Then
F_{\mathrm{emp}}(\theta(t))\to 0
as
t\to 0
, while
F_{\mathrm{exp}}(\theta(t))
continues to satisfy the rate structure of Theorem
36
.

Proof.

Under perfect memorization, for each
(x,y_{\mathrm{obs}})
in the support of
\mathcal{D}
we have
p_{\theta(t)}(y_{\mathrm{obs}}\mid x)\to 1
, hence
\delta^{(L)}(x,y_{\mathrm{obs}};\theta(t))=\mathrm{softmax}(z(x;\theta(t)))-e_{y_{\mathrm{obs}}}\to 0
. The per-sample gradient
\nabla_{\theta}\log p_{\theta}(y_{\mathrm{obs}}\mid x)=J(x;\theta)^{\top}\delta^{(L)}(x,y_{\mathrm{obs}};\theta)
therefore vanishes, and
F_{\mathrm{emp}}
collapses as the expectation of a vanishing PSD quantity.

F_{\mathrm{exp}}
does not collapse because its integrand averages over
y\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)
, yielding
H(x;\theta)
(Lemma
33
), which is bounded below on
\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}
by Assumption
34
; the rate structure then follows from Theorem
36
.
∎

Remark 40

(Why
F_{\mathrm{emp}}
and
F_{\mathrm{exp}}
do not PSD-dominate each other in general)
.

It is tempting to claim
F_{\mathrm{emp}}\preceq F_{\mathrm{exp}}
via Jensen’s inequality, but this is
incorrect
in general. Both quantities are averages of rank-one matrices
\delta^{(L)}(\delta^{(L)})^{\top}
over different joint distributions of
(x,y)
:
\mathcal{D}
versus
(q\otimes p_{\theta})
. Neither average PSD-dominates the other without additional structural assumptions tying the two distributions together (e.g., if
y_{\mathrm{obs}}(x)\sim p_{\theta}(\cdot\mid x)
, the two are equal in expectation). Corollary
39
’s content is strictly about the trajectory-limit
F_{\mathrm{emp}}\to 0
, not about any PSD ordering.

Corollary
39
is the cross-entropy instance of the estimator scope set out in Remark
4
: on post-memorization training trajectories, the empirical Fisher loses its rate signal to numerical floor even though the rate
structure
is still encoded in eigenvalue ratios. Measurement protocols on trained classifiers should therefore use the expected Fisher rather than the empirical Fisher when rate-fitting is the goal.

Scope.

Theorem
36
preserves the remaining scope limitations of Theorem
21
not addressed here: feedforward chain without residuals (addressed by Theorem
43
), canonical-basis-aligned dead direction (addressed partially in §
A.5
), element-wise hidden activation (the attention case is below). The square-hidden assumption is removed separately by the rectangular extension; the joint
\text{rectangular}\times\text{CE}
statement is given by Remark
38
, and Table
4
’s
6\to 8\to 4
rectangular configuration is a strict instance of that joint statement. The trajectory-rate scope under Adam+CE is governed by Remark
37
and Corollary
78
.

(5 seeds,

is fit against
t
along the canonical construction, with the cross-entropy rows under the expected Fisher (Lemma

). Predicted exponent:
2.000
for

.

9.2  Residual DAGs and
\sigma_{\min}
depth-invariance

Theorem
21
, Theorem
36
, and Theorem
103
assume a feedforward chain with no skip connections. Modern networks (ResNets, transformer FFN blocks, transformer attention blocks) all wrap sub-chains in additive-identity residual connections of the form

h_{\mathrm{out}}=h_{\mathrm{in}}+F(h_{\mathrm{in}}),

where
F
is a short feedforward sub-chain. An identity skip is not a weight matmul, so it does not carry a
t
-factor in the dead direction’s chain-rule expansion. The natural question is how the rate ladder of Theorem
21
adjusts when skips are present, and whether a clean graph-distance reading exists. This subsection answers both: an additive identity skip is a zero-weight shortcut, so the dead-direction rate at any layer is determined by the shortest
weighted
-path distance from that layer to the output, with weight edges costing
1
and skip edges costing
0
. The empirical characterisation that motivated this construction is summarised below; the formal statement follows.

Setup.

A
residual computational graph
has nodes
V=\{v_{0},v_{1},\ldots,v_{L}\}
representing pre-activations (
v_{0}
input,
v_{L}
output) and two kinds of edges, which may
coexist
at the same endpoints:

•

Weight edges

W_{1},\ldots,W_{L}
, each from
v_{i-1}
to
v_{i}
, representing the linear map (optionally followed by elementwise activation
\phi
) of a feedforward layer.

•

Identity-skip edges

\text{skip}\subseteq V\times V
, each
(v_{j},v_{k})
with
k>j
, representing the additive identity
h_{k}\mathrel{{+}{=}}h_{j}
. An additive-identity residual block from
v_{j}
to
v_{k}
is encoded by simultaneously having the weight-edge chain
v_{j}\to v_{j+1}\to\cdots\to v_{k}

and
the skip edge
(v_{j},v_{k})
: the forward computation is
h_{k}=F(h_{j})+h_{j}
where
F
is the weight-chain output.

Hence the computational graph can have multiple forward paths between two nodes when skips are present; the backward-delta receives a sum of contributions from each such path.

The symmetric dead-aligned perturbation perturbs each weight
W_{i}
in the dead direction by
t
:
W_{i}(t)=W_{i}^{*}+tu_{i}u_{i-1}^{\top}
, with
W_{i}^{*}
a rank-deficient singular configuration (per the setup of §
A.1
) and
u_{i}
the dead direction at node
v_{i}
. Skip edges are fixed identities and are not perturbed.

Definition 41

(Shortest-weighted-path distance
K(\ell)
)
.

Consider the graph
G_{\mathrm{bwd}}
obtained from the forward DAG by reversing edges and assigning each weight edge cost 1 and each identity-skip edge cost 0. Let
K(\ell)
be the minimum total cost of any path in
G_{\mathrm{bwd}}
from
v_{L}
to
v_{\ell}
. Equivalently,
K(\ell)
is the smallest number of
weights
the backward dead-direction chain must traverse to reach pre-activation
\ell
when taking every available residual skip.

Lemma 42

(Backward dead-component under residuals)
.

Along the symmetric approach on a residual computational graph, the dead-direction component of the backward delta at layer
\ell
satisfies

\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\delta^{(\ell,u_{\ell})})^{2}\bigr]=\Theta\!\bigl(t^{2K(\ell)}\bigr),

where
K(\ell)
is the shortest-weighted-path distance (Definition
41
). Correction terms enter at higher order:
O(t^{2K(\ell)+\min(\ell,1)})
from smooth activations and
O(t^{2K(\ell)+1})
from longer-path contributions through unused residual skips; all corrections vanish as
t\to 0
.

Proof.

By the chain rule on the forward DAG,
\delta^{(\ell,u_{\ell})}
decomposes as a sum over all directed paths
\pi
from
v_{L}
back to
v_{\ell}
in the reversed graph
G_{\mathrm{bwd}}
:

\delta^{(\ell,u_{\ell})}\;=\;\sum_{\pi:v_{L}\to v_{\ell}}J_{\pi}\,\delta^{(L,u_{L})},

where
J_{\pi}
is the product of edge operators along
\pi
: each weight edge
W_{i}
contributes a factor
(W_{i}^{\top})_{u,u}=t
in the dead-direction-to-dead-direction entry (by the perturbation construction), and each identity-skip edge contributes a factor of
1
. For a path
\pi
with
|\pi|_{W}
weight edges (and any number of skip edges),
J_{\pi}=\Theta(t^{|\pi|_{W}})
. (Under (P1) linear and (P2) smooth activations, the dead-row diagonal contribution above carries through directly. Under (P3) ReLU, off-diagonal entries of
W_{i}^{\top}
at depth
\geq 2
also route non-dead inputs into the dead row at strength
\Theta(1)
; the path-sum
\delta^{(\ell,u_{\ell})}
then includes mixed contributions whose dead-direction component is captured by the same Schur-form reduction used for
G_{\ell}
in Lemma
17
adapted to the residual graph: the dead row of the per-node path-Gram has off-row
O(t^{K(\ell)})
to non-dead coordinates, the non-dead block is
\Theta(1)
, and the dead-direction Schur complement absorbs at most a fixed fraction of the dead diagonal. The leading rate
\Theta(t^{2K(\ell)})
on the dead-direction entry is preserved.)

Let
\mathcal{P}^{*}(\ell)
denote the set of shortest paths (those with
|\pi|_{W}=K(\ell)
). Then

\delta^{(\ell,u_{\ell})}\;=\;\Bigl(\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}^{*}(\ell)}c_{\pi}\Bigr)\cdot t^{K(\ell)}\cdot\delta^{(L,u_{L})}+O(t^{K(\ell)+1}),

where each
c_{\pi}
is a positive constant (product of dead-direction factors along
\pi
, all with the same sign since the perturbation
tuu^{\top}
contributes
+1
per dead-direction factor and identity skips contribute
+1
). The leading-order coefficient
\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}^{*}(\ell)}c_{\pi}
is strictly positive, so no destructive interference can occur. Squaring and taking expectation with
\mathbb{E}[(\delta^{(L,u_{L})})^{2}]=\Theta(1)
(from MSE’s
\sigma^{2}I
or CE’s
H(x;\theta)
restricted to
\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}
) yields

\mathbb{E}[(\delta^{(\ell,u_{\ell})})^{2}]=\Theta(t^{2K(\ell)}).

Smooth-activation corrections enter through the
\phi^{\prime}(a_{\ell}^{(u)})
factors on each weight edge along the shortest path; the
\ell
-th layer’s factor is
\phi^{\prime}(0)+O(t^{\ell})
by Lemma
15
, so these contribute a multiplicative
(1+O(t^{\min(\ell,1)}))
. Longer-path contributions through unused skips contribute at relative order
t^{1}
and above.
∎

Theorem 43

(Multi-Layer K-FAC G-factor Bridge, residual networks)
.

Under the residual-computational-graph setup, Assumption
34
if using cross-entropy, and activation classes (P1)–(P3), the dead-direction entry of the G-factor at layer
\ell
satisfies

(G_{\ell}(\theta(t)))_{u_{\ell}u_{\ell}}=C_{\ell}^{\mathrm{res}}\cdot t^{2K(\ell)}\cdot\bigl(1+r_{\ell}^{G}(t)\bigr),

where
K(\ell)
is the shortest-weighted-path distance (Definition
41
) from
v_{L}
back to
v_{\ell}
, and
r_{\ell}^{G}(t)
collects smooth-activation and finite-
t
residual-skip corrections. All corrections vanish as
t\to 0
.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
42
to get the magnitude of
\delta^{(\ell,u_{\ell})}
, giving dead-row diagonal
(G_{\ell})_{u_{\ell}u_{\ell}}=\Theta(t^{2K(\ell)})
. Non-dead entries of
G_{\ell}
are
\Theta(1)
by the same argument as Theorem
21
’s non-dead analysis (inherited at each node: non-dead directions do not accumulate
t
factors through either weight edges or residual skips). The dead-direction Schur reduction follows the template of Lemma
17
: in canonical coordinates, the dead-row off-diagonals to non-dead coordinates are
O(t^{K(\ell)})
at leading order (one factor of
t
per weight edge in the shortest path; the residual-skip edges contribute factors of
1
but do not introduce additional
t
-factors), and the non-dead block is
\succ 0
at
\Theta(1)
. Writing the dead-direction Schur complement as
d-v^{\top}M^{-1}v
with
d=\Theta(t^{2K(\ell)})
,
v=O(t^{K(\ell)})
,
M=\Theta(1)
, the term
v^{\top}M^{-1}v=O(t^{2K(\ell)})
matches the dead diagonal in order, but the rank-1 outer-product structure (from the canonical-coordinate independence of dead and non-dead activations) absorbs at most a fixed fraction (Lemma
17
; see Remark
19
on the qualitative status of
c<1
). The dead-direction Schur complement is thus
\Theta(t^{2K(\ell)})\cdot(1-c)
with
c<1
, yielding the stated entry rate.
∎

Corollary 44

(
\lambda_{\min}
statement on residual DAGs under complement genericity)
.

Under Theorem
43
together with a non-dead-block-control hypothesis (the residual-DAG analog of the rectangular extension’s complement assumption) requiring the non-dead block of
G_{\ell}
to be uniformly
\Theta(1)
in
t
at every node along the residual DAG, the
\lambda_{\min}
statement holds:

\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell}(\theta(t)))\;=\;(G_{\ell}(\theta(t)))_{u_{\ell}u_{\ell}}\;=\;\Theta(t^{2K(\ell)}).

Without the complement hypothesis, the dead-direction entry of (a) still scales as
\Theta(t^{2K(\ell)})
but
\lambda_{\min}
may additionally pick up complement-null directions (e.g., gauge zeros, or block boundaries where the path graph permits multiple shortest paths with linearly-dependent products) that are identically zero rather than decaying.

Remark 45

(Trained-network non-cancellation)
.

Lemma
42
establishes non-cancellation under the canonical symmetric perturbation
W_{i}(t)=W_{i}^{*}+tu_{i}u_{i-1}^{\top}
, where each weight edge contributes the same sign in the dead-direction sub-block by construction. On a trained network whose dead direction
u
is identified post-hoc as the bottom singular direction of the activation/Fisher spectrum (operational selection rule, §
5.1
), the canonical-trajectory bridge of Corollary
58
applies: the dead direction is the trained Fisher null direction, the path-product expansion is computed in the trained coordinates, and non-cancellation follows from the Schur-form positivity of the trained-network’s non-dead block (an empirical non-dead-block-control hypothesis adapted to the trained spectrum). Residual-stream
\sigma_{\min}
depth-invariance observed on pretrained transformers is consistent with this hypothesis: there is no leading-order cancellation at the residual stream.

Corollaries and concrete cases.

Corollary 46

(Pure feedforward)
.

With no residual edges,
K(\ell)=L-\ell
, recovering Theorem
21
.

Corollary 47

(Per-layer residuals at every layer)
.

If every layer is wrapped in a per-layer residual,
K(\ell)=0
for all
\ell
(the shortest path takes every identity skip). The leading-order rate collapses to
\Theta(1)
; the rate-carrying signal degenerates to a finite-
t
correction.

Corollary 48

(Single block residual over a
k
-layer sub-chain)
.

Consider a feedforward chain of
L
weight layers (weight
W_{i}
maps
v_{i-1}\to v_{i}
) with exactly one additive-identity residual block spanning weights
W_{p},W_{p+1},\ldots,W_{p+k-1}
, that is, a skip edge from
v_{p-1}
(block input) to
v_{p+k-1}
(block output) coexists with the weight chain
v_{p-1}\to v_{p}\to\cdots\to v_{p+k-1}
. Let
K_{\mathrm{post}}:=L-(p+k-1)
be the post-block feedforward distance. Then the shortest-weighted-path distance is

K(\ell)=\begin{cases}L-\ell,&\text{if }\ell\geq p+k-1\text{ (at or after block exit)},\\
K_{\mathrm{post}}+(p+k-1-\ell),&\text{if }p\leq\ell<p+k-1\text{ (inside block)},\\
K_{\mathrm{post}}+(p-1-\ell),&\text{if }\ell\leq p-1\text{ (at or before block input)}.\end{cases}

Intuition: the skip lets any backward path that reaches
v_{p-1}
bypass the entire
k
-weight block via the identity edge (cost 0 instead of
k
), saving exactly
k
weights. A path with target
\ell
strictly inside the block (
p\leq\ell\leq p+k-2
) cannot use the skip (the skip’s entrance is at
v_{p-1}
, below
\ell
’s position), so it must traverse the weights from
v_{p+k-1}
back through
W_{p+k-1},\ldots,W_{\ell+1}
into the block, paying
(p+k-1)-\ell
weights plus the post-block
K_{\mathrm{post}}
. Verified empirically: for
L=4
,
p=3
,
k=2
, the formula gives
K(1)=1,K(2)=0,K(3)=1,K(4)=0
with predicted
\alpha=(2,0,2,0)
; a parametric freeze-probe on a two-layer residual block at the chain end reports
\alpha=(2.20,0.20,2.02,0.02)
, matching up to the expected
\sim 0.2
residual-skip correction.

Scope.

Theorem
43
covers any additive-identity residual DAG. It does
not
cover: pre-activation residuals with learnable projection (where the skip is not pure identity), LayerNorm, attention’s softmax nonlinearity inside
F
, or
gated
residuals (highway networks, GRU- and LSTM-style gates, Mamba-style selective skips). For the first, the projection matrix on the skip path is itself a perturbed weight, so the skip edge has cost 1 rather than 0 in the graph distance;
K(\ell)
remains integer-valued, but the rate formula
2K(\ell)
now counts the projection weights too. The formalism extends directly with this reassignment of edge costs. For gated residuals of the form
X_{\mathrm{out}}=(1-g)\odot X_{\mathrm{in}}+g\odot F(X_{\mathrm{in}})
where the gate
g
is itself learned, the skip edge cost in
K(\ell)
depends on whether
g
stays
\Theta(1)
or admits a dead-direction perturbation; a separate edge-cost analysis tracking gate factors would be required, and we do not provide it here. For LayerNorm and attention’s softmax, see the corresponding extensions below.

Activation-side dual:
\sigma_{\min}
under residuals.

The A-factor /
\sigma_{\min}
dual of Theorem
43
is obtained by mirroring the shortest-weighted-path construction on the
forward
graph.

Definition 49

(Forward shortest-weighted-path distance
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)
)
.

Let
G_{\mathrm{fwd}}
be the residual computational graph with each weight edge cost
1
and each identity-skip edge cost
0
. Define
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)
as the minimum total cost of any directed forward path in
G_{\mathrm{fwd}}
from
v_{0}
to
v_{\ell}
: the smallest number of
weights
that must be traversed from the input to reach node
v_{\ell}
when every available residual skip is taken.

Lemma 50

(Forward dead-component under residuals)
.

Along the symmetric transversal approach of §
9.2
on a residual computational graph, with input
x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{h})
so that
X_{0}^{(h)}=\Theta(1)
a.s., the dead-direction component of the post-activation at node
v_{\ell}
satisfies

X_{\ell}^{(h)}(\theta(t))\;=\;c_{\ell}\cdot t^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)}\cdot X_{0}^{(h)}+O\bigl(t^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)+1}\bigr),

where
c_{\ell}>0
is the sum of positive dead-direction path constants over the shortest forward paths
\pi\in\mathcal{P}^{*}_{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)=\{\pi:v_{0}\to v_{\ell},|\pi|_{W}=K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)\}
. In particular,
\mathbb{E}[(X_{\ell}^{(h)})^{2}]=\Theta(t^{2K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)})
.

Proof.

Mirror of Lemma
42
on the forward graph. By the forward chain rule,
X_{\ell}^{(h)}
decomposes as a sum over all directed paths
\pi
from
v_{0}
to
v_{\ell}
, with each path contributing
J_{\pi}\cdot X_{0}^{(h)}
where
J_{\pi}=\Theta(t^{|\pi|_{W}})
: each weight edge contributes a dead-direction factor of
t
(from the perturbation
W_{i}(t)_{h,h}=t
and the canonical
(W_{i}^{*})_{h,h}=0
), and each identity skip contributes
1
. All factors are non-negative, so no destructive interference occurs: the sum over shortest paths
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)
has strictly positive coefficient
c_{\ell}
. For (P1) linear, this is exact. For (P2) smooth, each traversed weight edge additionally applies
\phi
at a pre-activation of magnitude
\Theta(t)
or smaller; Taylor expanding
\phi(u)=\phi^{\prime}(0)u+O(u^{2})
gives a multiplicative
(\phi^{\prime}(0))^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)}(1+O(t))
factor, preserving the leading rate whenever
\phi^{\prime}(0)\neq 0
. For (P3) ReLU, the gate at each weight edge aligns with
\mathrm{sign}(X_{0}^{(h)})
under canonical coordinates: on the event
\{X_{0}^{(h)}>0\}
(probability
1/2
under Gaussian input) the chain activates with leading coefficient
1
, and on
\{X_{0}^{(h)}<0\}
the chain zeroes at the first weight edge (contributing
0
to
X_{\ell}^{(h)}
, but the skip-only path, if any, still delivers
X_{0}^{(h)}
). Taking the second moment yields
\mathbb{E}[(X_{\ell}^{(h)})^{2}]=\Theta(t^{2K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)})
in all three activation classes.
∎

Corollary 51

(Activation
\sigma_{\min}
on residual networks)
.

Under the residual computational graph setup and activation classes (P1)–(P3), at any
residual-stream
node
v_{\ell}
(i.e., a node reached by an identity-skip path from
v_{0}
and not post-composed with a normalization layer that would zero the row-mean), for
X_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times h}
the matrix of post-activation outputs at
v_{\ell}
over
N
samples,

\sigma_{\min}\bigl(X_{\ell}(\theta(t))\bigr)\;=\;\sqrt{N}\cdot\Theta\bigl(t^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)}\bigr),

where
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)
is the forward shortest-weighted-path distance (Definition
49
) from
v_{0}
to
v_{\ell}
. With no skip edges,
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)=\ell
, recovering Corollary
26
. At post-LN nodes,
\sigma_{\min}(\mathrm{LN}(X_{\ell}))=0
identically (LN’s mean-subtraction null direction
\mathbf{1}_{d}/\sqrt{d}
, equivalently
\gamma^{-1}/\|\gamma^{-1}\|
for trained
\gamma
per Proposition
63
), so the corollary’s prediction does not apply at those sites; see the post-LN-vs-block-output discussion below.

Proof.

By Lemma
50
, the dead channel of
X_{\ell}
has squared magnitude
\Theta(t^{2K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)})
per sample. Non-dead channels of
X_{\ell}
have magnitude
\Theta(1)
, independent of
t
at leading order, by the same argument as the non-dead analysis in Theorem
43
. Canonical-coordinate independence of
x^{(h)}
from non-dead inputs yields off-diagonal vanishing of
X_{\ell}^{\top}X_{\ell}/N
at leading order (residual extension of Lemma
17
). Hence
X_{\ell}^{\top}X_{\ell}/N
is block-diagonal in canonical coordinates with dead entry
\Theta(t^{2K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)})
and non-dead entries
\Theta(1)
; its smallest eigenvalue is the dead entry, giving
\sigma_{\min}(X_{\ell})/\sqrt{N}=\Theta(t^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)})
.
∎

Corollary 52

(Per-layer residuals:
\sigma_{\min}
preserved)
.

If every layer is wrapped in an identity-skip residual, then
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)=0
for all
\ell\geq 0
(the skip chain from
v_{0}
reaches
v_{\ell}
via identities only). Hence
\sigma_{\min}(X_{\ell})=\Theta(\sqrt{N})
, constant in
t
at leading order: the residual stream preserves the input
\sigma_{\min}
signal through the entire depth.

Remark on forward/backward duality under residuals.

On pure feedforward chains,
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell-1)=\ell-1
and
K(\ell)=L-\ell
, so
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell-1)+K(\ell)=L-1
, reproducing Corollary
25
. Under residuals this generalizes to

\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})\cdot\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})\;=\;\Theta\bigl(t^{2(K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell-1)+K(\ell))}\bigr),

which is
layer-dependent
when skips are present: a layer whose input or output is bypassed by a skip sees a reduced exponent. In particular, with per-layer residuals everywhere, both
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}\equiv 0
and
K\equiv 0
, and the A-G product becomes
\Theta(1)
: no rate signal remains in either factor.

Remark on biases.

Corollary
58
is stated for the unbiased setup inherited from §
9.2
. Under the bias augmentation of Theorem
112
, each bias
b_{\ell}
contributes an additive
\Theta(t)
to the dead-direction pre-activation at
v_{\ell}
(the augmented-weight column perturbation is
t
). For
\ell
with
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)\geq 2
, this bias contribution would dominate the weight-chain contribution at leading order, capping the effective forward dead-direction rate at
1
. Thus the
\sigma_{\min}
corollary is
not
directly bias-stable in the way
G
-factor rate is. In practice this matters only when
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)\geq 2
; under per-block residuals (the pre-norm transformer case),
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}\equiv 0
at block outputs and biases do not disturb the rate-
0
conclusion.

Remark on LayerNorm inside residual sub-paths.

Pre-norm transformer blocks take the form
X_{\mathrm{out}}=X_{\mathrm{in}}+F(\mathrm{LN}(X_{\mathrm{in}}))
where
F=W_{2}\cdot\phi(W_{1}\cdot\,\cdot\,)
is a two-weight feedforward. LN applied to
X_{\mathrm{in}}
mean-centers and variance-normalizes row-wise; the dead channel of
\mathrm{LN}(X_{\mathrm{in}})
is
(X_{\mathrm{in}}^{(h)}-\mu(X_{\mathrm{in}}))/\sigma(X_{\mathrm{in}})
, and because non-dead channels of
X_{\mathrm{in}}
are
\Theta(1)
while the dead channel is
\Theta(t^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(v_{\mathrm{in}})})
, the mean
\mu(X_{\mathrm{in}})=\Theta(1)
dominates, giving
\mathrm{LN}(X_{\mathrm{in}})^{(h)}=\Theta(1)
regardless of the upstream rate. Propagating through
F
yields
F(\mathrm{LN}(X_{\mathrm{in}}))^{(h)}=\Theta(t^{k_{F}})
where
k_{F}
is the number of weight layers in
F
(rather than
\Theta(t^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(v_{\mathrm{in}})+k_{F}})
as would hold without LN). At the block output,

X_{\mathrm{out}}^{(h)}\;=\;\underbrace{X_{\mathrm{in}}^{(h)}}_{\Theta(t^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(v_{\mathrm{in}})})}\;+\;\underbrace{F(\mathrm{LN}(X_{\mathrm{in}}))^{(h)}}_{\Theta(t^{k_{F}})},

so the effective rate at
v_{\mathrm{out}}
is
\min(K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(v_{\mathrm{in}}),k_{F})
: graph-theoretic
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}
on the residual DAG if the skip wins, or
k_{F}
if the LN-reset sub-path wins. For the standard pre-norm stack with per-block residuals,
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(v_{\mathrm{in}})=0
at every block boundary (the skip chain from
v_{0}
reaches every block input), so the skip dominates and Corollary
58
’s prediction
\sigma_{\min}(X_{\mathrm{block\_out}})=\Theta(\sqrt{N})
at rate
0
holds exactly, independent of
k_{F}
. (This analysis assumes the LN affine parameters at default
\gamma=1,\beta=0
. Under trained
\gamma\neq 1
, the dead direction of LN’s forward map is
\gamma^{-1}/\|\gamma^{-1}\|
rather than
\mathbf{1}_{d}/\sqrt{d}
per Proposition
63
; the rate-
0
skip-bypass conclusion at the residual stream is unaffected, but the dead direction itself rotates with
\gamma
.)

Two distinct measurement sites must be distinguished:

•

Block output (post-residual-add, pre-next-LN):

\sigma_{\min}(X_{\mathrm{block\_out}})
tracks
\min(K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(v_{\mathrm{in}}),k_{F})
. With per-block residuals this reduces to
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}
on the residual DAG, consistently rate
0
. This matches the empirical signature where 2-, 3-, and 4-block LN stacks show
\sigma_{\min}
log–log slope
\approx 0
at every block output while
u^{\top}Gu
gives fractional non-integer rates in the bound-gap of Theorem
60
.

•

Post-LN (inside block, at LN output):
LN outputs are mean-centered row-wise, so
\mathrm{LN}(X)\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times h}
has the uniform direction
\mathbf{1}/\sqrt{h}
in its left-kernel:
\sigma_{\min}(\mathrm{LN}(X))=0
identically, an architectural null-space degeneracy independent of
t
. Post-LN
\sigma_{\min}
is therefore
not
a valid rate observable without first projecting out the uniform direction and measuring
\sigma_{\min}
on the
(h-1)
-dimensional range.

Consequently, the activation-side rate observable on residual+LN architectures is well-defined only at the residual-stream content (block-output sites, before the next LN application).

Scope: post-norm LN and attention.

The remark above assumes pre-norm LN (LN inside each residual block’s sub-path
F
). In
post-norm
architectures
X_{\mathrm{out}}=\mathrm{LN}(X_{\mathrm{in}}+F(X_{\mathrm{in}}))
, LN acts on the residual-add output, so
X_{\mathrm{out}}
is mean-centered row-wise and
\sigma_{\min}(X_{\mathrm{out}})=0
identically. Post-norm stacks therefore admit no rate-bearing
\sigma_{\min}
observable at block outputs without explicit projection onto
\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}
. Softmax-based attention inside
F
does not affect Corollary
58
at block outputs (the skip bypasses
F
entirely), but the in-block dead-direction rate through softmax remains open.

Composition additivity on heterogeneous blocks.

Theorem
43
establishes the
K
-distance framework when the graph’s elementary operations are weight matmuls (cost
1
) and identity skips (cost
0
). Real architectures compose higher-level
blocks
(attention, MLP FFN, pre-norm residual sub-chains), each with its own internal bridge analysis. The composition rule for these is Theorem
30
(§
29
): for a sequential composition
M=B_{n}\circ\cdots\circ B_{1}
with per-block backward and forward rates
k_{i}^{\mathrm{bk}},k_{i}^{\mathrm{fwd}}
(Definition
29
) on a shared canonical dead direction, and under the scalar-transfer hypothesis, the backward rate at the input of
B_{i}
is
\sum_{j\geq i}k_{j}^{\mathrm{bk}}
and the forward rate at its output is
\sum_{j\leq i}k_{j}^{\mathrm{fwd}}
.

The block rates instantiate directly: a feedforward chain of
k
weights has
k_{B}^{\mathrm{bk}}=k_{B}^{\mathrm{fwd}}=k
; a pre-norm residual block (§
9.2
) with an identity skip has
k_{B}^{\mathrm{bk}}=k_{B}^{\mathrm{fwd}}=0
(skip-dominated); a single weight matmul has
k=1
; and an attention block depends on the attention extension (§
9.4
), with a parametric freeze-probe giving
k_{\mathrm{attn}}^{\mathrm{bk}}=2
in the single-head no-residual setup.

Corollary 53

(Reduction to the basic and residual bridges)
.

Setting each
B_{i}
to a single weight matmul (
k_{i}^{\mathrm{bk}}=k_{i}^{\mathrm{fwd}}=1
) recovers Theorem
21
with
K(\ell)=L-\ell
. Setting each
B_{i}
to a pre-norm residual block (
k_{i}=0
) gives rate
0
everywhere, matching Corollary
52
. Setting each
B_{i}
to a feedforward sub-chain of
k_{i}
weights gives
K(\ell)=\sum_{j\geq i}k_{j}
, equivalent to Theorem
43
’s graph-distance
K(\ell)
on the flattened weight graph.

Proposition 54

(
W_{V}
–
W_{O}
rate-difference invariant)
.

For a probe attention block (standalone or embedded in any composition, with or without residuals, with or without LN) under canonical init with shared dead direction
u=e_{d}
and symmetric perturbation
W_{V},W_{O}\to W^{*}+t\cdot e_{d}e_{d}^{\top}
, the freeze-probe rates on
W_{V}
and
W_{O}
’s grad-output dead-channel Fisher satisfy

\alpha_{W_{V}}-\alpha_{W_{O}}\;=\;2

exactly, regardless of composition depth, surrounding blocks, or presence of LN/residuals.

Proof.

Let
\Delta:=\partial L/\partial Y_{\mathrm{block}}
denote the dead-direction gradient flowing into the probe block’s output. The grad-output of
W_{O}
(whose layer output is
Y_{\mathrm{block}}
) is
\Delta
itself. The grad-output of
W_{V}
(whose layer output is
V=W_{V}X_{\mathrm{blk\_in}}
) follows the chain
W_{V}\to V\to AV\to Y_{\mathrm{block}}
:

\partial L/\partial V\;=\;A^{\top}\cdot(\partial L/\partial(AV))\;=\;A^{\top}\cdot\Delta\cdot W_{O},

using
Y_{\mathrm{block}}=(AV)W_{O}^{\top}
. Project both gradients onto
u=e_{d}
:

(\Delta\cdot u)_{n,:}\;=\;\Delta_{n,d}\,e_{d},\qquad(A^{\top}\Delta W_{O})\cdot e_{d}\;=\;A^{\top}\cdot\Delta\cdot(W_{O}^{\top}e_{d}).

At canonical init with
W_{O}^{*}+t\cdot e_{d}e_{d}^{\top}
, the
d
-th row of
W_{O}
is
t\cdot e_{d}^{\top}
, so
W_{O}^{\top}e_{d}=t\cdot e_{d}
. Hence

(\partial L/\partial V)\cdot e_{d}\;=\;t\cdot A^{\top}\cdot\Delta\cdot e_{d}\;=\;t\cdot A^{\top}\cdot(\Delta e_{d}).

Taking squared mean over
(n,m)
tokens (the Fisher factor):

u^{\top}G_{W_{V}}u\;=\;\mathbb{E}\bigl[(A^{\top}\Delta e_{d})^{2}\bigr]\cdot t^{2}\;=\;t^{2}\cdot(u^{\top}G_{W_{O}}u)\cdot c,

with
c=\mathbb{E}[(A^{\top}\tilde{\Delta})^{2}]/\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\Delta}^{2}]
a positive
O(1)
constant from the softmax matrix’s row-stochastic structure (
\tilde{\Delta}:=\Delta e_{d}
is the dead column of the upstream gradient). Taking logarithm and fitting rate in
t
:
\alpha_{W_{V}}=2+\alpha_{W_{O}}
. The constant
c
contributes to the Fisher prefactor, not the slope.
∎

Remark 55

(The invariant works as a stable probe of attention internal structure)
.

Proposition
54
is stable across all tested configurations:
n\in\{1,4,6\}
attention chains, with or without LN,
d\in\{16,64,128,768\}
,
n_{h}\in\{1,2,4,8\}
. The observed differences
\alpha_{W_{V}}-\alpha_{W_{O}}
are
2.000\pm 0.000
in every case, even where the individual rates
\alpha_{W_{V}}
and
\alpha_{W_{O}}
exhibit the softmax-coupling anomalies of Remark
32
. The invariant follows from the sequential
W_{V}\to W_{O}
structure within each attention block (one Linear’s worth of
t
-factor in grad-output) and is therefore preserved under any deformation of the rest of the network that doesn’t alter the within-block sequence. This makes it a clean diagnostic observable: measuring
\alpha_{W_{V}}-\alpha_{W_{O}}
and confirming it equals
2
is a lightweight consistency check for the attention block’s canonical structure.

Remark 56

(Residual bypass: the softmax-coupling anomaly is moot in practical transformers)
.

The softmax-coupling anomaly of Remark
32
is a property of non-residual sequential attention stacks, which do not appear in practical architectures. Every attention block in ViT, BERT, GPT, and LLaMA-family transformers is residual-wrapped:
y=x+\mathrm{Attn}(\mathrm{LN}(x))
. Under Corollary
58
, the residual skip dominates:
k_{\mathrm{block}}^{\mathrm{res}}=0
at the block-output level (forward
K
-distance
0
via the identity path), regardless of what happens inside
\mathrm{Attn}
. Consequently, rate predictions at the residual stream of a practical transformer are governed by the skip, not by the attention internals; the softmax-coupling anomaly is bypassed entirely. The anomaly
does
affect the internal component rates (
W_{O},W_{V},W_{Q},W_{K}
) within each block, but those are not the primary predictive observables at practical scale:
\sigma_{\min}
on the residual stream is.

Corollary 57

(Attention as a composition corollary of its standalone rate)
.

The attention block’s standalone backward rate
k_{\mathrm{attn}}^{\mathrm{bk}}=2
is established in §
9.4
(Theorem
67
), so Theorem
30
yields the rate for any composition involving attention blocks, including residual transformer stacks (via Corollary
53
with residual-block rates being
0
). The composition framework reduces the attention extension from “attention inside arbitrary architecture” to “attention block in isolation,” the smaller target that §
9.4
resolves.

Remark on block-rate canonical alignment (NDC).

Theorem
30
assumes each block shares the canonical dead direction
u=e_{h}
. If blocks use different canonical coordinates (e.g., a rotation is applied between blocks), the composition rate can exceed
\sum_{j}k_{j}
(misalignment breaks the factored structure). This is the Non-Dead-Canonical (NDC) assumption: the dead direction is coherent across composed blocks. Proposition
118
(the rotation case) handles the per-block rotation separately; composition with rotated blocks follows by treating each rotated block as a separate rate-carrying unit.

Figure 7
:
Residual-DAG
\sigma_{\min}
depth-invariance (Corollary
58
). The mechanism the corollary identifies. (a) In a residual block
X_{i+1}=X_{i}+f_{i+1}(X_{i})
, the additive identity skip provides a forward-
K
-distance-zero route from
X_{0}
to every node, so the dead-direction component cannot decay below
X_{0}
at leading order:
\sigma_{\min}(X_{\ell})/\sigma_{\min}(X_{0})\geq 1
at every depth. (b) The depth profile contrast: a feedforward chain (no skips) decays as
t^{\ell}
; a residual stream stays at the floor (rate
0
). On a trained transformer the same observable is a per-checkpoint structural-health diagnostic: a measured U-shape or monotone decline is a falsifier of the no-cancellation hypothesis.

Corollary 58

(Bottom-of-active-spectrum depth-invariance on residual DAGs)
.

On a residual computational graph with
exact-identity
skip connections (weight edges of cost
1
, identity-skip edges of cost
0
; gated or highway residuals are outside this scope), under the canonical-aligned approach
\theta(t)
of Theorem
2
, with input residual stream
X_{0}
of rank
r_{0}:=\rank(X_{0})
,

\sigma_{(r_{0})}(X_{\ell}(\theta(t)))\;=\;\sqrt{N}\cdot\Theta\bigl(t^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)}\bigr),

where
\sigma_{(k)}
denotes the
k
-th-largest singular value, so
\sigma_{(r_{0})}
is the smallest
active
(non-numerical-zero) singular direction of
X_{0}
. For full-rank inputs (
r_{0}=d
),
\sigma_{(r_{0})}=\sigma_{\min}
and the statement reduces to
\sigma_{\min}(X_{\ell}(\theta(t)))=\sqrt{N}\cdot\Theta(t^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)})
. With per-layer identity-skip residuals (e.g., post-norm and pre-norm transformer blocks where the skip is exact identity),
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)\equiv 0
for all
\ell
, so the residual stream preserves the input’s bottom-of-active-spectrum signal at rate
0
through the entire depth.

Sketch.

The forward chain rule decomposes
X_{\ell}^{(h)}
as a sum over directed paths from
v_{0}
to
v_{\ell}
, each contributing
t^{|\pi|_{W}}
with non-negative coefficient; the shortest path dominates, giving
X_{\ell}^{(h)}=\Theta(t^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)})
. The argument applies path-by-path to any non-cancelled bottom-of-spectrum direction, hence the rank-aware
\sigma_{(r_{0})}
form. Full proof: §
9.2.1
.
∎

Three readings of the depth-invariance.

The same content takes three useful forms.
As a graph distance.
The dead-direction rate at any node
v_{\ell}
is determined by the shortest weighted path from
v_{0}
to
v_{\ell}
, with weight edges costing
1
and identity skips costing
0
. With per-layer skips everywhere, every node has
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)=0
and the rate is
\Theta(1)
across all depth.
As a structural-health diagnostic.
The ratio
\sigma_{(r_{0})}(X_{\ell})/\sigma_{(r_{0})}(X_{0})
should remain
\Theta(1)
across a residual transformer’s depth at any training checkpoint. A measured dip (pronounced U-shape, monotone decline) is the falsifier: it indicates the no-cancellation hypothesis breaks for that architecture, that the skip’s signal is being subtracted out by the residual branch in the bottom-of-active-spectrum direction.
As a rate-collapse contrast.
A skipless pure-attention chain collapses this observable doubly-exponentially with depth; the residual skip instead preserves it at rate
0
, which is what the corollary makes precise.

Structural status of Corollary
58
.

The mathematical content of Corollary
58
is elementary: with an additive-identity skip
X_{\ell+1}=X_{\ell}+F(X_{\ell})
and a non-cancellation hypothesis on the residual branch, the bottom singular value cannot decrease at leading order. The shortest-weighted-path formalism is correct but follows from basic graph theory once the per-edge factorisation is in place. We state and label the result because it gives a single observable,
\sigma_{(r_{0})}(X_{\ell})/\sigma_{(r_{0})}(X_{0})
, on which the no-cancellation hypothesis is empirically falsifiable on a per-checkpoint basis; the corollary is best read as the analytic packaging of that diagnostic. The genuinely architectural content lies in the prerequisites of the bridge framework (Theorem
21
, the architectural instantiations, the attention composition anomaly), which this corollary applies as a downstream observable.

Position relative to prior rank-collapse and neural-collapse work.

The same activation-side observable that
Dong et al. 2021
;
Noci et al. 2022
establish collapses doubly-exponentially in pure-attention chains is here shown to be
rate-zero preserved
along any residual DAG: the identity skip is the operational mechanism that turns the rank-collapse signal into depth-invariance, recovering the empirical residual-stream stability observed in modern transformers. Neural collapse (NC1)
(
Papyan et al. 2020
)
is the special case of
\sigma_{\min}
on a class-mean matrix at the terminal phase of training; the corollary is the per-layer during-training generalisation along a residual DAG. The rank-aware
\sigma_{(r_{0})}
form keeps the prediction measurable when the input residual stream’s smallest active singular value sits near the numerical floor.

Architectural vs training-conditional content.

The rate-
0
conclusion
\sigma_{(r_{0})}(X_{\ell})/\sigma_{(r_{0})}(X_{0})=\Theta(1)
is a property of the additive-identity skip structure: under canonical alignment, the per-edge dead-direction Jacobians have the path-wise factorisation of the proof, so the identity skip dominates the bottom-of-active-spectrum at any
\theta
for which the no-cancellation hypothesis (Remark
59
) holds. This includes random initialisation; the corollary’s rate-
0
pass/fail is therefore an architectural diagnostic, not a training-signal indicator. The training-conditional content is the
magnitude
of the depth profile (the amplification factor between consecutive blocks), not the rate-
0
conclusion itself. When using the corollary as a structural-health probe at LLM scale, the training signal is read off the magnitude, not the pass/fail of the architectural prediction.

9.2.1
Proof of Corollary
58

Consider the residual computational graph
G_{\mathrm{fwd}}
with weight edges of cost
1
and identity-skip edges of cost
0
, and define
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)
as the minimum total cost of any directed forward path from
v_{0}
to
v_{\ell}
.

Forward dead-direction-component decomposition on the residual DAG.

The dead-direction component
X_{\ell}^{(h)}
decomposes as a sum over all directed paths
\pi:v_{0}\to v_{\ell}
in
G_{\mathrm{fwd}}
, with each path contributing
J_{\pi}\cdot X_{0}^{(h)}
where
J_{\pi}
is the product of the per-edge dead-direction Jacobian factors along
\pi
: each weight edge contributes a factor of
t
(from
W_{i}(t)_{h,h}=t
in canonical coordinates), and each identity skip contributes
1
. Hence
J_{\pi}=c_{\pi}\cdot t^{|\pi|_{W}}
where
c_{\pi}\geq 0
is the dead-direction path constant (positive on the smooth or canonical-aligned activation, zero on inactive ReLU sub-paths) and
|\pi|_{W}
counts weights along
\pi
. The decomposition is a claim about the dead-direction component specifically, not about the full network: in canonical coordinates the per-edge dead-direction Jacobian is scalar (factor
t
for weights,
1
for skips), and along the canonical-aligned trajectory the off-diagonal couplings into the dead direction vanish at leading order, so the per-edge contributions multiply along each path independently of the off-diagonal couplings.

For (P1) linear, all
c_{\pi}>0
, so
X_{\ell}^{(h)}=(\sum_{\pi}c_{\pi}t^{|\pi|_{W}})X_{0}^{(h)}
; the shortest-path contribution dominates:
X_{\ell}^{(h)}=c_{\ell}t^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)}X_{0}^{(h)}+O(t^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)+1})
with
c_{\ell}>0
the sum over shortest paths. For (P2) smooth, each weight edge additionally applies
\phi
at a pre-activation of magnitude
\Theta(t)
or smaller; Taylor expanding
\phi(u)=\phi^{\prime}(0)u+O(u^{2})
gives a multiplicative
(\phi^{\prime}(0))^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)}(1+O(t))
factor whenever
\phi^{\prime}(0)\neq 0
, preserving the leading rate. For (P3) ReLU, the gate at each weight edge aligns with
\mathrm{sign}(X_{0}^{(h)})
by the canonical-aligned construction of Theorem
2
: on the event
\{X_{0}^{(h)}>0\}
the chain activates with leading coefficient
1
; on
\{X_{0}^{(h)}<0\}
the chain zeroes at the first weight edge but the skip-only path (if
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)=0
) still delivers
X_{0}^{(h)}
. Taking the second moment yields
\mathbb{E}[(X_{\ell}^{(h)})^{2}]=\Theta(t^{2K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)})
in all three activation classes.

Activation matrix singular value.

The activation matrix
X_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times h}
has
X_{\ell}^{\top}X_{\ell}/N\to A_{\ell+1}
a.s. by the strong law (the rows of
X_{\ell}
are iid because the rows of
X_{0}
are iid by the Gaussian-isotropic input assumption of Theorem
2
, and the per-row map through the canonical network is deterministic). By the per-coordinate analysis above and the Schur-form integral-reduction Lemma
17
,
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell+1})
is determined by the dead-direction Schur complement: the dead-row entry is
\Theta(t^{2K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)})
, the non-dead block is
\succ 0
at
\Theta(1)
, and the cross-row entries are
O(t^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)})
which gives a Schur cancellation of order
\Theta(t^{2K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)})
leaving
\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell+1})=\Theta(t^{2K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)})
. Hence
\sigma_{\min}(X_{\ell})/\sqrt{N}=\Theta(t^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)})
.

Per-layer residuals.

If every layer is wrapped in an exact-identity skip residual,
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)=0
for all
\ell\geq 0
(the skip chain from
v_{0}
reaches
v_{\ell}
via identities only, which carry zero
t
-cost). The skip-only path delivers
X_{0}^{(h)}
unchanged at
\Theta(1)
; the weight-path contributions are
\Theta(t)
each. The non-negative coefficient property
c_{\pi}\geq 0
established above ensures the weight-path contributions cannot
cancel
the skip’s
\Theta(1)
contribution at leading order: sign coherence on the canonical trajectory rules out destructive interference between the skip and weight branches in the dead direction. Hence
\sigma_{\min}(X_{\ell})=\Theta(\sqrt{N})
, constant in
t
at leading order, and the residual stream preserves the input
\sigma_{\min}
signal through the entire depth.

Rank-aware extension.

The decomposition above singles out a particular dead direction (the bottom-of-spectrum coordinate) but uses no property of that coordinate beyond its non-cancellation by the residual branch. The same argument applies path-by-path to any non-cancelled singular direction: for an input residual stream
X_{0}
of rank
r_{0}:=\rank(X_{0})
, the smallest active direction
\sigma_{(r_{0})}(X_{0})
propagates through the residual DAG with the same per-edge factor structure (
c_{\pi}\geq 0
for weight edges, identity for skips). Hence
\sigma_{(r_{0})}(X_{\ell}(\theta(t)))=\sqrt{N}\cdot\Theta(t^{K^{\mathrm{fwd}}(\ell)})
in the same activation classes (P1)–(P3); the literal-
\sigma_{\min}
conclusion is the
r_{0}=d
specialisation. ∎

Remark 59

(Scope at trained-network scale and the no-cancellation assumption)
.

The proof above uses canonical-aligned coordinates where each weight edge contributes a non-negative dead-direction factor
c_{\pi}\geq 0
, so the identity skip’s contribution
1
cannot be cancelled by the residual branch. On a general trained residual network (not on the canonical trajectory) the residual branch
F_{\ell}
in
X_{\ell+1}=X_{\ell}+F_{\ell}(X_{\ell})
