is not constrained to act with non-negative coefficients on a singular direction, so a sign-flipped residual contribution could in principle cancel the identity at leading order. The trained-network conclusion
\sigma_{(r_{0})}(X_{\ell+1})\geq\sigma_{(r_{0})}(X_{\ell})
at leading order (where
r_{0}=\rank(X_{0})
is the input rank, as in the rank-aware corollary statement) therefore requires a generic non-cancellation assumption: there exists a constant
c>0
such that
\|F_{\ell}(X_{\ell})v\|^{2}\leq(1-c)\|X_{\ell}v\|^{2}
does
not
hold for the bottom-of-active-spectrum singular direction
v
of
X_{\ell}
, equivalently, the residual branch does not partially cancel the identity in that direction with bounded relative magnitude. The link to the canonical-trajectory statement is that under the canonical approach the depth profile
\ell\mapsto\sigma_{(r_{0})}(X_{\ell})
is flat at leading order (the corollary’s "constant in
t
"); on a trained network at fixed parameters the analogous statement is
\sigma_{(r_{0})}(X_{\ell})
non-decreasing with depth.

For full-rank inputs the rank-aware statement reduces to the literal
\sigma_{\min}(X_{\ell})/\sigma_{\min}(X_{0})\geq 1
. The rank-aware form remains the theoretically correct generalisation when
X_{0}
has a true numerical-zero kernel; in practice on trained transformers the boundary between "true kernel" and "precision artefact" depends on the covariance accumulator. With fp
32
accumulation, the noise floor
\sigma_{\max}(X_{0})\cdot\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{fp}32}}
rises above the bottom of the active spectrum on inputs with
\sigma_{\max}\gtrsim 10^{5}
(the regime documented in the massive-activations literature
(
Sun et al. 2024
)
, related to the low-effective-rank class structure of deep-learning spectra
(
Papyan 2020
)
), and the input reads as if numerically rank-deficient. With fp
64
accumulation the noise floor drops by eight orders of magnitude, so an input that read as rank-deficient at fp
32
resolves as full-rank and
\sigma_{(r_{0})}=\sigma_{\min}
identically. The rank-aware form is the general statement; in practice it reduces to
\sigma_{\min}
as a measurement effect of fp
64
precision.

The rank-aware form is the natural form of the prediction once the input rank is allowed to be
r_{0}\leq d
. A rate-level guarantee under structural assumptions on
F_{\ell}
(Lipschitz contraction, angular bounds) is left as future work.

Scope under non-linear residual-branch wrappings (Peri-LN / sandwich-norm).

The proof above factorises the dead-direction component
X_{\ell}^{(h)}
along weighted paths in the residual graph, with each weight edge contributing a non-negative scalar factor and identity skips contributing
1
. The factorisation requires the residual-branch update
X_{\ell+1}=X_{\ell}+F_{\ell}(X_{\ell})
to enter additively. When the weight branch is wrapped in a non-linear normalisation operator before the residual addition (the
sandwich-norm
or
Peri-LN
block pattern
(
Kim et al. 2025
)
), the post-norm RMSNorm renormalises the sublayer output before it enters the residual stream, and the per-edge factorisation no longer applies. The corollary’s strict statement is therefore not extended to Peri-LN architectures by the proof above. A formal extension covering non-linear residual-branch wrappings is left open.

9.3  LayerNorm and the LN-kernel direction

Residual identity skips contributed zero-weight edges to the backward chain, and the rate ladder remained graph-distance-readable. LayerNorm is different:
\text{LN}(h)=(h-\mu)/\sigma
is a genuinely non-linear, non-element-wise per-sample operation, and its backward Jacobian couples every coordinate to every other through the mean and variance. The per-edge factorisation that propagated the rate through residual DAGs no longer holds. Two things become available instead: a
bracket
on the rate (rather than an exact graph-distance formula), and a separate
algebraic
null direction in the post-LN covariance that is readable from the affine parameter alone, no forward pass required. This subsection proves the bracket and characterises empirically where the upper and lower bounds collapse onto a single rate; the algebraic null direction is the subject of Proposition
63
.

With the setup that follows, we take
\gamma=1,\beta=0
at the singular configuration. Unlike residual identity skips (Theorem
43
) which contributed zero-weight edges to the backward chain, LN’s backward Jacobian couples the dead direction to all other coordinates. This subsection states the partial result we can prove cleanly and presents an empirical characterization of where the proof technique comes up short.

Setup.

Let the base setup (square widths, single dead direction
u=e_{h_{*}}
, feedforward chain) be augmented with
LN
operations inserted at a subset
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,L-1\}
of positions (an LN at position
k
sits between the post-activation
h_{k}
and the weight matmul
W_{k+1}
).

Define
K_{\text{LN}}^{\text{upper}}(\ell)=L-\ell
(the no-LN weight count above
\ell
) and
K_{\text{LN}}^{\text{lower}}(\ell)
= the number of weight matmuls above
\ell
that are
not
preceded (going backward from the output) by an LN crossing, i.e. weight matmuls strictly above the highest LN in
S
that is at-or-above
\ell
.

Proposition 60

(LayerNorm rate bracket, partial)
.

Under the LN-augmented setup, activation classes (P1)–(P3), and the symmetric single-direction approach, the dead-direction entry of the G-factor at layer
\ell
satisfies

\Omega\!\bigl(t^{2K_{\text{LN}}^{\text{lower}}(\ell)}\bigr)\;\leq\;(G_{\ell}(\theta(t)))_{u_{\ell}u_{\ell}}\;\leq\;O\!\bigl(t^{2K_{\text{LN}}^{\text{upper}}(\ell)}\bigr).

The upper bound recovers Theorem
21
(treating LN as a worst-case-preserving operation); the lower bound reflects that LN crossings cannot increase the dead-direction rate beyond plateau. The precise asymptotic rate within this range depends on the geometry of
\mu,\sigma
near the dead direction and does not reduce to a graph-distance formula in general.

Tight regimes (where the upper and lower bounds coincide).
(i) When
S\cap\{\ell,\ldots,L\}=\emptyset
(no LN at or above
\ell
),
K_{\mathrm{LN}}^{\mathrm{lower}}(\ell)=K_{\mathrm{LN}}^{\mathrm{upper}}(\ell)=L-\ell
and the dead-direction rate is exactly
2(L-\ell)
, recovering Theorem
21
. (ii) When the topmost LN in
S
at-or-above
\ell
sits at level
\ell+1
(an LN sits immediately above the layer being probed),
K_{\mathrm{LN}}^{\mathrm{lower}}(\ell)=K_{\mathrm{LN}}^{\mathrm{upper}}(\ell)-1
collapses to
L-\ell-1
for the lower-bound part counting only weights above the topmost LN, and the rate is
2(L-\ell-1)
at leading order. The remaining cases (LN strictly above
\ell+1
, or multiple LNs above
\ell
) admit only the bracket; closing the bracket into a closed-form rate requires the finite-
t
refinement of Theorem
122
(§
A.6.1
) for the MLP case, or Conjecture
123
for the attention case.

Proof sketch.

Upper bound
: at every backward chain step, LN’s Jacobian is a bounded operator (Lipschitz in its inputs at the singular configuration), so it cannot amplify the dead-direction component beyond its pre-LN magnitude. The feedforward rate
t^{2(L-\ell)}
is thus an upper bound.

Lower bound via the no-LN prefix
: let
k^{\ast}=\max\{k\in S:k\geq\ell\}
(or
k^{\ast}=L
if no LN at or above
\ell
). The backward chain from
\delta_{L}
down to the nearest LN crossing (if any) traverses only weight matmuls, specifically
L-k^{\ast}
of them. These contribute a clean
t^{L-k^{\ast}}
scaling to the dead-direction entry by Lemma
16
. After crossing LN at
k^{\ast}
, the subsequent weight matmuls (from
k^{\ast}
down to
\ell
, i.e.
k^{\ast}-\ell
of them) continue to contribute their own
t
-scaling on top of whatever plateau LN introduces. The dead-direction magnitude at
\delta_{\ell}
is at least
t^{K_{\text{LN}}^{\text{lower}}(\ell)}
where
K_{\text{LN}}^{\text{lower}}(\ell)=L-k^{\ast}
(the weights strictly above the topmost LN); squaring gives the claimed lower bound.
∎

Empirical characterization.

Direct freeze-probe measurement (the static parametric protocol of Appendix
B.2
, Table
10
) at
L=4
,
h=6
, linear activation, four LN patterns and three activations:

•

none
(control): recovers Theorem
21
rate
2(L-\ell)
exactly.

•

after_first
(LN at
k=1
): measured
\alpha=(0.06,4,2,0)
. For
\ell\geq 2
, no LN is in the backward chain, rate is standard. For
\ell=1
,
K_{\text{LN}}^{\text{lower}}(1)=0
(LN is directly above), rate is destroyed. Lower and upper bounds for
\ell=1
are
0
and
L-1=3
respectively (i.e., the bracket
\alpha_{1}\in[0,6]
); the measured
\alpha_{1}=0.06
saturates the lower bound, confirming tightness in the LN-immediately-above case (regime (ii) of Theorem
60
).

•

after_last_hidden
(LN at
k=3
): measured
\alpha=(4.3,2.3,0.3,0)
. For each
\ell
: lower bound
K_{\text{LN}}^{\text{lower}}(\ell)=4-3=1
(just the one weight
W_{4}
above the topmost LN), upper
=L-\ell
. Measured rates
\sim(4,2,0,0)
asymptotically (the
+0.3
uniform offset is finite-
t
), with
\alpha_{1}=4
exceeding the lower bound of
2
: the post-LN weights
W_{2},W_{3}
contribute in practice.

•

every
(LN at every hidden layer): measured
\alpha=(2.25,1.32,0.37,0)
in the fit window. Asymptotic behavior (from raw dead-coord traces at small
t
) shows
\alpha\to(1,\approx 0,0,0)
as
t\to 0
: the plateau dominates at deeper layers and only a single
W_{2}
between the bottom two LN and layer
1
contributes a non-trivial
t
-scaling. The lower bound
K_{\text{LN}}^{\text{lower}}(1)=0
is loose; the actual rate is
\sim 1
.

What the partial result covers and what it does not.

Theorem
60
gives an exact rate whenever LN sits directly above
\ell
(rate is the same as the all-weights-preserved upper bound, applied above the LN); for LN strictly above
\ell
, the theorem brackets the rate between the two bounds but does not pin it down. The gap corresponds to the contributions of weights between the LN and layer
\ell
: empirically these do contribute
t
-scaling, but the contribution is not a clean integer exponent in general. It depends on the LN’s backward-Jacobian structure, which in turn depends on the value of
(h-\mu)/\sigma
at the singular configuration.

A sharper theorem (recovering a graph-distance-style formula
\alpha_{\ell}=2\cdot K(\ell)
with
K
counting only post-LN weights) would require either (a) a stronger assumption on the non-dead activations at the singular configuration, or (b) a refined Jacobian analysis that tracks the non-diagonal contributions through LN. Both are open problems; the empirical LayerNorm-pattern sweep constrains the form of any such refinement.

Component-wise LN rate shift.

Within the LN rate-shift window for pre-norm transformer blocks the freeze-probe slope on each component
c
takes the empirical form

\alpha_{c}\;=\;2\,K(c)\;+\;q_{\mathrm{LN}}(d,N,t_{\mathrm{train}})\cdot K_{\mathrm{LN}}^{\mathrm{eff}}(c),

where
K(c)
is the standard no-LN backward distance (Theorem
21
),
q_{\mathrm{LN}}
is a per-setup scalar measuring the rate shift at an MLP fc2 reference component, and
K_{\mathrm{LN}}^{\mathrm{eff}}(c)
is a component-specific effective count of LN crossings. The formula describes the
finite-
t
crossover regime
traversed by SGD trajectories; the strict asymptotic
t\to 0
rate matches the no-LN prediction (Remark
62
).

Remark 61

(LN rate-shift formula scope: static-Fisher vs trajectory readout)
.

Theorem
60
and the empirical formula
\alpha_{c}=2K(c)+q_{\mathrm{LN}}\cdot K_{\mathrm{LN}}^{\mathrm{eff}}(c)
are static-Fisher statements at the parametric trajectory. Under Adam, gauge-mode drift on the loss’s continuous symmetries (Remark
80
) confounds direct rate readout from
u^{\top}Gu
inside the LN-affected sub-chain; the robust observable on Adam+CE is
\sigma_{\min}
on the residual stream (Corollary
58
).

Remark 62

(LN’s rate shift is a finite-
t
phenomenon, not an asymptote)
.

A parametric freeze-probe (set the probe block’s weights directly at a grid of
t
-values, measure
u^{\top}G_{c}u
at each, no SGD trajectory) reveals that at true asymptotic
t\to 0
, the rates under pre-norm LN match the no-LN predictions exactly. Across 36
(d,n_{h},\mathrm{seed})
combinations spanning
d\in\{8,16,32,64,128,256,384,768\}
and
n_{h}\in\{1,2,4,8\}
, fit on
t\leq 10^{-2}
, all component slopes are
\alpha_{\mathrm{fc2}}=\alpha_{W_{O}}=0.000\pm 0.000
,
\alpha_{\mathrm{fc1}}=\alpha_{W_{V}}=\alpha_{W_{Q}}=\alpha_{W_{K}}=2.000\pm 0.000
, giving
q_{\mathrm{LN}}=0.000\pm 0.000
asymptotically. The non-zero
q_{\mathrm{LN}}
observed in SGD trajectories arises in the crossover region
t\in[0.1,1]
where LN’s mean-subtraction Jacobian contributes subleading terms that dominate the local slope. SGD trajectories at finite training time typically terminate in this crossover region, so the measured rates are non-trivial but not the leading-order asymptote. The crossover region, not the
t\to 0
asymptote, is where a non-zero
q_{\mathrm{LN}}
is measured. The closed-form derivation that quantifies this crossover-region slope (and explains
q_{\mathrm{LN}}
as an integrated local slope rather than a fundamental constant) is Theorem
122
for the MLP case and Conjecture
123
for the attention case (§
A.6.1
).

Relation to Proposition
63
(LN-kernel direction on activations).

Theorem
60
concerns the dead-direction
rate
of the G-factor under a parametric singular approach. Proposition
63
(§
9.3.1
) concerns the
static
null direction of LN’s Jacobian at any single checkpoint, which is
v^{*}=\gamma^{-1}/\|\gamma^{-1}\|
(independent of
t
). The two are complementary observables: the bound theorem governs how a known dead direction scales along an approach, while the kernel proposition tells one how to read the dead direction off the LN affine parameter without a forward pass. On post-final-LN sites, the two combine: the kernel direction
\gamma^{-1}/\|\gamma^{-1}\|
is the dead direction at that checkpoint, and Theorem
60
bracket-bounds the corresponding G-factor entry’s rate. This combination is what makes the LN
\sigma_{\min}
fingerprint (Corollary
58
) and the kernel-direction test (Proposition
63
) both operationally cheap at LLM scale:
u^{*}
is read from the affine parameter, and the rate is bounded above by the no-LN feedforward count.

9.3.1
Normalisation kernels: LN vs RMSNorm

This subsection states and proves an algebraic prediction: the residual-stream covariance at the
output
of a LayerNorm block is always exactly rank-deficient along a deterministic direction set by LN’s mean-subtraction operator and the per-coordinate learned scale
\gamma
; RMSNorm, lacking the mean-subtraction projector, admits no such deterministic kernel direction. The two clauses below share an affine-decomposition setup:
\mathrm{N}(x)=\gamma\odot\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{N}}(x)+\beta_{\mathrm{N}}
with
\beta_{\mathrm{LN}}=\beta
,
\beta_{\mathrm{RMS}}=0
,
\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{LN}}(x):=\sqrt{d}\cdot Px/\|Px\|
where
P:=I-\mathbf{1}_{d}\mathbf{1}_{d}^{\top}/d
is LN’s mean-subtraction projector, and
\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{RMS}}(x):=x/\sqrt{\|x\|_{2}^{2}/d}
(no projector).

Proposition 63

(Normalisation kernels: LN admits one, RMSNorm does not)
.

(a) LN admits a deterministic kernel direction.
For any random
X\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
PX\neq 0
almost surely (i.e.,
X
not concentrated on the constant-vector line
\mathrm{span}(\mathbf{1}_{d})
), the covariance
C:=\cov(\mathrm{LN}(X))
has:

1.

If
Z:=\{i:\gamma_{i}=0\}=\emptyset
:
C\cdot\gamma^{-1}=0
with
\gamma^{-1}:=(\gamma_{1}^{-1},\ldots,\gamma_{d}^{-1})^{\top}
, so the unit kernel direction is

v^{*}\;=\;\gamma^{-1}/\|\gamma^{-1}\|.

2.

If
Z\neq\emptyset
:
C\cdot e_{i}=0
for every
i\in Z
, so
\mathrm{span}\{e_{i}:i\in Z\}\subseteq\ker C
; the symmetric indicator
v^{*}=(1/\sqrt{|Z|})\sum_{i\in Z}e_{i}
is one unit kernel direction.

In the uniform-
\gamma
case (
\gamma=c\mathbf{1}_{d}
,
c\neq 0
), case (i) applies and
v^{*}=\mathbf{1}_{d}/\sqrt{d}
.

(b) RMSNorm has no universal kernel direction.
For
\gamma
with
\gamma_{i}\neq 0
for all
i
, there is no unit direction
v=v(\gamma)
depending only on the affine parameter
\gamma
(and not on the input distribution) such that
v^{\top}\cov(\mathrm{RMSNorm}(X))v=0
for all input distributions
X
with
\cov(X)\succ 0
. (If
\gamma
has zero coordinates
Z=\{i:\gamma_{i}=0\}
, the structurally-dead subspace
\mathrm{span}\{e_{i}:i\in Z\}
trivially lies in
\ker\cov(\mathrm{RMSNorm}(X))
, mirroring case (a)(ii); this is a property of
\gamma
, not of the RMSNorm operator, and distinguishes it from LN’s mean-subtraction kernel in case (a)(i) which fires for any
\gamma
.)

The structural distinction is the mean-subtraction projector: LN’s
\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{LN}}
lives in
\mathbf{1}_{d}^{\perp}
algebraically, hence the deterministic kernel for any
\gamma
; RMSNorm’s
\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{RMS}}
does not, hence no kernel direction from
\gamma
alone.

Remark 64

(What is the contribution)
.

The proof below is short. LN’s mean-subtraction projector
P
makes
\mathbf{1}_{d}^{\top}\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{LN}}\equiv 0
, so
\cov(\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{LN}})\mathbf{1}_{d}=0
algebraically and the
\gamma
-rescaling shifts this kernel direction to
\gamma^{-1}/\|\gamma^{-1}\|
readable from the affine alone. The substantive content is the empirical falsifier with known direction this algebra produces: a forward-pass-free prediction that distinguishes LayerNorm from RMSNorm at the affine-parameter level. The dichotomy ties to the framework’s rate primitive via the selection rule (Theorem
3
, Remark
6
): the LN kernel direction is structurally tangential at the post-LN node, so LN’s algebra creates a tangential direction that the rate-classification machinery would identify regardless of training. The corresponding observation for RMSNorm is the differential negative.

Proof.

(a) LN.
By construction
\mathbf{1}_{d}^{\top}\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{LN}}(X)=\sqrt{d}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{d}^{\top}PX/\|PX\|=0
almost surely (since
\mathbf{1}_{d}^{\top}P=\mathbf{1}_{d}^{\top}-(\mathbf{1}_{d}^{\top}\mathbf{1}_{d}/d)\mathbf{1}_{d}^{\top}=0^{\top}
). Hence
\mathbf{1}_{d}^{\top}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{LN}}]=0
by Fubini, and

\cov(\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{LN}})\mathbf{1}_{d}\;=\;\mathbb{E}[\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{LN}}\cdot(\mathbf{1}_{d}^{\top}\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{LN}})]-0\;=\;0.

With
\mathrm{LN}(X)=\mathrm{diag}(\gamma)\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{LN}}(X)+\beta
and
\beta
contributing nothing to covariance (
\cov(AY+b)=A\cov(Y)A^{\top}
),
C=\mathrm{diag}(\gamma)\cov(\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{LN}})\mathrm{diag}(\gamma)
.

Case (a)(i),
Z=\emptyset
.

\gamma^{-1}
is well-defined;
C\gamma^{-1}=\mathrm{diag}(\gamma)\cov(\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{LN}})\mathbf{1}_{d}=0
via
\mathrm{diag}(\gamma)\gamma^{-1}=\mathbf{1}_{d}
coordinate-wise.
Case (a)(ii),
Z\neq\emptyset
.

(Ce_{i})_{j}=\gamma_{j}\cdot\cov(\tilde{x}_{\mathrm{LN}})_{ji}\cdot\gamma_{i}=0
for
i\in Z
(since
\gamma_{i}=0
), so every
e_{i}\in\ker C
. When
|Z|>1
with
\bar{Z}\neq\emptyset
, the empirically-measured bottom singular direction is whichever unit vector in
\mathrm{span}\{e_{i}:i\in Z\}
has smallest residual variance, not algebraically pinned to the symmetric indicator; the appropriate empirical test is principal-angle coherence into
\mathrm{span}\{e_{i}:i\in Z\}
. The uniform-
\gamma
statement is immediate from case (i).

(b) RMSNorm.
Writing
\mathrm{RMSNorm}(X)=r(X)\cdot\gamma\odot X
with
r(X)=\sqrt{d/\|X\|^{2}}
a positive scalar,
\cov(\mathrm{RMSNorm}(X))=\mathrm{diag}(\gamma)\cov(r(X)X)\mathrm{diag}(\gamma)
. For
v(\gamma)
universal,
(\gamma\odot v)^{\top}\cov(r(X)X)(\gamma\odot v)=0
would need to hold for every input distribution. Take
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
:
r(X)X=\sqrt{d}\cdot X/\|X\|
is
\sqrt{d}
times a uniform vector on
S^{d-1}
, with
\cov=I_{d}
by spherical symmetry. Hence
\|\gamma\odot v\|^{2}>0
for any
v\neq 0
(using
\gamma_{i}\neq 0
), contradicting universality.
∎

Remark 65

(Three readings of the LN kernel direction)
.

The same content takes three useful forms.
As an algebraic identity.

\cov(\mathrm{LN}(X))\,v^{*}=0
at
v^{*}=\gamma^{-1}/\|\gamma^{-1}\|
for any input distribution
X
with
PX\neq 0
a.s. The identity is exact, not asymptotic, and depends only on LN’s mean-subtraction projector
P
and the affine
\gamma
.
As a forward-pass-free dead direction.
The selection rule of Theorem
3
classifies eigenvalues of the Fisher metric into tangential and transversal groups by their rate exponents along an approach. The LN kernel
v^{*}
is structurally tangential at the post-LN node: it lives in
\ker\cov(\mathrm{LN}(X))
algebraically, and any rate-classifier applied to the spectrum identifies it as a tangential (gauge) direction regardless of the trajectory or training state. The direction is readable from
\gamma
alone: no forward pass, no gradient, no sampling.
As a falsifiable architectural dichotomy.
LN admits the kernel by virtue of its mean-subtraction; RMSNorm does not (Proposition
63
(b)). The empirical test is direct: read
v^{*}=\gamma^{-1}/\|\gamma^{-1}\|
off any LN-equipped model, compute the centered-covariance bottom singular direction
u_{\bot}
on a calibration batch, and measure
|\cos(u_{\bot},v^{*})|
. LN models pass at
|\cos|\geq 0.988
; RMSNorm models fail at
|\cos|\leq 0.17
at the same coordinate.

The bound above is asymptotic; at the finite
t
where SGD trajectories sit, the LayerNorm rate enters a crossover region whose closed form on the LN-equipped MLP block is given in Appendix
A.6
.

9.4  Single-head attention

Attention sits outside the bridge framework’s standard hypotheses on two counts. The softmax nonlinearity is not element-wise, so it falls outside activation classes (P1)–(P3). The bilinear
QK^{\top}
structure couples
W_{Q}
and
W_{K}
inside the softmax, so the dead-direction Jacobian no longer factorises through independent per-weight columns. The natural question is whether a closed-form rate survives. We answer in the affirmative for a standalone single-head block: the forward and backward block rates are both
k=2
, with the softmax contributions appearing only at sub-leading order. This subsection proves the standalone forward and backward block rates of a single-head self-attention block under canonical init, closing the attention extension at the single-block level. Combined with Theorem
30
(with the
n\geq 4
scope caveat noted below), the single-block rate suffices for many compositions involving attention.

Setup and convention.

Single-head self-attention on input
X\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times d}
, in row-data convention:

Q=XW_{Q}^{\top},\quad K=XW_{K}^{\top},\quad V=XW_{V}^{\top},\quad A=\mathrm{softmax}(QK^{\top}/\sqrt{d}),\quad Y=(AV)W_{O}^{\top}.

Canonical init:
W_{Q}^{*}=W_{K}^{*}=W_{V}^{*}=W_{O}^{*}=\mathrm{diag}(1,\ldots,1,0)
(square symmetric matrices); symmetric dead-direction perturbation
W_{i}(t)=W_{i}^{*}+t\cdot e_{d}e_{d}^{\top}
for
i\in\{Q,K,V,O\}
(shared dead direction
u=e_{d}
). Because each
W_{i}^{*}
is symmetric and
e_{d}
is the same canonical direction on the input and output sides, the dead direction
u=e_{d}
aligns the last input
and
output coordinates simultaneously, which avoids any input/output-side ambiguity in this section. Input
X
has
\mathbb{E}[X_{n}^{\top}X_{n}/N]=I_{d}
,
X_{n,d}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
per sample, with finite fourth moment
\mathbb{E}[X_{n,d}^{4}]<\infty
.

Theorem 66

(Single-head attention forward standalone rate)
.

Under the above setup, the dead-direction output of the attention block satisfies

Y_{n,d}(\theta(t))\;=\;t^{2}\cdot\bar{X}_{n,d}+O(t^{4}),\qquad\bar{X}_{n,d}:=\sum_{m}(A_{0})_{n,m}\,X_{m,d},

where
A_{0}:=\mathrm{softmax}(Q_{0}K_{0}^{\top}/\sqrt{d})
is the attention matrix computed from non-dead channels at
t=0
, and
\bar{X}_{n,d}=\Theta(1)
a.s. Hence the forward block rate is
k_{\mathrm{attn}}^{\mathrm{fwd}}=2
.

Proof.

The dead channel of
Q,K,V
picks up a single factor of
t
from its respective weight:
Q_{n,d}(t)=t\cdot X_{n,d}
, and similarly
K_{n,d}(t)=tX_{n,d}
,
V_{n,d}(t)=tX_{n,d}
. Non-dead channels are
t
-independent at leading order.

Expand the bilinear
QK^{\top}
at entry
(n,m)
:

(QK^{\top})_{n,m}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}Q_{n,i}K_{m,i}=\underbrace{\sum_{i<d}X_{n,i}X_{m,i}}_{=:S_{n,m},\ t\text{-independent}}+\;t^{2}X_{n,d}X_{m,d}.

Thus
QK^{\top}/\sqrt{d}=S/\sqrt{d}+O(t^{2})
, and by Lipschitz continuity of softmax at the operating point,

A(t)=\mathrm{softmax}((QK^{\top})/\sqrt{d})=A_{0}+O(t^{2}),\qquad A_{0}:=\mathrm{softmax}(S/\sqrt{d}),

where the implicit constant in
O(t^{2})
depends on the conditioning of
A_{0}
(softmax Lipschitz constant at the operating point); for nearly-one-hot attention patterns this constant degenerates and contributes to the
n\geq 4
composition anomaly noted in Corollary
68
below. The dead column of
AV
:

(AV)_{n,d}=\sum_{m}A_{n,m}(t)\cdot V_{m,d}(t)=t\cdot\sum_{m}(A_{0})_{n,m}X_{m,d}+O(t^{3})=t\cdot\bar{X}_{n,d}+O(t^{3}).

Finally, applying
W_{O}^{\top}
: the dead-row of
W_{O}
has only the
(d,d)
entry perturbed to
t
, so

Y_{n,d}(t)=\sum_{i}(AV)_{n,i}\cdot(W_{O})_{d,i}=t\cdot(AV)_{n,d}+0=t^{2}\cdot\bar{X}_{n,d}+O(t^{4}).

\bar{X}_{n,d}
is a non-degenerate
\Theta(1)
quantity (attention-weighted average of Gaussian dead channels, non-zero almost surely).
∎

Theorem 67

(Single-head attention backward standalone rate)
.

Assume the upstream output gradient
\partial L/\partial Y_{n,d}
is
\Theta(1)
at the dead row (driven by the loss base case:
\sigma^{-2}
for MSE per Lemma
16
, or
c_{0}
for expected Fisher under cross-entropy per Lemma
33
and the multi-direction CE assumption). Then the input dead-direction gradient at the attention block satisfies
\partial L/\partial X_{n,d}=\Theta(t^{2})
, giving block backward rate
k_{\mathrm{attn}}^{\mathrm{bk}}=2
.

Proof sketch.

By the chain rule, the dead-output gradient backpropagates through (i)
W_{O}
’s dead column (
t
-factor), (ii) the attention output
AV
’s dead column (factor
t
from
V
’s dead column), and (iii) optionally the softmax Jacobian routes through
Q
and
K
.

W_{O}\to V
path.

\partial L/\partial V_{m,d}=\sum_{n}A_{n,m}(t)\cdot(W_{O})_{d,d}(t)\cdot\partial L/\partial Y_{n,d}
, with
A(t)=A_{0}+O(t^{2})
from Theorem
66
and
(W_{O})_{d,d}(t)=t
, giving the
V
-dead gradient
\Theta(t)
. Composing with
V_{m,d}(t)=X_{m,d}\cdot W_{V}^{(d,d)}(t)=X_{m,d}\cdot t
in the upstream chain rule, the
V
-path contribution to
\partial L/\partial X_{n,d}
is
\Theta(t^{2})
(one factor from
W_{V}
’s dead column, one from
W_{O}
’s).

Q,K
paths via softmax Jacobian.
Softmax derivatives are
A_{0}\otimes I-A_{0}A_{0}^{\top}
-structured (rank-deficient:
\mathbf{1}^{\top}\cdot J_{\mathrm{softmax}}=0
), and the upstream input to softmax is
(QK^{\top})/\sqrt{d}
with dead-channel entry of order
t\cdot t=t^{2}
. The gradient through
Q
thus contributes at order
\Theta(1)\cdot\mathrm{Jac}_{\mathrm{softmax}}\cdot t\cdot t=\Theta(t^{3})
at the dead-direction, subleading to the
V
-path’s
\Theta(t^{2})
, provided
A_{0}
has bounded Lipschitz constant (well-conditioned attention pattern). Symmetric for the
K
path.

The
V
-path therefore dominates with rate
2
, matching the forward rate. Both rates equal
2
by the same argument structure that gives
k_{\mathrm{attn}}^{\mathrm{fwd}}=2
(one
t
-factor at the input projection, one at the output projection, with softmax-induced bilinear coupling at the
QK^{\top}
level adding only subleading
t^{3}
).
∎

Corollary 68

(Attention composition rates with anomaly scope)
.

Setting
k_{\mathrm{attn}}^{\mathrm{bk}}=2
in Theorem
30
, the heterogeneous composition rates on
n_{\mathrm{attn}}
attention blocks and
n_{\mathrm{mlp}}
two-weight MLP blocks in sequence (no residual) are
\alpha=2(n_{\mathrm{attn}}+n_{\mathrm{mlp}})
at the input, with internal rates
\alpha_{\ell}=2(L-\ell)
for
L
the total number of blocks. Block-composition validation at
n=2
across the four ordered attention/MLP block pairs (
10
seeds per configuration with seed std
<0.001
) matches the prediction to
4
decimals.

n\geq 4
scope caveat.
Composition additivity holds for
n_{\mathrm{attn}}\leq 3
in pure-attention chains; at
n_{\mathrm{attn}}\geq 4
, softmax cross-block coupling produces a measurable deviation from naive additivity. The structural prediction is closed in Proposition
69
: three closed-form formulas (the
W_{V}
offset and the
W_{Q}=W_{K}
rate parameter-free; the
W_{O}
rate matching once its saturation depth
k^{\star}
is fit) matching parametric data to integer slope precision across the tested
N\in\{4,6\}
and probe positions
p\in\{0,2,3,5\}
. The phenomenology (
W_{O}
saturation at
\alpha\approx 8
,
W_{Q}/W_{K}
input-position scaling,
\alpha_{W_{V}}-\alpha_{W_{O}}=2
invariant) is what the theorem derives from a unified mechanism. On practical residual-wrapped transformers the anomaly is bypassed because the residual skip gives
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}=0
regardless of attention chain depth (Corollary
58
).

Proposition 69

(Refined attention-chain per-component rates, closing Remark
32
structurally)
.

Consider a pure-attention chain of
N
single-head self-attention blocks (no residuals, no MLP, no LayerNorm) at the canonical-aligned uniform-perturbation trajectory
W_{*}^{(j)}(t)=W_{*}^{(j)*}+t\cdot e_{d}e_{d}^{\top}
for all
*\in\{Q,K,V,O\}
and all
j\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}
, with input
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and squared-error loss against a canonical-init target plus Gaussian noise. Fix a probe block
p\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}
and let
u=e_{d}
denote the canonical dead direction. Let
\alpha_{W_{*}}(p,N)
denote the asymptotic log–log slope of
u^{\top}G_{W_{*}^{(p)}}u
versus
t
as
t\to 0
, where
G_{W_{*}^{(p)}}=\mathbb{E}[gg^{\top}]
is the gradient covariance at the output of
W_{*}^{(p)}
(so
\alpha=2\cdot\mathrm{rate}(\sqrt{u^{\top}Gu})
, twice the rate of the gradient’s dead-channel scalar). Let
k:=N-1-p
(depth from output). Suppose there exists a finite
softmax-Jacobian rank-collapse depth

k^{\star}=k^{\star}(d,n_{h},\mathrm{seq\_len})
at which the cumulative product
\prod_{j>p}J_{\mathrm{softmax}}^{(j)}
stops contributing additional dead-channel rate to the backward chain (cf. Remark
72
). Then:

(a)

\alpha_{W_{O}}(p,N)=4\min(k,k^{\star})
(backward-chain rate, saturated at
k^{\star}
).

(b)

\alpha_{W_{V}}(p,N)=\alpha_{W_{O}}(p,N)+2
(within-block invariant, preserved under saturation).

(c)

\alpha_{W_{Q}}(p,N)=\alpha_{W_{K}}(p,N)=4p+2
(forward-chain rate, no saturation).

Empirical validation.
At
(d,n_{h},\mathrm{seq\_len})=(16,2,4)
the formulas match all six tested
(N,p)
configurations
\times
four components (
24
measurement cells in total) at
N\in\{4,6\}
,
p\in\{0,2,3,5\}
to
integer slope precision
, with
k^{\star}=2
measured from the saturation onset of
\alpha_{W_{O}}
. Per-cell std across
3
seeds is
<10^{-3}
on the fitted slope (parametric freeze-probe in fp64, fit on
t\leq 10^{-2}
). Cross-configuration validity (other
(d,n_{h},\mathrm{seq\_len})
) requires re-measuring
k^{\star}
.

Sketch.

We derive (b), (c), and the un-saturated half of (a) directly from the bridge framework via canonical-coordinate calculus and the chain rule. The saturation onset
k^{\star}
in (a) is empirical with a structural mechanism (cumulative softmax-Jacobian rank collapse) but no derived closed form for
k^{\star}(d,n_{h},\mathrm{seq\_len})
; cf. Remark
72
.

(a) Un-saturated backward chain.
The dead-channel gradient
\partial L/\partial Y^{(p)}_{n,d}
at the probe’s output is computed by backward propagation from the loss through blocks
p+1,\ldots,N-1
. Each upstream block contributes a factor of
t^{2}
via the dual of Theorem
66
’s
V
-path (Theorem
67
:
\partial Y^{(j)}_{n^{\prime},d}/\partial Y^{(j-1)}_{n,d}=t^{2}A^{(j)}_{n^{\prime},n}
at leading order; one
t
-factor each from
W_{V}^{(j)}
’s and
W_{O}^{(j)}
’s dead diagonal). Composing
k=N-1-p
such steps gives
\partial L/\partial Y^{(p)}_{n,d}=\Theta(t^{2k})
, hence
\alpha_{W_{O}}(p,N)=4k
for
k\leq k^{\star}
.

(a) Saturation onset and value (mechanism statement).
Each backward step factors through the softmax Jacobian
J_{\mathrm{softmax}}^{(j)}=\mathrm{diag}(A_{0}^{(j)})-A_{0}^{(j)}A_{0}^{(j)\top}
, which is rank
\leq\mathrm{seq\_len}-1
(the all-ones direction lies in its kernel). It is a structural property of cumulative softmax-Jacobian products that the product
\prod_{j>p}J_{\mathrm{softmax}}^{(j)}
has effective rank that decays with depth and converges to a low-rank attractor (a phenomenon documented in the attention-rank-collapse literature
(
Dong et al. 2021
;
Noci et al. 2022
)
; the same mechanism, observed forward-side as token-representation collapse, here acts backward-side on the dead-channel gradient). Once the cumulative Jacobian’s image dimension stops shrinking (at depth
k^{\star}
) additional backward steps map within the attractor and apply a
\Theta(1)
rotation/projection but no further
t
-factor; the dead-channel gradient rate caps at
2k^{\star}
and
\alpha_{W_{O}}
caps at
4k^{\star}
. The depth
k^{\star}
at which this transition happens depends on the conditioning of
A_{0}^{(j)}=\mathrm{softmax}(S^{(j)}/\sqrt{d})
at canonical init (with
S^{(j)}_{n,m}=\sum_{i<d}X^{(j)}_{n,i}X^{(j)}_{m,i}
the non-dead bilinear form); we observe
k^{\star}=2
at
(16,2,4)
.

(b) The
W_{V}
–
W_{O}
invariant.
The gradient at
V^{(p)}
’s dead-channel output,
\partial L/\partial V^{(p)}_{m,d}
, depends on
Y^{(p)}_{n,d^{\prime}}
only through dead-row
d^{\prime}=d
of
W_{O}^{(p)}
, because
V^{(p)}_{m,d}
feeds
(AV)_{n,d}
which feeds
Y^{(p)}_{n,d}
via
(W_{O}^{(p)})_{d,d}
alone (non-dead rows of
W_{O}^{(p)}
map dead
V
to non-dead
Y
at order
0
, not
t
). So
\partial L/\partial V^{(p)}_{m,d}=\sum_{n}A^{(p)}_{n,m}\cdot(W_{O}^{(p)})_{d,d}\cdot\partial L/\partial Y^{(p)}_{n,d}
, picking up one extra factor of
t
from
(W_{O}^{(p)})_{d,d}
relative to
\partial L/\partial Y^{(p)}_{n,d}
itself. Squaring gives
\alpha_{W_{V}}-\alpha_{W_{O}}=2
, preserved under chain composition because saturation acts above the probe while
V\to W_{O}
ordering is local.

(c) Forward-chain rate at
W_{Q},W_{K}
.
The dominant contribution to
\partial L/\partial Q^{(p)}_{n,d}
does
not
route through
\partial L/\partial Y^{(p)}_{n,d}
at the dead row (which carries the saturated rate from (a) plus the
(W_{O})_{d,d}=t
factor); it routes through
\partial L/\partial Y^{(p)}_{n,d^{\prime}}
at
non-dead
rows
d^{\prime}\neq d
, which have rate
\Theta(1)
(non-dead loss-gradient components do not decay through canonical block structure). The chain rule:

\partial L/\partial Q^{(p)}_{n,d}\;=\;\sum_{d^{\prime},m,m^{\prime}}\partial L/\partial Y^{(p)}_{n,d^{\prime}}\cdot\partial Y^{(p)}_{n,d^{\prime}}/\partial A^{(p)}_{n,m}\cdot\partial A^{(p)}_{n,m}/\partial Q^{(p)}_{n,d},

with
\partial A^{(p)}_{n,m}/\partial Q^{(p)}_{n,d}
proportional to
K^{(p)}_{m,d}/\sqrt{d}
via the softmax Jacobian. The dead-channel of the probe block’s input
X^{(p)}_{n,d}
scales as
\Theta(t^{2p})
from the cumulative forward chain (
p
upstream attention blocks, each contributing
t^{2}
via Theorem
66
); then
K^{(p)}_{m,d}=t\cdot X^{(p)}_{m,d}
has rate
2p+1
(one extra factor from the
(d,d)
entry of
W_{K}^{(p)}
). The non-dead
d^{\prime}\neq d
summands give
\Theta(1)\cdot\Theta(1)\cdot\Theta(t^{2p+1})=\Theta(t^{2p+1})
, dominating the dead-row
d^{\prime}=d
contribution which carries an additional
(W_{O})_{d,d}=t
factor. Squaring:
\alpha_{W_{Q}}(p,N)=2(2p+1)=4p+2
. By the symmetric role of
Q
and
K
in
QK^{\top}
, the same rate applies to
W_{K}
.
∎

Conjecture 70

(Cross-configuration validity of the attention-chain rate formulas)
.

The closed-form expressions of Proposition
69
parts (b), (c), and the un-saturated half of (a) hold for every
(d,n_{h},\mathrm{seq\_len})
in the practical attention regime (
d\geq 8
,
n_{h}\geq 1
,
\mathrm{seq\_len}\geq 2
, dot-product softmax with
1/\sqrt{d}
normalisation, canonical alignment). The saturation depth
k^{\star}(d,n_{h},\mathrm{seq\_len})
is finite and bounded above by
\min(\mathrm{seq\_len}-1,n_{h}\cdot\mathrm{seq\_len})
(the rank of the cumulative softmax-Jacobian product). Both claims are validated at
(d,n_{h},\mathrm{seq\_len})=(16,2,4)
to integer slope precision; cross-configuration validation and a closed-form expression for
k^{\star}
are open.

Remark 71

(Scope of Proposition
69
)
.

Components (b), (c), and the un-saturated half of (a) are derived from the bridge framework. The saturation onset
k^{\star}
in (a) is empirical: its quantitative depth bound is not derived, and closing it is the analytical residual (Remark
72
). Cross-configuration validity is the subject of Conjecture
70
; the formulas are config-specific in the constant
k^{\star}
but config-independent in the structural form.

Remark 72

(Open: closed-form for
k^{\star}(d,n_{h},\mathrm{seq\_len})
)
.

The saturation depth
k^{\star}
in Proposition
69
(a) is established empirically at
(d,n_{h},\mathrm{seq\_len})=(16,2,4)
as
k^{\star}=2
. A closed-form expression in terms of these parameters is open: it requires sharpening the cumulative-Jacobian rank-collapse mechanism into a quantitative depth bound, which would compose attention-rank-collapse rates
(
Dong et al. 2021
;
Noci et al. 2022
)
with the dead-channel projection. The structural prediction (the three formulas (a)–(c) with
k^{\star}
as a parameter) is independent of the closed form and is the operationally useful statement; per-architectural-configuration
k^{\star}
can be measured from a single
\alpha_{W_{O}}(k)
-vs-
k
sweep.

Remark 73

(Resolution of Remark
32
’s three empirical findings)
.

The empirical signatures listed in Remark
32
are derived consequences of Proposition
69
:

•

W_{O}

saturation at
\alpha\approx 8
for
n-1-\ell\geq 3
: from (a),
\alpha_{W_{O}}(p,N)=4\min(k,2)
at
k^{\star}=2
; the saturated value is
4\cdot 2=8
.

•

W_{Q},W_{K}

rates scale with input position
p
rather than output position
k
: from (c),
\alpha_{W_{Q}}(p,N)=4p+2
depends only on
p
.

•

\alpha_{W_{V}}-\alpha_{W_{O}}=2
invariant: from (b), preserved under saturation as a within-block ordering consequence.

Scope.

Theorems
66
and
67
cover single-head self-attention under canonical init with shared dead direction across
\{W_{Q},W_{K},W_{V},W_{O}\}
. Extensions:

•

Multi-head, shared dead direction across heads.
Each head has its own
Q,K,V
projections. Under the assumption that the same dead direction
u=e_{d}
acts on each head (a canonical-construction property, not generic), per-head analysis yields rate
2
per head, and concatenation preserves it. A parametric freeze-probe sweep across
d\in\{16,32,64,128,256,384,768\}
and
n_{h}\in\{1,2,4,8\}
(all
d_{h}=d/n_{h}\geq 2
), 3 seeds per configuration (72 combinations total), gives asymptotic rate (fit on
t\leq 10^{-2}
) of
2.000\pm 0.000
on
W_{Q}
,
W_{K}
,
W_{V}
across all configurations.

•

Multi-head, per-head independent dead directions.
If each head carries its own dead direction in its
d_{h}
-dimensional sub-block, the multi-direction theorem (Theorem
106
) applies per head with
m\leq n_{h}
directions; rates are independent across heads. Not separately tested at multi-head scale at the time of writing; natural follow-up.

•

Cross-attention with
X_{q}\neq X_{k}
.
The bilinear
QK^{\top}
entry
(QK^{\top})_{n,m}
contains
t^{2}(X_{q})_{n,d}(X_{k})_{m,d}
at the dead channel; the rate-2 forward propagation extends if both
\mathbb{E}[(X_{q})_{n,d}^{2}]
and
\mathbb{E}[(X_{k})_{m,d}^{2}]
are
\Theta(1)
. If only one of
X_{q},X_{k}
has a dead-channel contribution (e.g., a static encoder-side dead direction with no decoder-side counterpart), the rate degenerates to a single-side factor.

•

Masked / causal attention.

S_{n,m}
is set to
-\infty
for
m>n
; the structure of
A_{0}
changes but the
t
-scaling argument is unaffected. Rate
2
holds.

•

Residual attention (transformer block).
Combined with Theorem
43
, the residual skip dominates and the attention block’s
k_{\mathrm{attn}}=2
is absorbed into
K^{\mathrm{fwd}}=0
at block outputs. Standard for pre-norm transformers.

•

Position encodings
(RoPE, learnable absolute, learnable relative). These compose with attention as natural follow-ups; closing each requires verifying that the position-encoding nonlinearity (e.g., RoPE phase mixing) preserves the dead-direction’s
t
-scaling, which we have not done.

The Gaussian input assumption is used only to ensure the
\bar{X}_{n,d}
second moment is
\Theta(1)
uniformly; any input distribution with
\mathbb{E}[X_{n,d}^{2}]=\Theta(1)
and finite fourth moment suffices.

Remark 74

(Static-Fisher rate vs. Adam
+
CE attention trajectory)
.

Theorems
66
and
67
are static-Fisher statements at the parametric trajectory. On a transformer block trained with Adam
+
CE, the per-component trajectory rate readout is
doubly
confounded: by the empirically-observed Adam non-equivariance under the CE-shift and ReLU-rescaling gauges (Remark
80
), and by softmax cross-block coupling at
n\geq 4
pure-attention chains (Remark
32
). The robust transformer-scale observable is therefore residual-stream
\sigma_{\min}
(Corollary
58
), not
u^{\top}Gu
on attention weights.

Remark 75

(Joint scope with rectangular widths and cross-entropy)
.

The single-head attention rate is local to one block, so it composes disjointly with the rectangular extension (which applies per-Linear inside the block) and the CE extension (which replaces only the output-head base case; attention is a hidden block, so its per-block rate is unaffected and the joint statement holds with
k_{\mathrm{attn}}=2
and the output-head
\Theta(1)
base case driven by Assumption
34
rather than
\sigma^{-2}
).

Figure 8
:
Refined attention-chain composition rates (Proposition
69
). The three closed forms the proposition gives, each on real data. (a)
\alpha_{W_{O}}(p,N)=4\min(N{-}1{-}p,\,k^{\star})
: the backward chain saturates at
k^{\star}=2
, the cumulative softmax-Jacobian rank-collapse depth. (b)
\alpha_{W_{V}}-\alpha_{W_{O}}=2
: a within-block invariant preserved under saturation (one Linear’s worth of
t
-factor in grad-output, regardless of the rest of the chain). (c)
\alpha_{W_{Q}}=\alpha_{W_{K}}=4p+2
: the forward chain has no saturation; the rate scales with input position
p
, not output position. The three formulas match all
24
measurement cells across
N\in\{4,6\}
and
p\in\{0,2,3,5\}
at
(d,n_{h},\mathrm{seq\_len})=(16,2,4)
to integer-slope precision.

Figure 9
:
Architectural freeze-probe roundup for the per-primitive lemmas of this section and the architectural catalogue (Appendix
A
; numbers from Appendix
B.2
). (a) Primitives that preserve the canonical rate ladder: measured versus predicted per-layer dead-direction rate
\alpha_{\ell}
, with the activation-dependent tolerance bands. Every point lies on the diagonal, and the multi-direction sweep adds
684/684
per-direction rates within tolerance. (b) Primitives that change the rate: each arrow runs from the canonical (no-op) rate to the measured rate, and the measured change matches the lemma’s predicted target (tick). A residual skip caps the in-block rate (Corollary
48
), off-canonical rotation collapses the nonlinear rate (Proposition
119
), and LayerNorm reduces the integer ladder to a fractional rate (Theorem
60
).

9.5  Remarks on Scope and Extensions

The 2-layer case: a stronger closed form.

At
L=2
, Theorem
21
gives
\lambda_{\min}(G_{1})=\Theta(t^{2})
and
\lambda_{\min}(G_{2})=\Theta(1)
under its canonical-aligned square-weights setup. The two-layer case in fact admits a stronger statement: a closed form holds under
weaker
hypotheses that Theorem
21
does
not
subsume.

•

Linear 2-layer:
For
W_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{h\times d_{\mathrm{in}}}
,
W_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}\times h}
(
rectangular
),
arbitrary rank

r^{*}<h
, and
arbitrary
activated direction
u\perp\col(W_{2}^{*})
,
v\perp\mathrm{row}(W_{1}^{*})
, one has the
closed form

G_{1}=\sigma^{-2}(W_{2}^{*})^{\top}W_{2}^{*}
at the optimum, with eigenvalues
\sigma^{-2}s_{1}^{2}\geq\cdots\geq\sigma^{-2}s_{r^{*}}^{2}>0=\cdots=0
where
s_{i}
are the singular values of
W_{2}^{*}
. Proof: at the optimum, the residual is
\varepsilon
, so
G_{1}=\mathbb{E}[g_{1}g_{1}^{\top}]=\sigma^{-2}(W_{2}^{*})^{\top}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon\varepsilon^{\top}]W_{2}^{*}
using
\varepsilon\perp x
. Along the symmetric approach,
\lambda_{\min}^{>0}(G_{1}(t))=\sigma^{2}t^{2}\|u\|^{2}=\Theta(t^{2(k-1)})
for
k=2
.

•

Smooth-nonlinear 2-layer:
Same setup as above plus a smooth activation satisfying
\phi(0)=0
,
\phi^{\prime}(0)\neq 0
,
\phi\in C^{2}
. Along the symmetric approach,
\lambda_{\min}^{>0}(G_{1}(t))=\phi^{\prime}(0)^{2}\sigma^{2}t^{2}\|u\|^{2}+O(t^{3})
. For
\phi=\mathrm{id}
,
\phi^{\prime}(0)=1
recovers the linear result. This case handles rectangular weights and a dead direction off-canonical in the input and output maps, provided it remains a coordinate axis in the hidden basis: the element-wise
\phi^{\prime}
does not commute with a rotation of that basis, so, unlike the fully rotation-covariant linear case, a rotated hidden-basis direction masks the rate. The single
\phi
application avoids the cross-layer coordinate mixing that forces canonical alignment at
L\geq 3
.

These 2-layer closed forms are
not
specializations of Theorem
21
: they hold under weaker hypotheses (rectangular weights, any rank
r^{*}<h
, any activated direction) and give a closed form rather than only a rate. Conversely, Theorem
21
extends the 2-layer rate to
L\geq 2
, but requires square weights, canonical-basis alignment, and
r^{*}=h-1
. The canonical-alignment assumption is WLOG in the linear case at any depth (rotation invariance), but is a genuine restriction in the nonlinear case at
L\geq 3
, where element-wise
\phi
couples non-canonical directions through the forward-backward chain, breaking the dead-direction Schur complement reduction (Lemma
17
) used in the proof.

Theorem
21
and the 2-layer closed forms are therefore
complementary
: the closed forms hold under weaker hypotheses at a single depth, while Theorem
21
extends to arbitrary depth
L
at the cost of stronger hypotheses. Joint-scope status of the closed extensions: Theorem
103
(rectangular widths) closes any
L
, and Theorem
106
(multi-direction with asymmetric per-layer exponents) closes multi-rank singular configurations; these commute (the rectangular Schur reduction acts on the non-dead block, the multi-direction Schur reduction acts on the dead block) and the joint statement holds. Genuinely open: non-canonical dead directions at
L\geq 3
under nonlinear activations (Proposition
119
’s negative result; near-canonical continuity remains open per Corollary
120
).

KL order scales with depth.

The KL order
k=L
along the symmetric approach arises because activating a dead direction through the depth-
L
chain requires
L
coordinated perturbations (one per layer). Each factor contributes
O(t)
; their product gives
O(t^{L})
in the effective weight and
O(t^{2L})
in the KL divergence. For
L=2
, this recovers
k=2
(deep-linear reduced-rank-regression merge, analogous to the Gaussian mixture merge at
k=2
). For general
L
, the KL order scales linearly with depth.

ReLU and non-smooth activations.

Classical bridge arguments require
\phi\in C^{2}
with
\phi^{\prime}(0)\neq 0
, which excludes ReLU. In the symmetric-canonical setup (P3), ReLU is handled by a direct argument: for
t>0
and
x^{(h)}
of definite sign,
a_{\ell}^{(h)}
has fixed sign along the entire approach trajectory, so
\phi^{\prime}(a_{\ell}^{(h)})\in\{0,1\}
is constant per sample. The Taylor argument is unnecessary. Numerically, the rate holds exactly, cleaner than smooth activations, which carry finite-
t
Taylor corrections. A formal distributional-derivative treatment for non-aligned dead directions would be an interesting extension.

For 2-layer ReLU networks at
L=2
(validated in Table
5
), the effective
\phi^{\prime}(0)^{2}=1/2
factor reflects isotropic Gaussian data passing through sign-symmetric ReLU: half the probability mass has
a_{1}^{(h)}>0
(contributing) and half has
a_{1}^{(h)}<0
(zero contribution), giving
\mathbb{E}[\phi^{\prime}(h)^{2}]=1/2
. The rate
\Theta(t^{2})
survives, with the dead-direction Schur reduction of Lemma
17
handling the sign-definite case directly (no Taylor correction needed).

Table 5
:
2-layer bridge validation across five activations (5 seeds,
6\to 8\to 4
network). Fitted exponent
\alpha
in
\lambda_{\min}^{>0}(G_{1})\sim t^{\alpha}
; predicted
\alpha=2.000
for
k=2
. Deviations reflect the finite-
t
Taylor correction
O(t)
for smooth activations; linear and ReLU match exactly within sampling noise.

1.996\pm 0.007
