the renormalised posterior
\pi_{0}\propto e^{-\eta^{2k}}
is symmetric,
\tfrac{1}{2}\eta^{2k}\sim\Gamma(\lambda,1)
, and the
variance collapses to the data-fluctuation-free component
\nu^{0}(k)=\lambda-(\Gamma(\lambda+1/2)/\Gamma(\lambda))^{2}
. The data average
Z\neq 0
tilts
\pi_{Z}

and adds a strictly positive contribution (numerically the dominant part for

k\geq 2
), so
\nu_{\mathrm{LO}}(k)>\nu^{0}(k)
. (Dropping the data average leaves only the data-fluctuation-free
\nu^{0}=V[\sqrt{T}]
: a parity argument removes the term
linear
in
Z
, but the quadratic term, the variance of the tilt, survives and is the bulk of
\nu_{\mathrm{LO}}
.)
∎

Remark 97

(Numerical values)
.

Two independent methods agree on
\nu_{\mathrm{LO}}(k)
: a deterministic
quadrature of the renormalised-posterior integral above, and a finite-
n
Monte
Carlo of the functional variance
V_{n}
on the power-law model

\mathcal{N}(s^{k},1)
. The regular case
k=1
(added as a consistency anchor,
outside the
k\geq 2
hypothesis) returns the textbook value
\nu=\lambda=1/2
,
which the data-fluctuation-free
\nu^{0}
does
not
reproduce.

\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}k&\lambda&\nu^{0}(k)\ (\text{data-free},\ =V[\sqrt{T}])&\nu_{\mathrm{LO}}(k)\ (\text{full, numerical})\\
\hline\cr 1&1/2&0.182&0.500\ \ (\text{regular anchor})\\
2&1/4&0.136&0.173\\
3&1/6&0.107&0.278\\
4&1/8&0.089&0.138\\
5&1/10&0.076&0.206\\
\end{array}

\nu_{\mathrm{LO}}(k)
is
not
monotone in
k
: odd and even orders form two
interleaved decreasing sequences (odd
k
sits higher), a parity effect of

\eta^{k}
under the symmetric renormalised posterior. The data-free component

\nu^{0}(k)=\lambda-(\Gamma(\lambda+1/2)/\Gamma(\lambda))^{2}
decreases
monotonically and underestimates
\nu_{\mathrm{LO}}
at every order; it has the
large-
k
asymptote
\nu^{0}(k)\sim\lambda-\pi\lambda^{2}=\tfrac{1}{2k}-\tfrac{\pi}{4k^{2}}+O(k^{-3})
.

Remark 98

(Empirical verification: universality and value)
.

Two claims are checked separately.
Universality
(the leading coefficient

\mathbb{E}_{q}[a_{k}^{2}]
cancels): on the 2-component Gaussian mixture at
k=2
across
four mixture weights
w\in\{0.5,0.4,1/3,0.2\}
, the functional-variance
estimate
\widehat{\nu}_{\infty}
is independent of
\mathbb{E}_{q}[a_{2}^{2}]
despite a
factor-of-
2.4
variation in that coefficient; an independent cross-check varying
the leading-coefficient scale
c
in
\mathcal{N}(c\,s^{k},1)
confirms

c
-independence at
k=2
and
k=3
.

Value
: the direct functional-variance computation pins

\nu_{\mathrm{LO}}(2)\approx 0.173
and
\nu_{\mathrm{LO}}(3)\approx 0.278
.
The independent
k=3
Monte Carlo returns

\widehat{\nu}_{\infty}=0.274\pm 0.038
, which is
4.4\sigma
above the
data-fluctuation-free
\nu^{0}(3)=0.107
and consistent with

\nu_{\mathrm{LO}}(3)
. The mixture estimates at
k=2
(
\widehat{\nu}_{\infty}
in

0.106
–
0.168
,
\sigma\sim 0.03
–
0.08
) confirm the
w
-independence but
are individually too noisy to separate
\nu^{0}(2)=0.136
from

\nu_{\mathrm{LO}}(2)\approx 0.173
; the value is pinned by the tighter
power-law computation and the
k=1,3
anchors. The empirical fluctuation is
therefore not negligible: the singular fluctuation is the data-averaged
functional variance, not its
Z=0
value.

Figure 10
:
Singular fluctuation along a 1D dead direction (Theorem
96
).
(a)
The full singular fluctuation
\nu_{\rm LO}(k)
(data-averaged functional variance, points; two independent methods) against the data-fluctuation-free component
\nu^{0}(k)=\lambda-(\Gamma(\lambda+1/2)/\Gamma(\lambda))^{2}
(lower curve).
\nu^{0}
omits the empirical fluctuation and underestimates
\nu_{\rm LO}
at every order;
\nu_{\rm LO}
is non-monotone, with odd
k
(including the regular anchor
\nu(1)=1/2
) sitting above the even-
k
sequence.
(b)
Universality: at
k=2
across mixture weights
w\in\{0.5,0.4,1/3,0.2\}
the estimate
\hat{\nu}_{\infty}
is independent of the leading coefficient
\mathbb{E}_{q}[a_{2}^{2}]
(which varies by
2.4\times
), confirming the cancellation. These mixture estimates are too noisy to separate
\nu_{\rm LO}(2)\approx 0.173
from the data-free
\nu^{0}(2)=0.136
(both shown); the value is settled by the tighter power-law Monte Carlo and the
k=1,3
anchors.

Remark 99

(Scope: dead direction only; non-uniform priors; multi-direction)
.

Theorem
96
is for a single 1D dead direction
with uniform prior. Three explicit boundaries:

•

Regular directions
(
k=1
,
a_{1}\neq 0
in
L^{2}
):
\nu_{\mathrm{LO}}
is continuous with the regular case, returning
\nu=\lambda=d/2
(
=1/2
in 1D), the standard realizable-Fisher value
(
Watanabe 2018
, Theorem 6). The data-fluctuation-free
\nu^{0}
does not:
it gives
1/2-1/\pi\approx 0.182
, which is one way to see that
\nu^{0}

cannot be the singular fluctuation.

•

Non-uniform priors
: a prior
\pi(\theta)\propto|\langle u,\theta-\theta_{0}\rangle|^{h}
shifts the renormalised shape parameter to

\lambda+h/(2k)
; the universality argument extends verbatim, with the
data-averaged functional variance computed at the shifted shape (and its
data-free component obtained by the same
\lambda\mapsto\lambda+h/(2k)

substitution in
\nu^{0}
).

•

Multi-direction
: along multi-component normal crossings
(Theorem
89
), the renormalised posterior factorises
into per-component pieces under the additive transversal normal form; the
universality argument extends per-component, and the aggregate
\nu
is

\sum_{i}\nu_{\mathrm{LO}}(k_{i})
in the additive case. The multiplicative
(Hironaka-resolved) normal-crossing case
K=u_{0}\prod_{i}g_{i}^{2k_{i}}
is the
natural next-target extension.

Part VI  Open problems and discussion

14  What remains open

The closures in §
13
carry the bridge across the bulk of Watanabe’s broader inventory. The remaining items are the following. None is, as far as we can see, structurally blocked; each is a research target.

Hironaka resolution at
r>3
.

Theorem
89
’s joint-recovery protocol is established for
r=2
and extended along the same lines to
r=3
via Remark
95
. For
r>3
the multiplicative Hironaka normal form
K=u(g)\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{i}^{2k_{i}}
would need partial re-engagement of the resolution machinery: the generic-transversal trajectory rate
\alpha_{\mathrm{gen}}=2(\sum_{i}k_{i}-1)
still holds, but disambiguating individual
k_{i}
from the rate sum alone requires
r-1
independent
\varepsilon
-anchored single-component rates, and choosing the
\varepsilon
-anchor scaling so the higher-component cross-terms remain subleading becomes delicate as
r
grows.

Trajectory-rate analog of
\nu
in original coordinates.

Theorem
96
establishes that
\nu_{\mathrm{LO}}(k)
is universal (a function of the KL order alone) and reports it numerically; only its data-fluctuation-free component
\nu^{0}(k)=V[\sqrt{T}]=\lambda-(\Gamma(\lambda+1/2)/\Gamma(\lambda))^{2}
has an elementary closed form, and a closed form for the full
\nu_{\mathrm{LO}}
(with the empirical-fluctuation average included) is itself open. A complementary observable would specify how the gen-train gap scales
along an approach
as a function of
\sigma_{\min}
in original parameter coordinates, anchored at the bridge framework’s predicted exponent
\lambda
. This is a distinct construction from the rate observables for
\lambda
surfaced throughout the present work: it requires both a training-side and a population-side loss reading along the trajectory. Promoting it to a real-network observable is straightforward in principle (track training and validation loss alongside the existing Fisher diagnostics) but requires the Watanabe-side integral on the resolved fiber to be closed for the bridge framework’s K-FAC-decomposed Fisher, which we leave for follow-on work.

Theoretical
\beta
-sweep of
(\lambda,m)
at
\beta\neq 1
.

The operational
\beta
-rescaling of the gen-train gap (§
13.4
) lets the trajectory side track tempered posteriors. What the present theorems do not yet address is the theoretical
\beta
-sweep of the RLCT and multiplicity themselves: under WBIC’s
\beta=1/\log n
tempering, the leading constants in Watanabe’s free-energy expansion are
\beta
-rescaled but the qualitative structure
(\lambda,m)
is preserved. A precise statement of how the trajectory rate exponent shifts under
\beta\neq 1
, beyond the convention-preserving rescaling, remains open.

Non-i.i.d. data.

Watanabe’s later work extends the free-energy formula to Markov processes and time series. The trajectory-rate framework’s score expansion uses i.i.d. samples; the extension to non-i.i.d. data (sequence-data SLT) has not yet been addressed and is a natural follow-on direction.

Posterior-sampling tasks with arbitrary
f(\theta)
.

Bayesian quantities of the form
\mathbb{E}_{\rm post}[f(\theta)]
for an arbitrary
f
that was not observed per checkpoint are structurally outside what a deterministic-trajectory framework can reach: the integrand cannot be re-evaluated from stored trajectory data alone. SGLD-based posterior sampling remains the native tool. The boundary is methodological: the trajectory rate covers everything readable from a per-checkpoint deterministic observable, and arbitrary-
f
posterior expectations belong to the stochastic-sampling side.

Amari-side connections not yet drawn on.

The dual
(\nabla,\nabla^{*})
connections of information geometry interact with the gauge-quotient picture of §
10
in ways the present framework has not exploited. The exponential / mixture flatness duality of standard families has not yet been used to characterise which families are “simple” (single-direction, low KL order) versus “complex” (multi-component) from the rate viewpoint. Both are natural directions for follow-on theory.

Closed-form rate modifiers for non-equivariant preconditioners.

Section
11
’s constructive resolution provides a
G
-equivariant Adam-family preconditioner that satisfies the quotient theorem’s sufficient condition. A closed-form per-trajectory rate modifier on the alignment-rotated manifold for
standard
non-equivariant Adam remains open, as it does for the other non-SGD preconditioners (K-FAC, Shampoo, Muon), none of which has a derived trajectory rate.
Pesme et al. 2021
give the analogous analysis for diagonal linear networks under SGD.

Trajectory-rate exponents for normalisations beyond LayerNorm.

The mean-subtraction dichotomy of Proposition
63
fixes which production normalisations carry an algebraic kernel direction, but the trajectory-rate exponent under BatchNorm, GroupNorm, and the RMSNorm variants is not derived.

15  Discussion

What the bridge gives each tradition.

Singular learning theory gains a rate axis: questions about how fast a singularity is approached, under what dynamics, and with what optimiser-specific corrections become well-posed at the trajectory level rather than only at the asymptotic-posterior level. Theorem
3
makes the single-direction contribution
1/(2k)
to the local RLCT recoverable from a Fisher rate exponent on smooth singular fibres under the transversality genericity condition (G), in original parameter coordinates rather than in resolved coordinates. Theorem
89
extends the recovery to the multi-component normal-crossing form
K=u(g)\prod_{i}g_{i}^{2k_{i}}
, giving the joint
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r})
tuple from trajectory rates; the Jacobian exponents
h_{i}
in Watanabe’s full RLCT enter as an explicit prior choice. Theorem
96
carries the bridge to the singular fluctuation
\nu
, the second summary invariant of singular generalisation, as a universal function of the KL order for
1
D dead directions with uniform prior (its data-fluctuation-free component has the closed form
\lambda-(\Gamma(\lambda+1/2)/\Gamma(\lambda))^{2}
; the full value is numerical). Information geometry gains an extension to its degeneracy locus: dead directions are objects in Amari’s language, with rates that are properties of the Fisher metric in a regime where the metric itself ceases to be invertible; the A–G duality is stated entirely in Kronecker-factored Fisher language; and the multi-layer K-FAC bridge extends the natural-gradient lineage to the singular regime where natural gradient is not defined.

Position within the broader SLT-DL landscape.

The trajectory-rate framework sits among a small cluster of recent programs operationalising different aspects of singular structure in deep networks. Each one starts from Watanabe (2009) but goes in its own direction; we name each and indicate the relationship to the present framework. The
local learning coefficient

(
Lau et al. 2025
;
Wang et al. 2024
;
Hoogland et al. 2024
)
estimates Watanabe’s
\lambda
numerically via SGLD posterior sampling around a checkpoint, with a refined variant attaching the estimate to specific weight subsets and a stagewise reading tracking
\lambda
across training. The trajectory-rate framework is the deterministic, K-FAC-block-local, sampling-free alternative: same
\lambda
, different operationalisation. The
susceptibility
programme
(
Baker et al. 2025
;
Gordon et al. 2026
)
probes the response of the loss landscape to localised perturbations of network behaviour, clustering directions by their susceptibility signature; the trajectory-rate framework provides a per-K-FAC-block rate that susceptibilities can be conditioned on. The
loss kernel
of
Adam et al. 2025
is a geometric probe of model internals (the covariance of per-sample losses under low-loss-preserving perturbations), with the Hessian-free
Bayesian influence functions
of
Kreer et al. 2025
;
Lee et al. 2025
extending it to data attribution sensitive to higher-order degeneracy; these are geometric companions to the leading-rate readout.
Compressibility and minimum description length

(
Urdshals et al. 2025
)
formalise the bit-length of a singular minimum and recover MDL in the regular case; the trajectory-rate framework gives the rate exponent per direction, which compressibility integrates over.
Programs as singularities

(
Murfet and Troiani 2025
)
connects degenerate statistical models to the geometry of programs; the trajectory rate is one observable on the singular-program manifold this line constructs.
Modes of sequence models

(
Chen and Murfet 2025
)
characterises the sensitivity of LLC estimation to input-distribution patterns; the trajectory-rate framework’s expected-Fisher protocol (§
9.1
) is the corresponding sensitivity statement on the rate side. None of these lines is a competitor; each probes a slice of the singular geometry the present framework also touches, and each provides a falsifier for the trajectory-rate readings on architectures where the lines overlap.

Forward-looking directions the bridge unlocks.

The theorems above give concrete leverage on several practical problems. We phrase each as an open question that the framework provides the analytic target for, rather than as a result the framework promises.

Compression and rank reduction.
Is the Fisher rate exponent the rate-correct subspace for LoRA placement, structured pruning, and low-rank fine-tuning? The framework provides the analytic criterion (dead-direction definition
u^{\top}Fu\to 0
) and a finer signal than rank-deficiency (the rate exponent). The compressibility-MDL line of
Urdshals et al. 2025
pursues a related direction from the LLC side; whether the per-direction rate exponent gives a better subspace-selection criterion than the standard activation-variance or empirical-Fisher heuristics is an open empirical question.

Continual-learning safe-direction constraints.
Methods that constrain updates to directions leaving the current task’s loss unchanged to leading order (orthogonal-gradient, null-space) currently identify these directions empirically. Is the dead-direction definition, a unit vector along which
u^{\top}Fu\to 0
, the analytic target these methods are approximating? Falsifying this requires comparing the empirically-selected safe directions to the framework’s predicted dead directions on a continual-learning benchmark.

Equivariant optimizer design under gauge invariance.
What does an equivariant preconditioner look like for each architectural gauge group? The quotient theorem (Corollary
78
) specifies the design constraint; DDCAdam (Algorithm
2
) constructs it for three classes (CE row-shift, ReLU rescale, LN scale). Extensions to attention-head permutation, RoPE phase symmetry, and other architectural gauges are open.
DePavia et al. 2025
study how parameter-space rotations affect Adam’s implicit bias.

Architectural primitives as rate-shaping choices.
Each per-architecture lemma is a structural fact about how a designer’s choice of block class shapes the singular geometry. Residual DAGs preserve rate
0
on the residual stream. LayerNorm has an algebraic kernel direction
\gamma^{-1}/\|\gamma^{-1}\|
at the post-norm position; RMSNorm has no such universal kernel. Attention chains break composition additivity at depth
\geq 4
. The open question for architecture search is whether the rate profile under canonical alignment is itself a useful objective: whether tuning a network’s design to make the rate ladder shallower or steeper changes generalisation in a predictable way.

Mechanistic interpretability.
Sparse-autoencoder feature directions and dead directions intersect: are features that are also dead the features the network has implicitly compressed or dropped, and does ablating them leave the loss unchanged to leading order? Superposition in transformer residual streams has a structural reason: Corollary
58
gives the geometric capacity that supports superposition feasibility. The LayerNorm kernel direction is a deterministic probe direction available in every LN-equipped model, with the algebraic guarantee
\mathrm{cov}(\mathrm{LN}(X))\,v^{*}=0
for any input distribution. Whether the per-K-FAC-block rate exponent and the LN kernel direction combine into a useful circuit-level diagnostic, one that flags which sub-circuit a feature lives in by its rate signature, is an open empirical question. The trajectory-rate readouts are complementary to other geometric probes in the recent SLT-DL literature: per-attention-head specialisation via the refined LLC
(
Wang et al. 2024
)
, the loss-kernel probe of
Adam et al. 2025
, and the sequence-mode analysis of
Chen and Murfet 2025
; each operationalises a different aspect of singular structure, and the trajectory rate adds the deterministic, sampling-free per-layer reading to that roster.

Trajectory-rate residuals as training diagnostics.
The rate primitive of Theorem
2
is the foundational construct of the framework; the per-layer K-FAC ladder, residual-stream
\sigma_{\min}
, LN kernel direction, and any further bridge-derived observable inherit a rate prediction under canonical alignment. Real networks at scale do not satisfy the conditions under which those predicted exponents are exactly realised. The framework’s role on such networks is a reference frame: the deviation between observed and predicted at any checkpoint is a per-layer, per-trajectory, rate-grounded observable, computable for any observable the bridge defines now or in the future. Whether the deviation profile across training predicts downstream quality (final benchmark scores, generalisation behaviour, pathology signatures) is an open empirical question the framework reduces to a well-defined measurement. The contribution here is the reference-frame structure itself: current and future bridge-derived observables inherit a definable deviation against an idealised target, independent of which observable is being read.

Diagnostic instrumentation for pretraining and fine-tuning.
Are the activation-side
\sigma_{\min}(X_{\ell})
and gradient-side
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})
readouts useful as structural-health indicators during large training runs? Both are consequences of the framework, computable from forward and backward passes at any checkpoint, and rate-grounded by Theorems
21
and Corollary
58
. Whether the readings catch training pathologies (loss spikes, dead-channel emergence, gauge-mode drift under Adam) before downstream metrics do is open, a specific instance of the residual-as-diagnostic framing above.

Algebraic-baseline vs trained singular structure.
A concrete instance of the residual-as-observable framing. Lemma
18
pins down the leading constant of the activation-Gram Schur complement in the canonical setup, yielding the width-only invariant
R(h):=\lambda_{\min}(A_{\ell})/(A_{\ell})_{u,u}
at the framework’s predicted dead direction. Reading this on a LayerNorm-equipped pretrained model at random initialisation, with
u=\gamma^{-1}/\|\gamma^{-1}\|
from Proposition
63
, gives
R\approx 1
within finite-sample drift; on the trained checkpoint,
R(\gamma^{-1})
collapses, indicating that training opens additional dead directions
below
the algebraic baseline. Is the gap between random-init and trained
R(\gamma^{-1})
a useful per-checkpoint observable, separating architectural-algebraic null structure (fixed by the LN parameter alone) from training-induced SLT-singular structure (the dead directions the optimiser opens as it consolidates, in the compression regime of §
12.1
)? The observable is forward-pass-only, computable per block, with no gradient access required, and, being static, reads the same in either regime; its empirical utility across architectures is open.

Closing.

This paper makes Watanabe’s KL-order invariant trajectory-readable in original parameter coordinates, and shows that a single rate exponent organises the observable roster of a deep network: per-layer K-FAC factors, residual-stream
\sigma_{\min}
, the LayerNorm kernel direction, attention-chain composition, and the Fisher-Riemannian
\log\det^{+}
volume identity. The same exponent carries Watanabe’s broader inventory (multi-component crossings, multiplicity, singular fluctuation, prior shifts, and tempered posteriors; Section
13
), and Section
14
sets out what remains genuinely open. Underneath all of it is one object: the dead direction, the unit vector that is at once Amari’s kernel-approaching direction of the Fisher metric and Watanabe’s tangent to the singular set, read by the same KL order. That shared invariant is the bridge, and it is why every result here is legible from both sides at once.

Appendices

The appendices collect material deferred from the body: the architectural rate catalogue, holding the per-primitive instantiations that carry the canonical ladder without new phenomena, the non-canonical boundary, and the LayerNorm finite-
t
crossover (Appendix
A
); and the controlled parametric validations that anchor the rate predictions on small testbeds where every condition is independently controllable (Appendix
B
). Proofs appear inline with their theorems in the body.

Appendix A
Architectural rate catalogue

This catalogue collects the architectural instantiations summarised in Table
3
but deferred from Section
9
: the primitives that carry the canonical rate ladder through composition without introducing new behaviour, the non-canonical boundary where the ladder ceases to hold, and the finite-
t
crossover of the LayerNorm rate. Each is stated with the same structure used in the body, a per-block KL order feeding the composition theorem (Section
8
) with its singular-geometry consequence, and each predicted rate is the one checked by the freeze-probe of Appendix
B.2
and plotted in Figure
9
.

A.1  Rectangular widths

Theorem
21
and its cross-entropy companion Theorem
36
assume square hidden dimensions
h_{\ell}=h
for all
\ell
. Real networks have per-layer widths that differ: embeddings (
d_{\text{vocab}}\to d_{\text{model}}
), MLP up/down projections (
d\to 4d\to d
), classifier heads (
d\to C
). This subsection extends the bridge theorem to rectangular widths via a reduction to the square case restricted to a nested chain of narrow subspaces. The only new work is a rectangular variant of the matrix-product singular-value lemma; the backward-chain argument of Theorem
21
transfers directly.

Setup.

Let
W_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{h_{\ell}\times h_{\ell-1}}
for
\ell=1,\ldots,L
, with the widths
h_{0},h_{1},\ldots,h_{L}
arbitrary positive integers. Let
h_{*}:=\min_{\ell}h_{\ell}
be the narrowest slot. Since the composed map
M=W_{L}\cdots W_{1}
has rank at most
h_{*}
, the natural “rank-deficient-by-one” singular configuration has
\mathrm{rank}(M^{*})=h_{*}-1
. In this regime the dead direction lives in an
h_{*}
-dimensional chain of subspaces that threads through the whole network.

Concretely, an
isometry chain
is a sequence of linear isometries
\iota_{\ell}:\mathbb{R}^{h_{*}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{h_{\ell}}
for
\ell=0,1,\ldots,L
(so
\iota_{\ell}^{\top}\iota_{\ell}=I_{h_{*}}
;
\iota_{0}
embeds the narrow chain into the input space) compatible with
\theta^{*}
in the sense that

W_{\ell}^{*}\,\iota_{\ell-1}\;=\;\iota_{\ell}\cdot\mathrm{diag}(1,1,\ldots,1,0)\qquad(\ell=1,\ldots,L),

where the diagonal acts on
\mathbb{R}^{h_{*}}
and zeros out the last (
h_{*}
-th) coordinate. Equivalently,
V_{\ell}:=\iota_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{h_{*}})
is a nested-rank chain of subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{h_{\ell}}
, invariant under
W_{\ell}^{*}
, on which
W_{\ell}^{*}
acts as the square-case
\mathrm{diag}(1,\ldots,1,0)
in the isometry-chosen bases. The dead direction at layer
\ell
is

u_{\ell}:=\iota_{\ell}(e_{h_{*}})\in\mathbb{R}^{h_{\ell}},\qquad(u_{\ell}\text{ is a unit vector};u_{\ell}\in V_{\ell}).

The dead-aligned symmetric approach perturbs each layer by
t
in the dead direction:

W_{\ell}(t)\;:=\;W_{\ell}^{*}\;+\;t\,u_{\ell}u_{\ell-1}^{\top}\qquad(\ell=1,\ldots,L).

We also assume a genericity condition on the orthogonal complement:

Assumption 100

(Non-degenerate complement action)
.

The action of
W_{\ell}^{*}
on
V_{\ell-1}^{\perp}
is non-degenerate in the sense that the map
W_{\ell}^{*}:V_{\ell-1}^{\perp}\to\mathbb{R}^{h_{\ell}}
has trivial kernel and image disjoint from the dead direction
\mathbb{R}u_{\ell}
. In particular, after the isometry-adapted basis change, the non-narrow block of
W_{\ell}^{*}
is full column rank and contributes
\Theta(1)
singular values.

Assumption
100
rules out the pathological case where the orthogonal complement carries additional hidden rank deficiencies that would compete with the narrow-chain rate. It is a generic condition: a random linear map between complements of compatible dimensions satisfies it with probability one.

Lemma 101

(Rectangular partial-product action on the narrow chain)
.

Under the isometry-chain setup, for any
\ell\in\{0,1,\ldots,L-1\}
:

W_{L}(t)\,W_{L-1}(t)\cdots W_{\ell+1}(t)\cdot\iota_{\ell}\;=\;\iota_{L}\cdot\mathrm{diag}(1,\ldots,1,t^{L-\ell}).

This statement does not require Assumption
100
; the narrow-chain action is self-contained.

Proof.

Compute the partial product’s action on
\iota_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{h_{*}})
. For any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{h_{*}}
:

\displaystyle W_{\ell}(t)\,\iota_{\ell-1}(v)

\displaystyle=W_{\ell}^{*}\iota_{\ell-1}(v)+t\,u_{\ell}u_{\ell-1}^{\top}\iota_{\ell-1}(v)

\displaystyle=\iota_{\ell}(Dv)+t\,u_{\ell}\cdot(u_{\ell-1}^{\top}\iota_{\ell-1}(v))

\displaystyle=\iota_{\ell}(Dv)+t\,\iota_{\ell}(e_{h_{*}})\cdot v_{h_{*}}

\displaystyle=\iota_{\ell}\bigl(Dv+t\,v_{h_{*}}e_{h_{*}}\bigr)

where
D=\mathrm{diag}(1,\ldots,1,0)
and we used
u_{\ell-1}=\iota_{\ell-1}(e_{h_{*}})
together with
\iota_{\ell-1}^{\top}\iota_{\ell-1}=I
to extract
v_{h_{*}}=u_{\ell-1}^{\top}\iota_{\ell-1}(v)
. Observe
Dv+tv_{h_{*}}e_{h_{*}}=\mathrm{diag}(1,\ldots,1,t)\cdot v
. Therefore

W_{\ell}(t)\,\iota_{\ell-1}\;=\;\iota_{\ell}\cdot\mathrm{diag}(1,\ldots,1,t).

Iterating from
\ell+1
to
L
composes
L-\ell
copies of this relation, producing
\iota_{L}\cdot\mathrm{diag}(1,\ldots,1,t)^{L-\ell}=\iota_{L}\cdot\mathrm{diag}(1,\ldots,1,t^{L-\ell})
.
∎

Corollary 102

(Smallest singular value under complement genericity)
.

Under Lemma
101
’s setup and Assumption
100
, the smallest singular value of the partial product satisfies

\sigma_{\min}\!\Bigl(W_{L}(t)\cdots W_{\ell+1}(t)\Bigr)\;=\;t^{L-\ell}\cdot\Theta(1),

with the
\Theta(1)
constant determined by the complement genericity.

Proof.

The partial product has image spanned by
\iota_{L}(\mathbb{R}^{h_{*}})
and the complement action
W_{L}^{*}\cdots W_{\ell+1}^{*}
restricted to
V_{\ell}^{\perp}
. Singular values split into two groups: (i)
h_{*}
singular values from the narrow chain, equal to
1,\ldots,1,t^{L-\ell}
by Lemma
101
; (ii) singular values from the complement action, all
\Theta(1)
by Assumption
100
. The minimum across both groups is
t^{L-\ell}\cdot\Theta(1)
for
t
small enough that the narrow and complement contributions decouple.
∎

Theorem 103

(Multi-Layer K-FAC G-factor Bridge, rectangular weights)
.

Under the isometry-chain setup and activation classes (P1)–(P3), for the symmetric dead-aligned approach
\theta(t)
with rank-
(h_{*}-1)
singular configuration
\theta^{*}
, the dead-direction entry of the G-factor satisfies:

(a)

For
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L-1\}
,

(G_{\ell}(\theta(t)))_{u_{\ell}u_{\ell}}\;:=\;u_{\ell}^{\top}G_{\ell}(\theta(t))\,u_{\ell}\;=\;C_{\ell}^{\mathrm{rect}}\cdot t^{2(L-\ell)}\cdot\bigl(1+r_{\ell}^{G}(t)\bigr),

with activation-class-dependent corrections
r_{\ell}^{G}(t)
matching Theorem
21
.

(b)

(G_{L}(\theta(t)))_{u_{L}u_{L}}=\Theta(1)
, with the same base-case structure as Theorem
21
(MSE) or Theorem
36
(cross-entropy under expected Fisher + Assumption
34
).

(c)

The shallowest-layer rate
2(L-1)
matches Theorem
2
at KL order
k=L
.

Note on hypotheses.
This statement does
not
require Assumption
100
; the dead-direction (transversal) rate follows from the narrow-chain structure alone. The assumption is needed only to promote this to a statement about
\lambda_{\min}
; see Corollary
104
below.

Proof.

Decompose each
\mathbb{R}^{h_{\ell}}
as
V_{\ell}\oplus V_{\ell}^{\perp}
where
V_{\ell}=\iota_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{h_{*}})
. The backward chain’s action restricted to the dead direction
u_{\ell}\in V_{\ell}
is self-contained: by Lemma
101
(which holds unconditionally, without Assumption
100
), the partial product
W_{L}(t)\cdots W_{\ell+1}(t)
maps
u_{\ell}
to
t^{L-\ell}u_{L}
, and the backward operator
(W_{L}\cdots W_{\ell+1})^{\top}
correspondingly maps the output dead-direction to
u_{\ell}
with gain
t^{L-\ell}
. This holds whether or not
V_{\ell}^{\perp}
-to-
V_{\ell}^{\perp}
action is well-behaved; the computation of
u_{\ell}^{\top}\delta^{(\ell)}
involves only the narrow chain.

Explicitly, by the same canonical-basis argument as Lemma
16
(whose derivation uses only the narrow-block structure of
W_{\ell+1}^{\top}
and the chain rule), in the isometry-adapted basis

u_{\ell}^{\top}\delta^{(\ell)}\;=\;t\cdot\phi^{\prime}(a_{\ell}^{(u)})\cdot u_{\ell+1}^{\top}\delta^{(\ell+1)},

with base case given by either Lemma
16
(MSE) or Lemma
35
(CE). Induction gives
\mathbb{E}[(u_{\ell}^{\top}\delta^{(\ell)})^{2}]=\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
for
\ell<L
and
\Theta(1)
for
\ell=L
. Activation-class corrections carry over since they depend only on hidden-layer nonlinearities, not on widths.
∎

Corollary 104

(
\lambda_{\min}
statement under complement genericity)
.

Under the additional hypothesis Assumption
100
, the non-narrow eigenvalues of
G_{\ell}(\theta(t))
are
\Theta(1)
in
t
, so the smallest eigenvalue equals the dead-direction entry at leading order:

\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell}(\theta(t)))\;=\;(G_{\ell}(\theta(t)))_{u_{\ell}u_{\ell}}\;=\;\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})\quad(\ell<L),\qquad\Theta(1)\quad(\ell=L).

Without Assumption
100
, the dead-direction entry still scales as
\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
by Theorem
103
, but
\lambda_{\min}
may additionally pick up complement-null directions that are identically zero (rather than decaying) and so do not carry a rate.

Proof.

By Lemma
17
(Schur-form integral reduction: the dead-direction Schur complement at the dead row equals the dead-direction entry at leading order, since the non-dead block is
\succ 0
at
\Theta(1)
and the cross-row entries do not exceed the Schur-cancellation order), the dead-direction eigenvalue of
G_{\ell}
is
\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
in the isometry-adapted basis. The isometry-chain extension preserves the dead-row / non-dead-block structure of the parent lemma since the chain is a direct-sum extension of the canonical narrow block with a non-degenerate complement (the isometry projection
\Pi
acts as identity on the dead-direction coordinate and rotates the non-dead complement). Under Assumption
100
, the complement block has all
\Theta(1)
eigenvalues; combined with the narrow block’s
h_{*}-1
non-dead eigenvalues at
\Theta(1)
and the dead-direction Schur-eigenvalue at
\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
, the minimum is attained at the dead-direction eigenvalue for
t
small enough, yielding the stated
\lambda_{\min}
equality.
∎

Joint scope.

The combination of rectangular widths with cross-entropy is closed (Remark
38
); the formal joint statement covers MLP classifiers and Papyan-style neural-collapse setups without residuals. The residual extension (Theorem
43
, §
9.2
) adds transformer FFN blocks, classifier heads attached through residuals, and ResNet-style CNNs to the in-scope list.

Beyond Approach A.

Theorem
103
uses Approach A: dead-direction rank-star
h_{*}
at most
\min_{\ell}h_{\ell}
, with an isometry chain threading through every layer along a shared narrow subspace of dimension
h_{*}
. A more general treatment allowing the rank-star to exceed
h_{*}
at some layer (the dead direction maps to a lower-dimensional image at a bottleneck), or admitting different dead directions per layer (rather than a shared chain), is open. Approach A covers the architectural slice where every layer carries the same dead-direction signature; the open generalisations are needed when bottlenecks force the dead direction to compose with rank-1 projections inside the chain, or when the dead direction realigns layer by layer (the multi-direction chunk §
A.2
handles the case of multiple parallel dead directions but assumes consistent indexing across layers, which is the same shared-chain structure).

A.2  Multi-direction singularities and asymmetric approaches

All previous bridge theorems assume a single dead direction perturbed at uniform rate
t
across all layers. Real singularities exhibit higher rank deficit (multiple dead directions) and the approach trajectory may scale different layers’ perturbations with different powers of the same scalar parameter
t
(i.e., layer
\ell
is perturbed by
t^{p_{\ell}}
for layer-dependent
p_{\ell}
, not by separate time variables). The structural classification of multiple dead directions in MLP architectures via the
neural-network rank
of a parameter (the minimum hidden width needed to represent
f_{w}
) is due to
Farrugia-Roberts 2022
;
Farrugia-Roberts 2024
; the present subsection extends that count-of-degenerate-directions framing to a rate per direction in the K-FAC G-factor. This subsection unifies three generalizations:

•

Arbitrary rank deficit
: the singular configuration has
\mathrm{rank}(W_{\ell}^{*})
deficient by
m\geq 1
rather than just
1
, giving
m
dead directions.

•

Non-symmetric approach
: each weight is perturbed by
t^{p_{\ell}}
with possibly layer-varying exponent
p_{\ell}\geq 1
, not just
t^{1}
.

•

Per-direction KL orders
: the
m
dead directions may have
different
per-layer exponent patterns
p_{\ell}^{(i)}
, giving each direction its own effective KL order.

These fold into a single multi-index bookkeeping. Each dead direction
i
contributes its own eigenvalue to
G_{\ell}
’s spectrum at its own rate
2\sum_{\ell^{\prime}>\ell}p_{\ell^{\prime}}^{(i)}
, and the directions do not interfere. The per-direction entries
(G_{\ell})_{u_{\ell}^{(i)}u_{\ell}^{(i)}}
are read by projecting onto the known dead direction
u_{\ell}^{(i)}
at each
t
-checkpoint, a parametric freeze-probe rather than a rank-sweep on a trained-network spectrum.

Setup.

Let
W_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{h_{\ell}\times h_{\ell-1}}
be as in §
A.1
. Fix
m\geq 1
(the rank deficit). The singular configuration
\theta^{*}
has rank deficiency of order exactly
m
at every layer, meaning there are
m
canonically-aligned dead directions

u^{(i)}_{\ell}=e_{h_{\ell}-m+i}\in\mathbb{R}^{h_{\ell}},\qquad i=1,\ldots,m,\quad\ell=0,1,\ldots,L,

(we index the last
m
coordinates at each layer as the dead coordinates, consistent with the G1 isometry-chain construction of §
A.1
; this is the
consistent indexing
requirement of Assumption
105
below, which makes direction
i
at layer
\ell
correspond to direction
i
at layer
\ell^{\prime}
for all
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
). Each
W_{\ell}^{*}
, in the canonical basis, has the block structure

W_{\ell}^{*}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}A_{\ell}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},

where
A_{\ell}
acts on the non-dead coordinates (rank
\min(h_{\ell},h_{\ell-1})-m
) and the
m\times m
dead block is zero. In particular,
W_{\ell}^{*}\,u^{(i)}_{\ell-1}=0
for each
i
. The perturbation is

W_{\ell}(t)\;=\;W_{\ell}^{*}\;+\;\sum_{i=1}^{m}t^{p_{\ell}^{(i)}}\,u_{\ell}^{(i)}(u_{\ell-1}^{(i)})^{\top},

where
p_{\ell}^{(i)}\geq 1
(real-valued; not necessarily integer; the proof’s coordinate-wise scaling extends mechanically to real exponents, while the parametric validation covers only integer values
p\in\{1,2,3\}
), possibly varying with both direction
i
and layer
\ell
. The single-direction symmetric case
m=1,p_{\ell}^{(1)}=1
recovers Theorem
21
.

Assumption 105

(Consistent dead-direction indexing across layers)
.

The dead direction with index
i
at layer
\ell
corresponds to the dead direction with the same index
i
at every other layer
\ell^{\prime}
, in the sense that the canonical-basis labels
u_{\ell}^{(i)}=e_{h_{\ell}-m+i}
are aligned across
\ell
. Equivalently, the inter-layer embedding maps preserve the dead-direction subspace’s basis labelling, analogous to the nested-isometry-chain hypothesis used in Theorem
103
for rectangular widths. Without this assumption, the per-direction multi-index bookkeeping below does not factor and additional per-layer alignment factors appear in the rate formula.

For the cross-entropy base case (analogous to §
9.1
), we use a multi-direction version of Assumption
34
: the data-averaged softmax Hessian
\mathbb{E}_{x}[H(x;\theta(t))]
is uniformly positive-definite on the projection of
\mathrm{span}\{u_{L}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{m}
onto
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
(since
\mathbf{1}\in\ker H
algebraically). Explicitly, there exists
c_{0}>0
and a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
t=0
such that for all
t\in\mathcal{U}
and all unit
v\in\mathrm{span}\{\Pi_{\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}}u_{L}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{m}
,

v^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{x}\bigl[H(x;\theta(t))\bigr]\,v\;\geq\;c_{0},

where
\Pi_{\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}}=I-\tfrac{1}{C}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
. This requires
\{\Pi_{\{\mathbf{1}\}^{\perp}}u_{L}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{m}
to be linearly independent on
\mathbf{1}^{\perp}
, which is automatic when no dead direction
u_{L}^{(i)}
is parallel to
\mathbf{1}
. It is a generic condition: provided
m<C
(rank deficit doesn’t exceed the output dimension), the dead directions are not aligned with the all-ones vector, and the model doesn’t collapse to a point mass on any
\{u_{L}^{(i)}\}
-spanned affine subspace.

Theorem 106

(Multi-direction bridge theorem)
.

Under the multi-direction setup, Assumption
105
, the multi-direction CE assumption above (or the MSE base case for squared-error loss), and activation classes (P1)–(P3):

(a)

(Per-direction dead-direction-entry rate.)
For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
and
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L-1\}
, the dead-direction-
i
entry of the G-factor at layer
\ell
satisfies

(G_{\ell}(\theta(t)))_{u_{\ell}^{(i)}\,u_{\ell}^{(i)}}\;=\;C_{\ell}^{(i)}\cdot t^{2\Pi_{\ell}^{(i)}}\cdot\bigl(1+r_{\ell}^{(i)}(t)\bigr),\qquad\Pi_{\ell}^{(i)}\;:=\;\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell+1}^{L}p_{\ell^{\prime}}^{(i)},

with
r_{\ell}^{(i)}(t)=O(t)
and activation-class-dependent constants matching Theorem
21
.

(b)

(Output layer.)
For each
i
,
(G_{L}(\theta(t)))_{u_{L}^{(i)}\,u_{L}^{(i)}}=\Theta(1)
(constant from
\sigma^{2}
for MSE or from
c_{0}
for the multi-direction CE assumption).

(c)

(Spectrum /
\lambda_{\min}
corollary, under non-dead-block control.)
Provided the non-dead block of
G_{\ell}
is uniformly
\Theta(1)
in
t
(the multi-direction analog of Lemma
20
; a multi-direction version of Assumption
100
of §
A.1
suffices), the
m
dead-direction Schur complements yield
m
independent eigenvalues at rates
\{2\Pi_{\ell}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{m}
, and
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})=\min_{i}\Theta(t^{2\Pi_{\ell}^{(i)}})
. When this assumption fails (e.g., rectangular-width-induced gauge directions in the non-dead block), the per-direction entry rate of (a) remains the operational observable.

(d)

(Reduction.)
The theorem reduces to Theorem
21
when
m=1
and
p_{\ell}=1
for all
\ell
(in which case
\Pi_{\ell}=L-\ell
).

Note on hypotheses.
The dead-direction-entry statement (a) is the load-bearing one and uses only Assumption
105
plus the canonical multi-direction setup; the spectrum statement (c) requires the additional non-dead-block control, paralleling the structure of Theorem
103
(the dead-direction entry is the theorem;
\lambda_{\min}
is a corollary).

Proof.

Key observation: the perturbation
\sum_{i}t^{p_{\ell}^{(i)}}\,u_{\ell}^{(i)}(u_{\ell-1}^{(i)})^{\top}
is diagonal in the canonical basis, with each term
t^{p_{\ell}^{(i)}}
contributing only to the
(h_{\ell}-m+i,h_{\ell-1}-m+i)
matrix entry (direction
i
at layer
\ell-1
maps to direction
i
at layer
\ell
with scale
t^{p_{\ell}^{(i)}}
; no cross-coupling between directions). Combined with the block-diagonal structure of
W_{\ell}^{*}
, this gives

W_{\ell}(t)\,u_{\ell-1}^{(j)}\;=\;t^{p_{\ell}^{(j)}}\,u_{\ell}^{(j)}\qquad\text{for each }j=1,\ldots,m.

Iterating along the partial product from layer
\ell
to
L
:

W_{L}(t)\cdots W_{\ell+1}(t)\,u_{\ell}^{(i)}\;=\;\Bigl(\prod_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell+1}^{L}t^{p_{\ell^{\prime}}^{(i)}}\Bigr)\,u_{L}^{(i)}\;=\;t^{\Pi_{\ell}^{(i)}}\,u_{L}^{(i)}.

That each intermediate
u_{\ell^{\prime}}^{(i)}
is again a canonical basis vector (consistent indexing, established in the setup) is what allows this multiplicative iteration: direction
i
at each layer stays at canonical index
h_{\ell^{\prime}}-m+i
.

Transposing, the backward operator
W_{\ell+1}(t)^{\top}\cdots W_{L}(t)^{\top}
sends
u_{L}^{(i)}
back to
u_{\ell}^{(i)}
with gain
t^{\Pi_{\ell}^{(i)}}
. The dead-direction-
i
backward delta at layer
\ell
is thus

u_{\ell}^{(i)}{}^{\top}\delta^{(\ell)}\;=\;t^{\Pi_{\ell}^{(i)}}\,u_{L}^{(i)}{}^{\top}\delta^{(L)}

at leading order. The base case is direction-
i
-specific:

•

MSE:

\mathbb{E}[(u_{L}^{(i)}{}^{\top}\delta^{(L)})^{2}]=\sigma^{2}
(from the Gaussian noise setup, uniform across all
m
directions).

•

Cross-entropy:
The multi-direction version of Assumption
34
gives
(u_{L}^{(i)})^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{x}[H]u_{L}^{(i)}\geq c_{0}
for each
i
, yielding
\mathbb{E}[(u_{L}^{(i)}{}^{\top}\delta^{(L)})^{2}]=\Theta(1)
uniformly on the neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
.

In either case, squaring gives the stated rate for direction
i
.

Per-direction independence via Schur reduction.
The cross-term
u_{\ell}^{(i)}{}^{\top}G_{\ell}u_{\ell}^{(j)}
for
i\neq j
is proportional to
\mathbb{E}[(u_{\ell}^{(i)}{}^{\top}\delta^{(\ell)})\cdot(u_{\ell}^{(j)}{}^{\top}\delta^{(\ell)})]
. From the partial-product formula above,
u_{\ell}^{(i)}{}^{\top}\delta^{(\ell)}
depends only on the
i
-th component of
\delta^{(L)}
, and similarly for
j
. For MSE, the
\sigma^{2}I
noise covariance is diagonal in the canonical basis so cross-coordinate covariances vanish identically. For CE, Lemma
33
gives
H(x;\theta)=\mathrm{diag}(p)-pp^{\top}
with off-diagonal data-averaged entry
-\mathbb{E}_{x}[p_{i}p_{j}]
at order
\Theta(1)
, so the dead-direction cross-entry is
(G_{\ell})_{u^{(i)}u^{(j)}}=\Theta(t^{\Pi_{\ell}^{(i)}+\Pi_{\ell}^{(j)}})
.

Order the dead directions by
\Pi^{(1)}\leq\Pi^{(2)}\leq\cdots\leq\Pi^{(m)}
. Because the off-diagonal
\Theta(t^{\Pi^{(i)}+\Pi^{(j)}})
is the geometric mean of the two diagonals
\Theta(t^{2\Pi^{(i)}})
and
\Theta(t^{2\Pi^{(j)}})
, it can
exceed
the smaller diagonal in raw magnitude (when
\Pi^{(i)}<\Pi^{(j)}
,
\Pi^{(i)}+\Pi^{(j)}<2\Pi^{(j)}
); a "subleading" comparison directly between the off-diagonal and the diagonals would therefore be wrong. We instead use the Schur reduction. Treat the dead-direction block of
G_{\ell}
in the basis
\{u^{(1)},\ldots,u^{(m)}\}
as

\begin{pmatrix}d_{1}&w_{12}&\cdots\\
w_{12}&d_{2}&\cdots\\
\vdots&&\ddots\end{pmatrix},

with
d_{i}=\Theta(t^{2\Pi^{(i)}})
and
w_{ij}=\Theta(t^{\Pi^{(i)}+\Pi^{(j)}})
, plus the non-dead block
M
(
\succ 0
,
\Theta(1)
) and dead-row off-diagonals
v_{i}=\Theta(t^{\Pi^{(i)}})
to the non-dead block. For each
i
, the dead-direction-
i
Schur complement against (the non-dead block plus all faster-decaying directions
j
with
\Pi^{(j)}>\Pi^{(i)}
) gives

d_{i}\;-\;v_{i}^{\top}M^{-1}v_{i}\;-\;\sum_{j>i\text{ (slower-decaying)}}w_{ij}^{2}/d_{j},

where the second-tier terms are the cross-direction Schur contributions. Each
v_{i}^{\top}M^{-1}v_{i}=\Theta(t^{\Pi^{(i)}})\cdot\Theta(1)\cdot\Theta(t^{\Pi^{(i)}})=\Theta(t^{2\Pi^{(i)}})
matches the dead diagonal in order and absorbs at most a fixed fraction (the rank-1 outer-product mechanism of Lemma
17
; see Remark
19
). Each cross-direction term
w_{ij}^{2}/d_{j}=\Theta(t^{2(\Pi^{(i)}+\Pi^{(j)})})/\Theta(t^{2\Pi^{(j)}})=\Theta(t^{2\Pi^{(i)}})
also matches in order, again absorbing at most a fixed fraction by the same outer-product argument. Hence the dead-direction-
i
Schur complement is
\Theta(t^{2\Pi^{(i)}})\cdot(1-c_{i})
with
c_{i}<1
, giving the per-direction eigenvalue rate stated in (a). Non-dead eigenvalues are
\Theta(1)
by the same argument as in Theorem
21
, used in clause (c) above.

Activation-class corrections.
For class (P2) smooth activations, each
\phi^{\prime}(a_{\ell^{\prime}}^{(u^{(i)})})
factor along the backward chain contributes
(1+O(t))
at direction
i
. Accumulating over
\ell^{\prime}=\ell+1,\ldots,L
, the total correction at layer
\ell
for direction
i
is
r_{\ell}^{(i)}(t)=O(t)
, matching Theorem
103
’s activation-correction structure. For (P1) linear and (P3) ReLU,
r_{\ell}^{(i)}(t)=0
.
∎

Corollary 107

(Single-direction, non-symmetric approach)
.

For
m=1
and layer-varying
p_{\ell}
, the single-direction rate is

(G_{\ell})_{u_{\ell}u_{\ell}}=\Theta\!\Bigl(t^{2\sum_{\ell^{\prime}>\ell}p_{\ell^{\prime}}}\Bigr).

Note: the exponent depends on
\{p_{\ell^{\prime}}\}_{\ell^{\prime}>\ell}
only,
not
on
p_{\ell}
itself. This is because
G_{\ell}
reflects the backward chain ABOVE layer
\ell
; layer
\ell
’s own perturbation does not appear.

Corollary 108

(Multiple directions, uniform symmetric approach)
.

For
m\geq 1
dead directions and
p_{\ell}^{(i)}=1
for all
i,\ell
, each direction contributes an eigenvalue at rate
2(L-\ell)
.
G_{\ell}
’s spectrum (total dimension
h_{\ell}
) thus contains
m
eigenvalues at the dead rate
\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
and
h_{\ell}-m
non-dead eigenvalues of
\Theta(1)
.

Corollary 109

(Multi-direction, asymmetric approach)
.

For
m\geq 1
directions with per-direction per-layer exponents
p_{\ell}^{(i)}
, the spectrum of
G_{\ell}
contains
m
eigenvalues at rates
\{2\Pi_{\ell}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{m}
(which may be distinct if the
\Pi_{\ell}^{(i)}
differ, coincident otherwise). The operational selection rule of Appendix
5.1
identifies rate-carrying eigenvalues by matching measured
\alpha_{i}
to predicted values; under this theorem,
multiple
matches at a single component are expected when
m\geq 2
and correspond to distinct dead directions, rather than being measurement noise. The ordering of which rate is "direction 1" vs "direction 2" is a labeling choice, not intrinsic.

Remark 110

(Static-Fisher rate vs. trajectory observability for multiple directions)
.

Theorem
106
is a static-Fisher statement on the parametric trajectory. Multiple dead directions amplify the Adam non-equivariance documented in Remark
80
: each direction independently couples to gauge-mode drift, so per-direction trajectory rate readout under standard Adam is doubly fragile. Validation of the per-direction rates accordingly uses a parametric freeze-probe under a theorem-compatible setup, not Adam-trajectory rate-fitting. The constructive closure for the gauge case (Corollary
86
) lifts to multi-direction: under DDCAdam, the per-direction rates
\{2\Pi_{\ell}^{(i)}\}_{i}
are recoverable from a real training trajectory in the same regime as under SGD, since the gauge-mode coupling that causes the doubly-fragile failure mode under Adam is removed by Proposition
83
’s equivariance.

Remark 111

(Joint scope with rectangular, cross-entropy, and biases)
.

The multi-direction extension touches only the dead-direction sub-block of
G_{\ell}
(each direction independently contributing a Schur complement of the form analysed above), and is therefore disjoint from the rectangular-widths reduction (which acts on the non-dead block), the CE output-head base-case replacement (Lemma
33
), and the bias-augmented Schur structure (each direction
i
carries its own perturbation
b_{\ell}^{(i)}(t)=t^{q_{\ell}^{(i)}}u_{\ell}^{(i)}
). The joint statements are sketched rather than separately proved; the disjoint-block structure of the multi-direction Schur reduction is the load-bearing reason each composition holds.

A.3  Biases

Previous bridge theorems state the forward pass as
h_{\ell}=\phi(W_{\ell}h_{\ell-1})
, without biases. In practice every weight layer has an accompanying bias vector:
h_{\ell}=\phi(W_{\ell}h_{\ell-1}+b_{\ell})
. This subsection extends the bridge theorem to cover biases, showing that under the natural singular-configuration convention
b_{\ell}^{*}=0
, biases do not alter the rate structure; their sole effect under smooth activations is a predictable finite-
t
Taylor correction.

Setup.

Let the singular configuration be
\theta^{*}=(\{W_{\ell}^{*}\},\{b_{\ell}^{*}=0\})
, with the
W_{\ell}^{*}
structure inherited from the relevant base setup (single- or multi-direction, square or rectangular). The natural choice
b_{\ell}^{*}=0
is justified by the rank-deficiency-of-the-Jacobian interpretation of “singular configuration”: biases do not affect the rank of the forward Jacobian, so their natural singular-configuration value is any value; setting them to
0
is the canonical choice (see Remark
113
).

For concreteness we state the single-direction symmetric case explicitly; the extension to multi-direction and non-symmetric approaches (Theorem
106
) proceeds analogously by adding independent bias perturbations per dead direction. The dead-aligned approach perturbs both weights and biases in the dead direction:

W_{\ell}(t)\;=\;W_{\ell}^{*}+t\cdot u_{\ell}u_{\ell-1}^{\top},\qquad b_{\ell}(t)\;=\;t^{q_{\ell}}\cdot u_{\ell},

where
q_{\ell}\geq 1
(real) is the bias perturbation exponent at layer
\ell
. The weight-only case corresponds to
b_{\ell}(t)\equiv 0
(equivalently
q_{\ell}\to\infty
); the symmetric joint case to
q_{\ell}=1
at all layers.

Theorem 112

(Multi-Layer K-FAC G-factor Bridge with biases)
.

Under the bias-augmented setup and activation classes (P1)–(P3):

(a)

For
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L-1\}
,

(G_{\ell}(\theta(t)))_{u_{\ell}u_{\ell}}\;=\;C_{\ell}\cdot t^{2(L-\ell)}\cdot\bigl(1+r_{\ell}^{G,b}(t)\bigr),

where
r_{\ell}^{G,b}(t)
is an extended finite-
t
correction:

r_{\ell}^{G,b}(t)\;=\;r_{\ell}^{G}(t)\;+\;r_{\ell}^{\text{bias}}(t),

with
r_{\ell}^{G}(t)
the no-bias correction from Theorem
21
and

r_{\ell}^{\text{bias}}(t)\;=\;\begin{cases}0,&\phi\in\{\text{linear (P1), ReLU (P3)}\},\\
O(t^{q_{\ell}}),&\phi\in\text{(P2)},\end{cases}

where
q_{\ell}
is the bias perturbation exponent at layer
\ell
. Unlike the no-bias case where the activation correction is
r_{\ell}^{G}(t)=O(t^{\min(\ell,1)})
(vanishing faster at deeper layers), the bias correction
r_{\ell}^{\text{bias}}(t)=O(t^{q_{\ell}})
is governed entirely by the bias exponent
q_{\ell}
. In particular, for
q_{\ell}=1
(the symmetric joint case), the correction is
O(t)
uniformly in
\ell
. Both corrections vanish as
t\to 0
, so the leading-order rate
t^{2(L-\ell)}
is preserved.

(b)

(G_{L}(\theta(t)))_{u_{L}u_{L}}=\Theta(1)
, unchanged from Theorem
21
.

(c)

The rate structure
t^{2(L-\ell)}
is preserved across all cases; bias perturbations do not contribute a new rate-carrying dead direction.

Proof.

We absorb biases into augmented weight matrices. Define
\tilde{W}_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{h_{\ell}\times(h_{\ell-1}+1)}
by
\tilde{W}_{\ell}=[W_{\ell}\mid b_{\ell}]
(weight matrix with bias appended as an extra column), and
\tilde{h}_{\ell-1}=[h_{\ell-1};1]\in\mathbb{R}^{h_{\ell-1}+1}
(activation with a constant
1
appended). Then the pre-activation at layer
\ell
is
W_{\ell}h_{\ell-1}+b_{\ell}=\tilde{W}_{\ell}\tilde{h}_{\ell-1}
, and the forward pass becomes

a_{\ell}=\tilde{W}_{\ell}\tilde{h}_{\ell-1},\qquad h_{\ell}=\phi(a_{\ell}),\qquad\tilde{h}_{\ell}=[h_{\ell};1].

The re-appending of the constant
1
at each layer’s output is explicit bookkeeping;
\phi
is still applied coordinate-wise to
a_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{h_{\ell}}
only (the
1
-coordinate is not passed through
\phi
, avoiding any concerns about
\phi(1)
).

Under this augmentation,
\tilde{W}_{\ell}^{*}
has the structure

\tilde{W}_{\ell}^{*}=[W_{\ell}^{*}\mid 0],\qquad\tilde{W}_{\ell}(t)=\tilde{W}_{\ell}^{*}+t\cdot u_{\ell}[u_{\ell-1};0]^{\top}+t^{q_{\ell}}\cdot u_{\ell}[0;1]^{\top},

where the two perturbation terms correspond to the weight and bias perturbations respectively. The dead-direction-to-dead-direction entry of
\tilde{W}_{\ell}(t)
(now in the extended output-input index space) still carries a
t
factor from the weight perturbation; the new
t^{q_{\ell}}
bias perturbation couples the dead output direction
u_{\ell}
to the constant
1
-input,
not
to a dead-input direction. In the backward chain, this means the
t^{q_{\ell}}
bias perturbation is
not
in a position to contribute to the backward-chain product from
u_{L}
to
u_{\ell}
, which routes through weight entries coupling
u_{\ell^{\prime}}
to
u_{\ell^{\prime}-1}
at each step. The backward-chain derivation of Lemma
16
applies to the weight block of
\tilde{W}_{\ell}
unchanged, yielding
\delta_{\ell}^{(u)}=\Theta(t^{L-\ell})
.

Schur structure of the bias-augmented A-factor.
The augmented A-factor
\tilde{A}_{\ell}=\mathbb{E}_{x}[\tilde{h}_{\ell-1}\tilde{h}_{\ell-1}^{\top}]
is
(h_{\ell-1}+1)\times(h_{\ell-1}+1)
with the block decomposition

\tilde{A}_{\ell}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}A_{\ell}&m_{\ell}\\
m_{\ell}^{\top}&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad m_{\ell}=\mathbb{E}_{x}[h_{\ell-1}(x;\theta(t))].

The dead-coordinate of
m_{\ell}
is
m_{\ell}^{(u)}=\Theta(t^{\ell-1})
by Lemma
15
’s forward dead-component magnitude, and the non-dead components are
\Theta(1)
. Combining with the dead-row/non-dead-block structure of
A_{\ell}
from Lemma
17
(dead diagonal
\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})
, dead off-row
O(t^{\ell-1})
, non-dead block
\Theta(1)
), the bias-augmented matrix has the same dead-row/non-dead-block structure: dead diagonal
\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})
, dead off-row entries (now including the bias-coupling)
O(t^{\ell-1})
, non-dead block (now
h\times h
after including the bias-1 coordinate) is
\Theta(1)
uniformly in
t
. The Schur reduction of Lemma
17
therefore extends verbatim to
\tilde{A}_{\ell}
, and the dead-direction Schur complement remains
\Theta(t^{2(\ell-1)})
.

For
linear (P1) and ReLU (P3)
: the forward dead-component magnitude is unchanged at leading order. For P1,
a_{\ell}^{(u)}=\Theta(t)+\Theta(t^{\ell})\cdot x
where the
\Theta(t)
bias contribution coexists with the
\Theta(t^{\ell})
weight contribution. The
backward
dead-delta is driven by the weight chain only (biases do not backprop through weight transposes), so
\delta_{\ell}^{(u)}=\Theta(t^{L-\ell})
at leading order, exactly as in Lemma
16
. For P3, the dead-coordinate pre-activation
a_{\ell}^{(u)}=b_{\ell}^{(u)}(t)+W_{\ell}^{(u,\cdot)}h_{\ell-1}=t^{q_{\ell}}+\Theta(t^{\ell})
is dominated by the bias contribution for
q_{\ell}\leq\ell
(in particular for the symmetric joint case
q_{\ell}=1
at
\ell\geq 2
), and is uniformly positive over the input distribution at all
t>0
; hence
\phi^{\prime}(a_{\ell}^{(u)})\equiv 1
on dead-coordinate samples and the backward calculation proceeds with the same constant factor as the bias-free case (when the dead coordinate is in the active sign cell at the canonical setup). For non-dead coordinates
j\neq u
, the bias perturbation is zero by the canonical-direction setup, and
\phi^{\prime}(a_{\ell}^{(j)})=\phi^{\prime}(\Theta(1))=\Theta(1)
unchanged from the no-bias case, so the non-dead block of
G_{\ell}
retains the
\Theta(1)
control of Lemma
20
. A
\phi^{\prime}
-discontinuity at a sign-cell shift applies only when the dead coordinate’s canonical sign cell is the negative one, a basis choice resolvable by sign-flipping
u_{\ell}
, and not a separate scope.

For
smooth activations (P2)
: the forward dead-component picks up the
\Theta(t)
bias contribution (from the
b_{\ell}^{(u)}=t^{q_{\ell}}
perturbation, dominant when
q_{\ell}=1<\ell
), so
a_{\ell}^{(u)}=\Theta(t)
at leading order rather than
\Theta(t^{\ell})
. The Taylor expansion of
\phi^{\prime}(a_{\ell}^{(u)})
around
\phi^{\prime}(0)
gives

\phi^{\prime}(a_{\ell}^{(u)})\;=\;\phi^{\prime}(0)+\phi^{\prime\prime}(0)\cdot\Theta(t)+O(t^{2}).

Accumulating across the
L-\ell
backward Jacobian factors above layer
\ell
multiplies this
(1+O(t))
correction, contributing an additive
r_{\ell}^{\text{bias}}(t)=O(t)
correction to the log-slope. The leading-order rate
t^{2(L-\ell)}
is unchanged: the bias contribution enters through the activation derivative correction alone, and the backward chain’s
t
-scaling is untouched.
∎

Remark 113

(Natural singular-configuration choice for biases)
.

Setting
b_{\ell}^{*}=0
at the singular configuration is a convention, not a restriction. At any non-zero
b_{\ell}^{*}
that is not aligned with the dead direction, the analysis proceeds identically: only the dead-coordinate of the bias contributes to the rate calculation, and we can shift to
b^{*}_{\text{dead}}=0
by redefining the zero-point of the dead coordinate (which is WLOG under the canonical basis). Non-zero biases in non-dead coordinates are
\Theta(1)
contributions that are absorbed into the
\Theta(1)
non-dead block of
G_{\ell}
and do not affect the dead-direction eigenvalue.

Empirical validation.

Direct validation at
L=4
,
h=6
, MSE, across three cases and three activations:

•

Case A
(weight-only,
b\equiv 0
): exact standard-theorem rates across linear, ReLU, GeLU, confirming biases-at-zero are inert.

•

Case B
(bias-only, weights fixed at
W^{*}
,
q\in\{1,2\}
): measured rate is
0
at every layer, every activation, confirming bias perturbations alone do not create a rate-carrying dead direction (part (c) of the theorem).

•

Case C
(joint perturbation):

–

Linear, ReLU: exact match with the weight-only prediction (part (a) with
r_{\ell}^{\text{bias}}=0
).

–

GeLU,
q_{\ell}=1
: measured
\alpha_{1}=7.64,\alpha_{2}=5.13,\alpha_{3}=2.57
(compared to the no-bias GeLU values
6.48,4.12,2.02
). The
additional
slope-shift from the bias, on top of the standard smooth-activation
r_{\ell}^{G}
term, is approximately
+1
per layer at our
t
-range
[10^{-1.2},10^{-0.3}]
. This is the expected behavior of an
O(t)
multiplicative correction of the form
(1+ct)^{2(L-\ell)}
fit in log-log space: the effective log-slope at finite
t
exceeds the leading-order
t\to 0
asymptote by a bounded amount that depends on
c
and the
t
-range, with
c
accumulating across the
L-\ell
backward Jacobian factors.

–

GeLU,
q_{\ell}=2
: measured
\alpha_{1}=7.24,\alpha_{2}=4.76,\alpha_{3}=2.37
, a smaller slope-shift than
q=1
(as predicted:
r_{\ell}^{\text{bias}}=O(t^{q_{\ell}})=O(t^{2})
is a smaller correction over the same
t
-range).

All cases match the theorem’s prediction: rate structure preserved, smooth-activation correction bounded and activation-class-dependent as stated.

Scope and composition with prior extensions.

Theorem
112
is stated and proved for the square-hidden, feedforward, single-direction, symmetric-approach setting. The bias column of
\tilde{W}_{\ell}=[W_{\ell}\mid b_{\ell}]
does not enter the backward chain at the dead direction (the bias column couples dead output to constant-
1
, not dead input), so the bias-augmented Schur reduction is disjoint from the rectangular non-dead-block reduction (Theorem
103
, where the narrow chain does not include the
1
-coordinate), the CE output-head replacement (Lemma
33
, which leaves the backward chain unchanged), the residual-DAG path-distance (Theorem
43
, where
K(\ell)
counts weight edges only), and the multi-direction per-direction Schur reduction (Theorem
106
, where each direction
i
carries its own perturbation
b_{\ell}^{(i)}(t)=t^{q_{\ell}^{(i)}}u_{\ell}^{(i)}
). The
r_{\ell}^{\mathrm{bias}}(t)
correction accumulates predictably as
O(t^{q_{\ell}^{(i)}})
per direction. We mark the joint statements as natural follow-ups, not “mechanical applications,” because closing each requires verifying the disjointness formally, which we have done above only for the square-hidden single-direction MSE base case.

A.4  SwiGLU

This subsection states the per-block forward rate for the SwiGLU MLP variant
used in modern decoder-only LLM families. The result is a
direct instantiation of the bridge framework on the SwiGLU forward map, not
an extension of the bridge theorem itself.

Proposition 114

(SwiGLU MLP block rate)
.

Let
\mathrm{SwiGLUMLP}(x)=W_{\mathrm{down}}\bigl(\mathrm{silu}(W_{\mathrm{gate}}x)\odot W_{\mathrm{up}}x\bigr)
with all three Linear layers at canonical init
W=\mathrm{diag}(1,\ldots,1,t)
(shared dead dimension
d
). The forward block rate is
k_{\mathrm{SwiGLU}}^{\mathrm{fwd}}=3
: the block output along
e_{d}
is
(1/2)\cdot t^{3}\cdot x_{d}^{2}+O(t^{4})
.

Proof.

At the dead dimension:
(W_{\mathrm{gate}}x)_{d}=t\cdot x_{d}
. Using
\mathrm{silu}(y)=y\cdot\sigma(y)
with
\sigma(0)=1/2
, we have
\mathrm{silu}(t\cdot x_{d})=(1/2)\cdot t\cdot x_{d}+O(t^{2})
. Also
(W_{\mathrm{up}}x)_{d}=t\cdot x_{d}
. Their elementwise product at dimension
d
:
(1/2)\cdot t^{2}\cdot x_{d}^{2}
. Finally
W_{\mathrm{down}}
with dead column contributes another factor of
t
:
y_{d}=t\cdot(1/2)\cdot t^{2}\cdot x_{d}^{2}=(1/2)\cdot t^{3}\cdot x_{d}^{2}
. The off-diagonal entries of all three matrices contribute at order
t^{0}
through non-dead channels and do not affect the dead-channel output at leading order. Empirical verification: parametric probe at
d\in\{16,64\}
gives forward slope
3.000
exactly, fit on
t\leq 10^{-2}
.
∎

Remark 115

(SwiGLU composition: extra
t
-factor per block)
.

Under Theorem
30
, a sequential stack of
n
SwiGLU MLP blocks (no residual) has dead-direction rate
3n
at the input of block
B_{1}
, versus
2n
for standard MLP stacks. For a transformer block combining attention
+
SwiGLU MLP, the per-block rate for rate-composition purposes is
k_{\mathrm{attn}}^{\mathrm{bk}}+k_{\mathrm{SwiGLU}}^{\mathrm{fwd}}=2+3=5
(under the heuristic that internal block rates add across sub-blocks; the backward composition requires separate per-sub-block analysis). SwiGLU-equipped decoder-only architectures (typical depth
L\in[28,32]
for
7
–
8
B-parameter models) therefore have a markedly different rate profile than standard fc1-
\phi
-fc2 MLP architectures.

Remark 116

(Practical application to modern LLM architectures)
.

Modern decoder-only LLMs widely use SwiGLU MLPs in place of the standard fc1-activation-fc2 form. Use
k_{\mathrm{SwiGLU}}^{\mathrm{fwd}}=3
(Proposition
114
) in place of the standard
k_{\mathrm{MLP}}^{\mathrm{fwd}}=2
when applying Theorem
30
to these architectures.

Figure 11
:
SwiGLU forward rate
k_{\mathrm{SwiGLU}}^{\mathrm{fwd}}=3
(Proposition
114
). Why SwiGLU has a higher block rate than the standard fc1-act-fc2 MLP. (a) The block cascade compounds three
t
-factors along the dead dimension, one each from
W_{\mathrm{gate}}
,
W_{\mathrm{up}}
, and
W_{\mathrm{down}}
, with
\mathrm{silu}
supplying the
\sigma(0)=1/2
coefficient; together they give forward block rate
3
. (b) The parametric prediction at canonical init validates the rate: SwiGLU slope
3.000
vs standard fc1-act-fc2 slope
2
, at
d\in\{16,64\}
, fit window
t\leq 10^{-2}
. SwiGLU-equipped decoder-only LLMs (LLaMA, Mistral, Qwen) inherit this higher per-block rate when applying composition additivity.

A.5  Non-canonical alignment

All prior bridge theorems assume the dead direction
u_{\ell}
is aligned with a canonical basis vector at every layer. Trained networks rarely satisfy this exactly: dead directions emerge during training at rotations of the canonical frame, and we want to know what survives. The answer divides cleanly along the activation axis. Under linear activation, rotation is without loss of generality, and Theorem
21
transfers verbatim. Under nonlinear activation, canonical alignment is essential: arbitrary rotation collapses the rate to model-dependent values that have no clean closed form. We give the positive result for linear activation, the negative result for nonlinear, and a sketch of why nonlinearity breaks the rotation invariance. We restrict throughout to square hidden (
h_{\ell}=h
for all
\ell
); the rectangular extension is orthogonal and addressed below.

Notation.

The rotation parameter
\alpha_{\mathrm{rot}}\in[0,1]
measures how far the dead direction is rotated away from the canonical basis:
\alpha_{\mathrm{rot}}=0
corresponds to canonical alignment (
u_{\ell}=e_{h}
), and
\alpha_{\mathrm{rot}}=1
to a uniformly random rotation of the dead direction.

Remark 117

(Genericity of non-canonical alignment)
.

The set of unit vectors
u\in\mathbb{R}^{h}
aligned with a coordinate basis vector has measure zero in the unit sphere
S^{h-1}
, so canonical alignment is a measure-zero condition. For randomly-initialised networks under SGD with smooth or ReLU activations, dead directions emerging during training generically lie at non-zero rotation
\alpha_{\mathrm{rot}}
from any coordinate basis. The negative result of Proposition
119
therefore applies generically, motivating an intermediate spectral-identification step (an operational selection rule that recovers the dead direction from the measured spectrum) at trained-network scale.

Proposition 118

(Linear activation: rotation is without loss of generality)
.

Under activation class (P1) (linear, identity) and the square-hidden setup (
h_{\ell}=h
for all
\ell
), Theorem
21
holds for arbitrary dead-direction unit vectors
u
with
u_{\ell}=Uu_{0}
for a fixed orthogonal
U\in O(h)
applied consistently at every layer (here
u_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{h}
denotes the input-space dead direction). The rate
(G_{\ell})_{uu}=\Theta(t^{2(L-\ell)})
is preserved exactly.

Proof.

Change of basis: let
U\in O(h)
be an orthogonal matrix sending
e_{h}
to
u_{0}
. Define rotated weights
\tilde{W}_{\ell}=U^{\top}W_{\ell}U
(the same
U
used at each layer’s input and output, valid because the square-hidden hypothesis means input and output ambient spaces both have dimension
h
). Under this rotation, the singular configuration
W_{\ell}^{*}=U\cdot\mathrm{diag}(1,\ldots,1,0)\cdot U^{\top}
(a symmetric matrix conjugate to
\diag(1,\ldots,1,0)
via
U
on both sides, valid because the dead-direction perturbation
t\cdot uu^{\top}
is symmetric and the same orthogonal applies on the input and output sides) becomes
\tilde{W}_{\ell}^{*}=\mathrm{diag}(1,\ldots,1,0)
(canonically aligned), and the perturbation
t\cdot uu^{\top}
becomes
t\cdot e_{h}e_{h}^{\top}
. Linearity preserves this equivalence: the forward pass
h_{\ell}=W_{\ell}h_{\ell-1}
transforms to
\tilde{h}_{\ell}=\tilde{W}_{\ell}\tilde{h}_{\ell-1}
with
\tilde{h}=U^{\top}h
, and the backward chain transforms analogously. Theorem
21
applies to the rotated system, and the rate prediction transfers back. Validated on the parametric rotated-dead-direction sweep: linear activation matches the canonical rate exactly at arbitrary rotation amounts
\alpha_{\mathrm{rot}}\in[0,1]
.
∎

Proposition 119

(Nonlinear activation: canonical alignment is essential, empirical demonstration)
.

Under activation classes (P2) smooth (e.g., GeLU, tanh) and (P3) ReLU, the dead-direction rate is
not
preserved under arbitrary rotation. The canonical-basis rate exponent
2(L-\ell)
is not realised, and the measured exponent at finite
\alpha_{\mathrm{rot}}
takes activation-, rotation-, and depth-dependent values that may be substantially smaller than the canonical prediction (e.g., at
\alpha_{\mathrm{rot}}=0.1
,
L=4
,
h=6
, the deepest-layer rates are
<1
vs the canonical
6
). We present this as an
empirical demonstration
that canonical alignment is essential, not a fully analytical proof; the analytical mechanism is sketched below and a rigorous lemma stating a quantitative rate bound under controlled rotation is left as future work.

Mechanism sketch.

The rate prediction derives from the canonical-basis block structure of
W_{\ell}^{*}
, which makes the dead-direction backward propagation factor cleanly (Lemma
16
). Elementwise activations
\phi
commute with rotation only through the null rotation (identity). For a non-canonical dead direction
u=Ue_{h}
with
U
non-diagonal, each backward Jacobian factor
\phi^{\prime}(a_{\ell})\odot\cdot
mixes the dead-direction-along-
u
with components of
a_{\ell}
that have non-vanishing
\phi^{\prime}
; these mixed contributions enter at
\Theta(1)
and dominate the backward delta’s dead-direction projection. A rigorous bound on the resulting rate as a function of
\alpha_{\mathrm{rot}}
would require explicit accounting of how the elementwise nonlinearity couples canonical and rotated coordinates, which we have not done.
∎

Empirical demonstration.

At
L=4
,
h=6
, with rotation amount
\alpha_{\mathrm{rot}}\in\{0,0.1,0.3,0.5,0.7,0.9,1.0\}
:

•

Linear
: rate preserved exactly at all
\alpha_{\mathrm{rot}}
(Proposition
118
).

•

ReLU
: at
\alpha_{\mathrm{rot}}=0
, rate is
(6,4,2,0)
(canonical); at
\alpha_{\mathrm{rot}}=0.1
, rate collapses to
(0.05,0.37,0.92,0)
; at
\alpha_{\mathrm{rot}}=1.0
, rate is
(0.18,0.29,0.18,0)
.

•

GeLU
: similar collapse,
\alpha_{\mathrm{rot}}=0.1
gives
(0.19,0.37,1.44,0)
;
\alpha_{\mathrm{rot}}=1.0
gives
(0.31,0.30,0.61,0)
.

This demonstrates by counterexample that the theorem’s rate prediction does not hold under arbitrary rotation in the nonlinear cases.

Corollary 120

(Near-canonical continuity, open)
.

Whether a quantitative rate bound is recoverable under
small
rotations (dead direction
u=Ue_{h}
with
\|U-I\|\leq\epsilon
) for nonlinear activations remains open. The rotated-dead-direction sweep shows the rate degrades rapidly as
\alpha_{\mathrm{rot}}\to 0^{+}
, suggesting any such bound would need to be highly
\epsilon
-dependent and may not be polynomial in
t
.

Practical takeaway.

In a trained network the dead direction is not expected to align with any natural coordinate basis (Remark
117
); the operational selection rule recovers it from the measured spectrum. Across the observable hierarchy:
\sigma_{\min}
-based identification is the cheapest tier (real-time cadence, no projection needed);
u^{\top}Gu
probing requires identifying
u
first (offline tier, via the selection rule);
\lambda_{\min}(G_{\ell})
is intermediate (periodic cadence,
n/d\geq 100
samples); the full Fisher spectrum is the most informative but offline-only at hidden widths
h\gtrsim 10^{4}
. The non-canonical case here is one source of why direct dead-direction projection requires an intermediate identification step, not a categorical exclusion of any tier.

Remark 121

(Scope: near-canonical continuity)
.

Proposition
119
establishes that canonical alignment is essential in the nonlinear cases. Corollary
120
flags the near-canonical continuity question as open: whether a quantitative rate bound is recoverable under small rotations
\|U-I\|\leq\epsilon
for nonlinear activations. The orthogonal scope items (rectangular-widths approach boundary, attention-softmax extensions) are addressed elsewhere in the framework, not here.

A.6  LayerNorm finite-
t
crossover

The LayerNorm rate bound of Section
9.3
is an asymptotic (
t\to 0
) statement; at the finite
t
where SGD trajectories live, the measured rate sits in a crossover region. The following gives its closed form on the LN-equipped MLP block.

A.6.1
LayerNorm finite-
t
rate-shift: closed form for the MLP case

The asymptotic-vs-finite-
t
distinction observed empirically under LayerNorm (
q_{\mathrm{LN}}\to 0
at
t\to 0
matching the no-LN predictions, but a non-zero local log-slope at finite
t
) is upgraded here to a proved analytical statement for the MLP case at
d\geq 5
, with a structural conjecture for the softmax-attention case below.

Setup.

Take a depth-2 MLP
y(x;W_{1},W_{2})=W_{2}\cdot\mathrm{LN}(W_{1}x)
with
\mathrm{LN}
applied at
\gamma=1,\beta=0
. Use the canonical singular approach
W_{1}(t)=W_{2}(t)=\mathrm{diag}(1,\ldots,1,t)
with
t\in(0,1]
; the dead direction is the last channel
u=e_{h}
. Input
x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
. Loss
\mathcal{L}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{x}[\|y(x;\theta)-Y(x)\|^{2}]
, target
Y(x)=y(x;\theta^{*})+\eta
where
\theta^{*}
has the dead entry of
W_{2}
zeroed (so the target output’s
h
-component is
0
at every input plus noise) and
\eta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{\text{noise}}^{2}I)
.

Throughout we write
u_{1}=W_{1}(t)x
, so
u_{1,j}=x_{j}
for
j\neq h
and
u_{1,h}=t\cdot x_{h}
. Define the non-dead-channel mean and centered variance

\bar{x}_{\neq h}\;:=\;\tfrac{1}{d-1}\sum_{j\neq h}x_{j},\qquad s^{2}\;:=\;\tfrac{1}{d-1}\sum_{j\neq h}(x_{j}-\bar{x}_{\neq h})^{2}.

By Cochran’s theorem on
d-1
iid Gaussian samples,
\bar{x}_{\neq h}
and
s^{2}
are independent, with
\bar{x}_{\neq h}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/(d-1))
and
(d-2)s^{2}\sim\chi^{2}_{d-2}
.

LayerNorm Jacobian and the
O(1)
leak.

The LN operation computes
\mu(t)=\tfrac{1}{d}\sum_{j}u_{1,j}
and
\sigma(t)^{2}=\tfrac{1}{d}\sum_{j}(u_{1,j}-\mu(t))^{2}
. Expanding in
t
:

\displaystyle\mu(t)

\displaystyle\;=\;\tfrac{d-1}{d}\,\bar{x}_{\neq h}\;+\;\tfrac{1}{d}\,t\,x_{h},

\displaystyle\sigma(t)^{2}

\displaystyle\;=\;\sigma_{0}^{2}\;+\;O(t^{2}),

with
\sigma_{0}^{2}:=\sigma(0)^{2}=\tfrac{1}{d}\sum_{j\neq h}(x_{j}-\tfrac{d-1}{d}\bar{x}_{\neq h})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{d}(\tfrac{d-1}{d}\bar{x}_{\neq h})^{2}=\tfrac{d-1}{d}s^{2}+\tfrac{(d-1)^{2}}{d^{3}}\bar{x}_{\neq h}^{2}
. For Gaussian-isotropic input,
\sigma_{0}^{2}
has mean
\tfrac{d-1}{d}\cdot 1+\tfrac{d-1}{d^{2}}\cdot\tfrac{1}{1}\cdot\tfrac{1}{d-1}=\tfrac{d-1}{d}+\tfrac{1}{d^{2}}=\tfrac{d^{2}-d+1}{d^{2}}
, an
O(1)
random variable. The dead-channel post-LN value is

z_{h}(t)\;=\;\frac{u_{1,h}-\mu(t)}{\sigma(t)}\;=\;\underbrace{-\tfrac{d-1}{d}\cdot\frac{\bar{x}_{\neq h}}{\sigma_{0}}}_{O(1)}\;+\;\underbrace{\tfrac{d-1}{d}\cdot\frac{t\cdot x_{h}}{\sigma_{0}}}_{O(t)}\;+\;O(t^{2}).

(2)

The
O(1)
leading term in (
2
) is the central object of the rate-shift mechanism:
LN does not annihilate the dead direction; it leaks an
O(1)
amplitude into the post-LN dead-channel signal via the mean-subtraction’s coupling to the non-dead channels.
Since
\bar{x}_{\neq h}/\sigma_{0}
is independent of
\sigma_{0}
when
\sigma_{0}
is well-conditioned (specifically, when
\mathbb{E}[1/\sigma_{0}^{2}]<\infty
), the leak’s law is determined by the joint distribution of
(\bar{x}_{\neq h},\sigma_{0}^{2})
. Cochran’s theorem makes them independent, and
\bar{x}_{\neq h}^{2}\sim\tfrac{1}{d-1}\chi^{2}_{1}
, so
\mathbb{E}[\bar{x}_{\neq h}^{2}/\sigma_{0}^{2}]=\tfrac{1}{d-1}\mathbb{E}[1/\sigma_{0}^{2}]
, finite when
d\geq 5
(since
\mathbb{E}[1/\chi^{2}_{k}]=1/(k-2)
for
k>2
, here
k=d-2
).

Output and gradient at the dead component.

The dead-channel network output is

y_{h}(t)\;=\;(W_{2})_{h,h}(t)\cdot z_{h}(t)\;=\;t\cdot z_{h}(t)\;=\;-\tfrac{d-1}{d}\cdot\frac{t\,\bar{x}_{\neq h}}{\sigma_{0}}\;+\;\tfrac{d-1}{d}\cdot\frac{t^{2}\,x_{h}}{\sigma_{0}}\;+\;O(t^{3}).

Hence
y_{h}(t)=O(t)
at leading order, in stark contrast to the no-LN case where
y_{h}^{\text{noLN}}(t)=t^{2}x_{h}
would be
O(t^{2})
.

The target’s dead-channel value is
Y_{h}=(W_{2}^{*})_{h,h}\cdot z_{h}^{*}(t)+\eta_{h}=0+\eta_{h}=\eta_{h}
, since
(W_{2}^{*})_{h,h}=0
. (Note:
z_{h}^{*}(t)
is computed at the target
W_{1}^{*}=\mathrm{diag}(1,\ldots,1,0)
; this differs from the student’s
z_{h}(t)
at
O(t^{2})
via the
\sigma
normalization, but the difference is annihilated by the
(W_{2}^{*})_{h,h}=0
prefactor at all orders.) The residual is

y_{h}(t)-Y_{h}\;=\;-\tfrac{d-1}{d}\,\frac{t\,\bar{x}_{\neq h}}{\sigma_{0}}\;+\;\tfrac{d-1}{d}\,\frac{t^{2}\,x_{h}}{\sigma_{0}}\;-\;\eta_{h}\;+\;O(t^{3}),

and the dead-direction loss-gradient is
\partial\mathcal{L}/\partial y_{h}=2(y_{h}-Y_{h})
.

Directional Fisher: the
t^{2}/d
term and its regime structure.

Squaring and taking the expectation over
(x,\eta)
, with
\bar{x}_{\neq h}
,
x_{h}
,
\sigma_{0}
, and
\eta_{h}
each independent of the others except for
\sigma_{0}
(which depends on
\{x_{j}\}_{j\neq h}
but not on
x_{h}
nor
\eta_{h}
):

\displaystyle u^{\top}G_{W_{2}}(t)\,u

\displaystyle\;=\;4\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[(y_{h}-Y_{h})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\;=\;4\,\bigl(\tfrac{d-1}{d}\bigr)^{2}\,t^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{\bar{x}_{\neq h}^{2}}{\sigma_{0}^{2}}\right]\;+\;4\,\bigl(\tfrac{d-1}{d}\bigr)^{2}\,t^{4}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{x_{h}^{2}}{\sigma_{0}^{2}}\right]\;+\;4\sigma_{\text{noise}}^{2}\;+\;O(t^{5})

\displaystyle\;=\;4\sigma_{\text{noise}}^{2}\;+\;\frac{4\,t^{2}}{d-1}\,\Phi(d)\;+\;4\,t^{4}\,\bigl(\tfrac{d-1}{d}\bigr)^{2}\,\Psi(d)\;+\;O(t^{5}),

(3)

where the dimensionless Gaussian expectations

\Phi(d)\;:=\;\bigl(\tfrac{d-1}{d}\bigr)^{2}\cdot(d-1)\cdot\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{\bar{x}_{\neq h}^{2}}{\sigma_{0}^{2}}\right],\qquad\Psi(d)\;:=\;\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{x_{h}^{2}}{\sigma_{0}^{2}}\right]

are
O(1)
functions of
d
alone (and
\Phi(d)\to 1
,
\Psi(d)\to 1
at large
d
by Gaussian concentration of
\sigma_{0}^{2}
, with convergence rate
O(1/\sqrt{d})
). Cross-terms vanish by independence and zero-mean of
\eta_{h},x_{h}
.

The expansion (
3
) establishes the key structural finding:
LN inserts a
t^{2}/(d-1)
term into
u^{\top}Gu
that is absent without LN
. Without LN,
y_{h}^{\text{noLN}}(t)=t^{2}x_{h}
, so
u^{\top}G_{W_{2}}^{\text{noLN}}u=4\sigma_{\text{noise}}^{2}+4t^{4}\cdot 1+O(t^{5})
, with no
t^{2}
term.

Theorem 122

(LN finite-
t
rate shift, MLP single-LN)
.

For the depth-2 MLP
y=W_{2}\cdot\mathrm{LN}(W_{1}x)
with canonical singular approach
W_{1}=W_{2}=\mathrm{diag}(1,\ldots,1,t)
,
t\in(0,1]
, Gaussian input
x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, target noise
\eta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{\text{noise}}^{2}I)
, and dimension
d\geq 5
(so that
\mathbb{E}[1/\sigma_{0}^{2}]<\infty
), the directional Fisher at the dead direction
u=e_{h}
in
W_{2}
’s output space satisfies

u^{\top}G_{W_{2}}(t)\,u\;=\;4\,\sigma_{\text{noise}}^{2}\;+\;\frac{4\,t^{2}}{d-1}\,\Phi(d)\;+\;4\,t^{4}\,\bigl(\tfrac{d-1}{d}\bigr)^{2}\,\Psi(d)\;+\;O(t^{5}),

with
\Phi(d),\Psi(d)>0
explicit dimensionless Gaussian expectations defined above. The local log-slope
\alpha(t):=t\cdot d\log(u^{\top}G_{W_{2}}u)/dt
exhibits three asymptotic regimes:

1.

Noise floor
(
t^{2}\cdot\Phi/(d-1)\ll\sigma_{\text{noise}}^{2}
):
\alpha(t)\to 0
, recovering the no-LN prediction at scale
t\to 0
.

2.

t^{2}
-plateau
(
\sigma_{\text{noise}}^{2}\ll t^{2}\cdot\Phi/(d-1)
and
t^{4}\cdot((d-1)/d)^{2}\Psi\ll t^{2}\cdot\Phi/(d-1)
, i.e. the
t^{2}
term dominates both the noise floor and the
t^{4}
term):
\alpha(t)\to 2
, a rate-2 plateau induced by LN’s mean-subtraction leak.

3.

Direct rate
(
t^{4}\cdot((d-1)/d)^{2}\Psi
dominant):
\alpha(t)\to 4
, the no-LN-direct rate.

The crossover scales between regimes are

t_{\text{noise}\to t^{2}}^{2}=(d-1)\sigma_{\text{noise}}^{2}/\Phi(d)
and

t_{t^{2}\to t^{4}}^{2}=\Phi(d)/((d-1)((d-1)/d)^{2}\Psi(d))=d^{2}\Phi(d)/((d-1)^{3}\Psi(d))
.

Proof.

Equation (
3
) is derived above by direct expansion of
z_{h}(t)
in (
2
), computing
y_{h}(t)=tz_{h}(t)
, forming the residual
y_{h}-Y_{h}
, squaring, and taking the expectation under the Gaussian product measure. The cross-terms vanish by zero-mean independence of
\bar{x}_{\neq h},x_{h},\eta_{h}
; the diagonal terms give the three contributions
\sigma_{\text{noise}}^{2}
,
